Deformations of Compact Calabi–Yau Conifolds
Yohsuke Imagi

Abstract
Let X X be a compact normal Kähler space whose canonical sheaf is a rank-one free 𝒪 X {{\mathcal{O}}}_{X} module and whose singularities are isolated, rational and quasi-homogeneous. We prove then that under a topological hypothesis the obstruction to deforming X X concentrates upon its singularities, generalizing partially the results of [ 44 , 28 ] . We prove also that under a certain hypothesis the locally trivial deformations of X X are unobstructed.



1
Introduction

In this paper we generalize the results of
[
6
,
37
,
49
,
50
,
60
,
61
,
62
]
. We deal with the following class of complex analytic spaces (which we call complex spaces for short).

Definition 1.1
.

A
compact Calabi–Yau
n
-conifold
is a compact normal Kähler space
X
of dimension
n
whose canonical sheaf is a rank-one free
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}
module and whose singularities are isolated, rational and quasi-homogeneous.

The deformations of a compact Calabi–Yau
n
-fold
X
may in general be obstructed
[
29
]
. But by
[
28
, Theorem 2.2]
, for
n=3
the obstruction concentrates (in the sense of Definition
8.16
) upon the singular set
X^{\rm sing}.
In particular, if
X^{\rm sing}
is isolated and if the germ
(X,x)
at every
x\in X^{\rm sing}
has unobstructed deformations then
X
itself has unobstructed deformations too.

In this paper we prove the following theorem. As in §
3
, if
X
is a compact Calabi–Yau conifold then for each
x\in X^{\rm sing}
there exist a Kähler cone
C_{x}
with vertex
{\rm vx}
and a germ isomorphism
(X,x)\cong(C_{x},{\rm vx}).
We can define in particular, for
q=0,1,2,\dots,
the
q^{\rm th}
Betti number
b_{q}(C_{x}^{{\rm reg}})
of the regular locus
C_{x}^{{\rm reg}}:=C_{x}\setminus\{{\rm vx}\}.
These are independent of the choice of
(C_{x},{\rm vx})
and depend only on the topology of
(X,x).

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
X
be a compact Calabi–Yau
n
-conifold such that for each
x\in X^{\rm sing}
we have
b_{n-2}(C_{x}^{{\rm reg}})=0
or
b_{n-1}(C_{x}^{{\rm reg}})=0.
The obstruction to deforming
X
then concentrate upon its singularities.

For
n=3,
by
[
28
, Theorem 2.2]
the same statement holds without the hypothesis that the singularities should be isolated and quasi-homogeneous. The Betti number condition is not necessary either. That proof however is based upon the method of
[
44
]
which applies only to
n=3.
Theorem
1.2
on the other hand applies to every
n.
We prove also

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
X
be a compact Calabi–Yau
n
-conifold with
n\geqslant 3
and
H^{2}_{X^{\rm sing}}(X,\Omega^{n-2}_{X})=0.
The locally trivial deformations of
X
are then unobstructed.

For
n=2
the singularities are rational double points (whose deformations are unobstructed) and Theorem
1.2
is proved in
[
50
,
51
]
. The locally trivial deformations are also unobstructed as we recall now. Denote by
\Def(X)
the Kuranishi space for deformations of
X,
which is smooth by Theorem
1.2
. For
x\in X^{\rm sing}
denote by
\Def(X,x)
the Kuranishi space for deformations of the germ
(X,x),
which is smooth because
(X,x)
is a hypersurface singularity. As in
[
10
]
the map
\Def(X)\to\prod_{x\in X^{\rm sing}}\Def(X,x)
defined by taking germs at
X^{\rm sing}
is a submersion. Its fibre over the reference point of
\prod_{x\in X^{\rm sing}}\Def(X,x)
defines therefore a smooth Kuranishi space for locally trivial deformations of
X.

As in
[
2
]
every Calabi–Yau conifold
X
has a quasi-étale cover which is the product of a torus, irreducible Calabi–Yau varieties and irreducible holomorphic symplectic varieties. But as
X^{\rm sing}
is isolated the product has only one factor. Let this be an irreducible symplectic variety. Its deformations are then unobstructed
[
46
, Theorem 2.5]
. Although
X
is supposed projective in the formal statement of this result, that hypothesis is unnecessary as is clear from its proof; all we need is
[
48
, Theorem 1]
, which applies to Kähler spaces. It is known also that the locally trivial deformations of
X
are unobstructed
[
3
, Theorem 4.7]
.

If
X
is not symplectic, less is known. By
[
21
, Corollary 1.5 and Remark 4.5]
, if the singularities of
X
are complete intersections and satisfy the
1
Du Bois condition then the deformations of
X
are unobstructed. The proof shows also that if
X^{\rm sing}
is in addition
n-1
Du Bois then the locally trivial deformations of
X
are unobstructed. For instance, if
(X,x)
is a quasi-homogeneous hypersurface singularity then the higher Du Bois conditions are conditions about its minimal exponent
\alpha
(introduced in
[
54
]
). More precisely, let
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n+1}
have a
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{*}
action of weights
w_{1},\dots,w_{n+1}\in\{1,2,3,\dots\}
with greatest common divisor
1
and let
(X,x)
be defined in
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n+1}
by a weighted homogeneous polynomial of degree
d;
then
\alpha=(w_{1}+\cdots+w_{n+1})/d.
Moreover, for
k=0,1,2,\dots
the germ
(X,x)
is
k
Du Bois if and only if
\alpha\geqslant k+1.
So the major advantage of Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
is that we do not need such restrictions. Also we do not need the singularities to be complete intersections either.

We explain now how we prove Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
. The formal structure of the proof is similar to that of
[
46
, Theorem 2.5]
above. In that theorem we introduce on the regular locus
X^{{\rm reg}}
a complete Kähler metric and show that part of the Hodge spectral sequence of
X^{{\rm reg}}
degenerates (as in
[
48
, Theorem 1]
). This with the
T^{1}
lift theorem implies readily that the deformation functors are unobstructed.

On the other hand, in the proof of Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
we introduce Kähler metrics called conifolds metrics in the sense of
[
12
, Definition 4.6]
,
[
31
, Definition 2.2]
,
[
33
, Definition 2.1]
and
[
34
, Definition 3.24]
; see also Definition
5.1
and Remark
5.2
. Such metrics are locally expressible as Riemannian cone metrics, which are incomplete metrics on
X^{{\rm reg}}
whereas those used in
[
48
]
are complete metrics on
X^{{\rm reg}}.

Also we cannot make such a simple statement as the Hodge spectral sequence degeneration
[
48
, Theorem 1]
. Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
are concerned with certain maps of the form
{{\mathcal{A}}}\to{{\mathcal{B}}}
where
{{\mathcal{A}}},{{\mathcal{B}}}
are sheaf cohomology groups of infinitesimal deformations of
X.
The heart of the proofs will be to show that the map
{{\mathcal{A}}}\to{{\mathcal{B}}}
is surjective. We do this as follows:

We take an element of
{{\mathcal{B}}}
and represent it by a relative harmonic form on
X^{{\rm reg}}.
We lift it to a relative differential form which will represent an element of
{{\mathcal{A}}}.
We then seek a relative harmonic form whose restriction to
\spec{{\mathbb{C}}}
will be the original harmonic form.

(1.1)

For more precise statements see Theorem
1.5
and (
1.2
) below.

We begin in §§
2
–
7
with the study of harmonic forms with respect to Kähler conifold metrics. In §
2
we deal with Riemannian cones, which are the model at singularities of the Kähler conifold metrics. The main result of the section is as follows:

Theorem 1.4

(Theorem
2.10
)
.

Let
C
be a Riemannian cone and
\phi
a harmonic form on
C^{{\rm reg}}.
Then
\phi
may be written as an infinite sum (
2.12
) of homogeneous harmonic forms without logarithm terms.

We prove this partly because it is itself of interest. It implies the other known results
[
31
, Remark B.3]
,
[
33
, Proposition 2.4]
and
[
34
, Lemma 3.15]
to the effect that no logarithm terms exist.

In §
3
we deal with Kähler cones, which are the cones on compact Sasakian manifolds. In §
4
we show that every compact Calabi–Yau conifold has Kähler conifold metrics. The main result is Lemma
4.5
. It says that we can glue in the Kähler cone metric without making any change at the points far from
X^{\rm sing}.
More precisely, we start with any Kähler form on
X
which is defined at every point including
X^{\rm sing}.
We modify it only near
X^{\rm sing}
by changing the Käher potential near
X^{\rm sing}.
For this we use Lemma
4.5
. The result, the conifold metric, is defined only on
X^{{\rm reg}}.
Here we do not need to think of Ricci-flat Kähler metrics as in
[
31
]
. Note that even for
X
non-singular we do not need to choose Ricci-flat Kähler metrics to prove the original statement of Bomologov, Tian and Todorov.

In Proposition
4.15
we show also that
H^{1}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})=0
unless
X^{\rm sing}
is empty. For this we use again the result of
[
2
]
.

In §§
5
–
7
we prove the results we shall need about harmonic forms on compact Kähler conifolds. The key issue is as follows. Let
\phi
be a harmonic
(p,q)
form (which satisfies the Laplace equation
\Delta\phi=0).
For
X
non-singular the integration by parts formula implies immediately that
{\rm d}\phi={\rm d}^{*}\phi=0.
For
X
singular however we do not know whether
{\rm d}\phi,{\rm d}^{*}\phi
decay so fast that the integration by parts formula will hold. This is true in the circumstances of Theorem
1.2
(in Lemma
7.1
). We can verify it by expanding
{\rm d}\phi
into the sum of homogeneous harmonic forms on the Kähler cones. The results of §
2
and the Betti number condition imply then the vanishing of those terms which will prevent us from getting the integration by parts formula.

There is also a similar step in the proof of Theorem
1.3
. The cohomology group
H^{1}(X,\Theta_{X})
relevant to the locally trivial deformations are not directly related to harmonic forms. The condition
H^{2}_{X^{\rm sing}}(X,\Omega^{n-2}_{X})
implies that it is isomorphic to a certain vector space of harmonic forms; for more details see Theorem
5.19
.

In §
8
we recall the standard algebraic geometry facts we will use. In particular, we give the more precise meaning to the conclusion of Theorem
1.2
. We recall also the versions we will use of
T^{1}
lift theorems. In §
9
we collect the facts we will use about Kähler forms and its relative versions. For this purpose we will work with
{{\mathbb{R}}}
-algebras, not with
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebras as we normally do in deformation theory; for more details see Definitions
8.12
,
9.4
and
9.7
. In §
10
we show that the standard tensor calculus on Kähler manifolds extend to their infinitesimal deformations. In §
11
we study the notion of
C^{\infty}
deformations we shall need for (
1.1
).

In §
12
we prove Theorem
1.2
. The key result may be stated as follows.

Theorem 1.5

(Theorem
12.7
)
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler
n
-conifold whose singularities are rational and of depth
\geqslant n,
satisfying the Betti number condition of Theorem
1.2
. Let
A
be an Artin local
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebra and
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a deformation of
X.
The natural map
{}_{c}H^{n-2}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})\to{}_{c}H^{n-2}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{1}_{X})
is then surjective.

The cohomology groups with left lower index c are defined in Definition
6.1
. Theorem
1.5
implies that we can in principle do the same computation of cohomology groups as in
[
28
, Theorem 2.2]
. As a result we can apply the
T^{1}
lift theorem to deduce Theorem
1.2
.

In §
13
and §
14
we prove Theorem
1.3
. To do so we go back to the little steps in §
6
and make sure that they generalize to infinitesimal deformations. As a consequence the two sections are rather long. The key result is the following, Theorem
14.11
: let
k\geqslant 0
be an integer and put
A_{k}:={{\mathbb{C}}}[t]/(t^{k+1}),
and let
X_{k}/A_{k}
be a deformation of
X;
then the
A_{k}
module
H^{1}(X,\Theta_{X_{k}/A_{k}})
is isomorphic to the space of relative harmonic
(n-1,1)
forms. We then carry out (
1.1
) and prove that the following holds.

Denote by
X_{k-1}/A_{k-1}
the deformation of
X
defined by
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X_{k-1}}:={{\mathcal{O}}}_{X_{k}}\otimes_{A_{k}}A_{k-1}.
The natural map
H^{1}(X,\Theta_{X_{k}/A_{k}})\to H^{1}(X,\Theta_{X_{k-1}/A_{k-1}})
is then surjective.

(1.2)

This with the
T^{1}
lift theorem implies Theorem
1.3
.

In §
15
we make remarks on the hypothesis
H^{2}_{X^{\rm sing}}(X,\Omega^{n-2}_{X})=0
of Theorem
1.3
. This is a condition on the singularities and the ordinary double points for instance do not satisfy it. We show that the affine cone on a toric Fano manifold satisfies it.

The Betti number hypothesis of Theorem
1.2
will in general be easier to verify. For instance, if
(C,{\rm vx})
is an ordinary double point then
C^{{\rm reg}}
is diffeomorphic to
T^{*}S^{n}\setminus S^{n},
the complement to the zero-section, and homotopic to an
S^{n-1}
bundle over
S^{n}.
So it satisfies the condition
b_{n-2}(C^{{\rm reg}})=0.

The readers interested only in the proof of Theorem
1.2
can skip §
6
and §
10
. They can also keep supposing that
{{\mathbb{K}}}={{\mathbb{C}}}
wherever we treat Artin
{{\mathbb{K}}}
-algebras in §
8
, §
9
, §
11
and §
12
; or in other words, they will not need the notion of relative Kähler forms with
{{\mathbb{K}}}={{\mathbb{R}}}.

2
Riemannian Cones

We begin by defining Riemannian cones.

Definition 2.1
.

A
Riemannian cone
is the data
(C,{\rm vx},C^{{\rm reg}},C^{{\rm lk}},r,g^{{\rm lk}})
where
C
is a metric space,
{\rm vx}
a point of
C,

C^{{\rm reg}}
the subset
C\setminus\{{\rm vx}\}
which is given a manifold structure,
C^{{\rm lk}}
a compact manifold without boundary such that there is a diffeomorphism
C^{{\rm reg}}\cong(0,\infty)\times C^{{\rm lk}}
which we will fix,
r
the composite of the diffeomorphism
C^{{\rm reg}}\cong(0,\infty)\times C^{{\rm lk}}
and the projection
(0,\infty)\times C^{{\rm lk}}\to(0,\infty),
and
g^{{\rm lk}}
a Riemannian metric on
C^{{\rm lk}}
such that the metric space structure of
C^{{\rm reg}}
is induced by the Riemannian metric
{\rm d}r^{2}+r^{2}g^{{\rm lk}}.
We call
{\rm vx}
the
vertex,

C^{{\rm lk}}
the
link,

r:C^{{\rm reg}}\to(0,\infty)
the
radius function
and
{\rm d}r^{2}+r^{2}g^{{\rm lk}}
the
cone metric.

We call
C
a Riemannian
l
-cone
if
C^{{\rm reg}}
is a manifold of dimension
l,
which is thus the real dimension.

We define homogeneous
p
-forms and harmonic
p
-forms on Riemannian cones.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
C
be a Riemannian cone and
p
an integer. Denote by
\Lambda^{p}_{C^{{\rm reg}}}
the sheaf on
C^{{\rm reg}}
of
C^{\infty}

p
-forms with complex coefficients. We say that
\phi\in\Gamma(\Lambda^{p}_{C^{{\rm reg}}})
is
homogeneous of order
\alpha\in{{\mathbb{C}}}
if
\phi=e^{(\alpha+p)\log r}({\rm d}\log r\wedge\phi^{\prime}+\phi^{\prime\prime})
where
r
is the radius function on
C^{{\rm reg}},

\phi^{\prime}
some
p-1
form on
C^{{\rm lk}},
and
\phi^{\prime\prime}
some
p
-form on
C^{{\rm lk}}.

We say that
\phi\in\Gamma(\Lambda^{p}_{C^{{\rm reg}}})
is
harmonic
if
\Delta\phi=0
where
\Delta
is computed with respect to the cone metric of
C^{{\rm reg}}.

We compute
{\rm d},{\rm d}^{*}
and
\Delta
on Riemannian cones.

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
C
be a Riemannian
l
-cone and
r:C^{{\rm reg}}\to(0,\infty)
its radius function. Denote by
\pi:C^{{\rm reg}}\cong(0,\infty)\times C^{{\rm lk}}\to C^{{\rm lk}}
the projection onto the second component. Define for
p\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
a
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-vector space isomorphism
\Gamma(\Lambda^{p}_{C^{{\rm reg}}})\cong\Gamma(\pi^{*}\Lambda^{p-1}_{C^{{\rm lk}}})\oplus\Gamma(\pi^{*}\Lambda^{p}_{C^{{\rm lk}}})
by writing each
\phi\in\Gamma(\Lambda^{p}_{C^{{\rm reg}}})
as
{\rm d}\log r\wedge\phi^{\prime}+\phi^{\prime\prime}
for some
\phi^{\prime}\in\Gamma(\pi^{*}\Lambda^{p-1}_{C^{{\rm reg}}})
and
\phi^{\prime\prime}\in\Gamma(\pi^{*}\Lambda^{p}_{C^{{\rm reg}}}).
Using these isomorphisms write the de Rham differential as
{\rm d}:\Gamma(\pi^{*}\Lambda^{p-1}_{C^{{\rm lk}}})\oplus\Gamma(\pi^{*}\Lambda^{p}_{C^{{\rm lk}}})\to\Gamma(\pi^{*}\Lambda^{p}_{C^{{\rm lk}}})\oplus\Gamma(\pi^{*}\Lambda^{p+1}_{C^{{\rm lk}}}).
Using the cone metric of
C^{{\rm reg}}
define
{\rm d}^{*}
and
\Delta,
and write them as
{\rm d}^{*}:\Gamma(\pi^{*}\Lambda^{p-1}_{C^{{\rm lk}}})\oplus\Gamma(\pi^{*}\Lambda^{p}_{C^{{\rm lk}}})\to\Gamma(\pi^{*}\Lambda^{p-2}_{C^{{\rm lk}}})\oplus\Gamma(\pi^{*}\Lambda^{p-1}_{C^{{\rm lk}}})
and

\Delta:\Gamma(\pi^{*}\Lambda^{p-1}_{C^{{\rm lk}}})\oplus\Gamma(\pi^{*}\Lambda^{p}_{C^{{\rm lk}}})\to\Gamma(\pi^{*}\Lambda^{p-1}C^{{\rm lk}})\oplus\Gamma(\pi^{*}\Lambda^{p}_{C^{{\rm lk}}})
respectively. These may then be expressed as matrices as follows:

{\rm d}=\begin{pmatrix}-{\rm d}&r\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\\
0&{\rm d}\end{pmatrix},

r^{2}{\rm d}^{*}=\begin{pmatrix}-{\rm d}^{*}&0\\
-r\frac{\partial}{\partial r}+2p-l&{\rm d}^{*}\end{pmatrix}
and

r^{2}\Delta=\begin{pmatrix}-(r\frac{\partial}{\partial r})^{2}&0\\
0&-(r\frac{\partial}{\partial r})^{2}\end{pmatrix}+(2+2p-l)\begin{pmatrix}r\frac{\partial}{\partial r}&0\\
0&r\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}\Delta+2l-4p&-2{\rm d}^{*}\\
-2{\rm d}&\Delta\end{pmatrix}
where
{\rm d},{\rm d}^{*}
and
\Delta
are computed on
C^{{\rm reg}}
on the left-hand sides and on
C^{{\rm lk}}
on the right-hand sides.
In particular, if
\phi=r^{\beta}({\rm d}\log r\wedge\phi^{\prime}+\phi^{\prime\prime})
is a homogeneous
p
-form of order
\beta-p\in{{\mathbb{C}}}
on
C^{{\rm reg}}
then

\begin{split}{\rm d}\phi=&\beta r^{\beta-1}{\rm d}r\wedge\phi^{\prime\prime}+r^{\beta}{\rm d}\phi^{\prime\prime}-r^{\beta-1}{\rm d}r\wedge{\rm d}\phi^{\prime},\\
{\rm d}^{*}\phi=&r^{\beta-2}{\rm d}^{*}\phi^{\prime\prime}-(\beta+l-2p)r^{\beta-2}\phi^{\prime}-r^{\beta-3}{\rm d}r\wedge{\rm d}^{*}\phi^{\prime},\\
\Delta\phi=&r^{\beta-2}{\rm d}\log r\wedge[\Delta\phi^{\prime}-(\beta-2)(\beta+l-2p)\phi^{\prime}-2{\rm d}^{*}\phi^{\prime\prime}]\\
&+r^{\beta-2}[\Delta\phi^{\prime\prime}-\beta(\beta+l-2-2p)\phi^{\prime\prime}-2{\rm d}\phi^{\prime}]\end{split}

(2.1)

where again
{\rm d},{\rm d}^{*}
and
\Delta
are computed on
C^{{\rm reg}}
on the left-hand sides and on
C^{{\rm lk}}
on the right-hand sides.

Proof.

These are the results of straightforward computation. The details about (
2.1
) for
l
even are given for instance by Chan
[
13
, Proposition 3.3]
. His computation applies to every
l
and implies also the matrix expressions above.
∎

We study homogeneous harmonic forms on Riemannian cones.

Proposition 2.4
.

Let
C
be a Riemannian
l
-cone and
p
an integer. Denote by
{{\mathcal{D}}}\subseteq{{\mathbb{C}}}
the set of
\alpha
for which there exists a non-zero
\phi\in\Gamma(\Lambda^{p}_{C^{{\rm reg}}})
homogeneous of order
\alpha
and satisfying
\Delta\phi=0
with respect to the cone metric of
C^{{\rm reg}}.
Then
{{\mathcal{D}}}\subseteq{{\mathbb{R}}}
and
{{\mathcal{D}}}
is discrete.

Proof.

Take
\alpha\in{{\mathcal{D}}}
and let
\phi\in\Gamma(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathbb{C}}}^{{\rm reg}}})
be non-zero, homogeneous of order
\alpha
and with
\Delta\phi=0.
Put
\beta:=\alpha+p
and use the notation of Proposition
2.3
. The equation (
2.1
) implies then

\displaystyle\Delta\phi^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(\beta-2)(\beta+l-2p)\phi^{\prime}+2{\rm d}^{*}\phi^{\prime\prime},

(2.2)

\displaystyle\Delta\phi^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\beta(\beta+l-2-2p)\phi^{\prime\prime}+2{\rm d}\phi^{\prime}.

(2.3)

Applying
{\rm d}
to (
2.3
) we find that
{\rm d}{\rm d}^{*}{\rm d}\phi^{\prime\prime}=\beta(\beta+l-2-2p){\rm d}\phi^{\prime\prime}.
So if
{\rm d}\phi^{\prime\prime}\neq 0
then
\beta(\beta+l-2-2p)
is an eigenvalue of the Laplacian, which implies that
\alpha=\beta-p
lies in a discrete subset of
{{\mathbb{R}}}
independent of
\phi.
Suppose therefore that
{\rm d}\phi^{\prime\prime}=0.
Put
\psi:={\rm d}\phi^{\prime}
so that (
2.3
) becomes

\Delta\phi^{\prime\prime}=\beta(\beta+l-2-2p)\phi^{\prime\prime}+2\psi.

(2.4)

Applying
{\rm d}
to (
2.2
) and using
{\rm d}\phi^{\prime}=\psi,

{\rm d}\phi^{\prime\prime}=0
and (
2.4
) we find that

\begin{split}\Delta\psi&=(\beta-2)(\beta+l-2p)\psi+2\Delta\phi^{\prime\prime}\\
&=(\beta-2)(\beta+l-2p)\psi+2\beta(\beta+l-2-2p)\phi^{\prime\prime}+4\psi\\
&=2\beta(\beta+l-2-2p)\phi^{\prime\prime}+[(\beta-2)(\beta+l-2p)+4]\psi.\end{split}

(2.5)

This and (
2.4
) imply
(\Delta\phi^{\prime\prime},\Delta\psi)=(\phi^{\prime\prime},\psi)M
where

M:=\begin{pmatrix}\beta(\beta+l-2-2p)&2\beta(\beta+l-2-2p)\\
2&(\beta-2)(\beta+l-2p)+4\end{pmatrix}.

(2.6)

This matrix is diagonalizable; and in fact,
P^{-1}MP=D

where

P=\begin{pmatrix}\beta+l-2-2p&\beta\\
1&-1\end{pmatrix}\text{ and }D:=\begin{pmatrix}\beta(\beta+l-2p)&0\\
0&(\beta-2)(\beta+l-2-2p)\end{pmatrix}.

(2.7)

So

(\Delta\phi^{\prime\prime},\Delta\psi)P=(\phi^{\prime\prime},\psi)PD
and looking at the first component we see that

\Delta[(\beta+l-2-2p)\phi^{\prime\prime}+\psi]=\beta(\beta+l-2p)[(\beta+l-2-2p)\phi^{\prime\prime}+\psi].

(2.8)

Thus if
(\beta+l-2-2p)\phi^{\prime\prime}+\psi\neq 0
then
\beta(\beta+l-2p)
is an eigenvalue of the Laplacian, which implies that
\alpha=\beta-p
lies in a discrete subset of
{{\mathbb{R}}}
independent of
\phi.
Suppose therefore that
(\beta+l-2-2p)\phi^{\prime\prime}+\psi=0.
Then by (
2.3
) we have

\Delta\phi^{\prime\prime}=\beta(\beta+l-2-2p)\phi^{\prime\prime}-2(\beta+l-2-2p)\phi^{\prime\prime}=(\beta-2)(\beta-2+l-2p)\phi^{\prime\prime}.

(2.9)

So if
\phi^{\prime\prime}\neq 0
then
(\beta-2)(\beta-2+l-2p)
is an eigenvalue of the Laplacian, which implies that
\alpha=\beta-p
lies in a discrete subset of
{{\mathbb{R}}}
independent of
\phi.
Suppose therefore that
\phi^{\prime\prime}=0.
Then by (
2.2
) we have
\Delta\phi^{\prime}=(\beta-2)(\beta+l-2p)\phi^{\prime}.
But by hypothesis
\phi\neq 0.
So
(\beta-2)(\beta-2+l-2p)
is an eigenvalue of the Laplacian, which implies that
\alpha=\beta-p
lies in a discrete subset of
{{\mathbb{R}}}
independent of
\phi.
This completes the proof.
∎

From the computation above we get the following three corollaries.

Corollary 2.5
.

Let
C
be a Riemannian
l
-cone and
p>\frac{l}{2}
an integer. Let
\phi\in\Gamma(\Lambda^{p}_{C^{{\rm reg}}})
be homogeneous of order
\alpha\in(-p,p-l)
and satisfy
\Delta\phi=0
with respect to the cone metric of
C^{{\rm reg}}.
Write
\phi=r^{p+\alpha}({\rm d}\log r\wedge\phi^{\prime}+\phi^{\prime\prime})
as in Proposition
2.3
. Then
{\rm d}\phi^{\prime}=(2+p-l-\alpha)\phi^{\prime\prime}.

Proof.

Put
\beta:=p+\alpha\in(0,2p-l)
and follow the proof of Proposition
2.4
. Applying again
{\rm d}
to (
2.3
) we find
{\rm d}{\rm d}^{*}{\rm d}\phi^{\prime\prime}=\beta(\beta+l-2-2p){\rm d}\phi^{\prime\prime}.
But now
\beta(\beta+l-2-2p)<0
so
{\rm d}\phi^{\prime\prime}=0.
Put again
\psi:={\rm d}\phi^{\prime}.
Then (
10.2
) holds; that is,

\Delta[(\beta+l-2-2p)\phi^{\prime\prime}+\psi]=\beta(\beta+l-2p)[(\beta+l-2-2p)\phi^{\prime\prime}+\psi].

(2.10)

But now
\beta(\beta+l-2p)<0
so
(\beta+l-2-2p)\phi^{\prime\prime}+\psi=0
as we have to prove.
∎

Corollary 2.6
.

Let
C
be a Riemannian
l
-cone and
p>\frac{l}{2}+1
an integer. Let
\phi\in\Gamma(\Lambda^{p}_{C^{{\rm reg}}})
be homogeneous of order
\alpha\in(2-p,p-l)
and satisfy
\Delta\phi=0
with respect to the cone metric of
C^{{\rm reg}}.
Then
\phi=0.

Proof.

As
p,\alpha
satisfy the hypotheses of Corollary
2.5
we can use its result; that is,
writing again
\phi=r^{\beta}(\phi^{\prime\prime}+{\rm d}\log r\wedge\phi^{\prime})
we have
{\rm d}\phi^{\prime}=(2+p-\alpha-l)\phi^{\prime\prime}=(2+2p-\beta-l)\phi^{\prime\prime}
with
\beta:=p+\alpha\in(2,2p-l).

Equation (
2.4
) holds too with
\psi:={\rm d}\phi^{\prime}
and

\Delta\phi^{\prime\prime}=\beta(\beta+l-2-2p)\phi^{\prime\prime}-2(\beta+l-2-2p)\phi^{\prime\prime}=(\beta-2)(\beta+l-2-2p)\phi^{\prime\prime}.

But
\beta\in(2,2p-l)
and
(\beta-2)(\beta+l-2-2p)<0
so
\phi^{\prime\prime}=0.

Equation (
2.2
) implies then
\Delta\phi^{\prime}=(\beta-2)(\beta+l-2p)\phi^{\prime}.

But again
\beta\in(2,2p-l)
so
\phi^{\prime}=0.
Thus
\phi=0.

∎

Corollary 2.7
.

Let
C
be a Riemannian
l
-cone and
p<\frac{l}{2}-1
an integer. Let
\phi\in\Gamma(\Lambda^{p}_{C^{{\rm reg}}})
be homogeneous of order
\alpha\in(2+p-l,-p)
and satisfy
\Delta\phi=0
with respect to the cone metric of
C^{{\rm reg}}.
Then
\phi=0.

Proof.

Put
q:=l-p.
Then
\alpha\in(2-q,q-l).
Suppose first that
C^{{\rm reg}}
is orientable. Then we can define the Hodge dual
*\phi
as a homogeneous harmonic
q
-form of order
\alpha,
to which we can apply Corollary
2.6
. So
*\phi=0
and
\phi=0.

If
C^{{\rm reg}}
is unorientable then the result we have just obtained applies to the pull-back of
\phi
to the double cover of
C^{{\rm reg}};
that is, the pull-back vanishes and accordingly so does
\phi.

∎

The following may be proved by the separation of variable method; see for instance
[
57
, Part I, Equation 5.8]
.

Proposition 2.8
.

Let
C
be a Riemannian
l
-cone and
\pi:C^{{\rm reg}}\cong(0,\infty)\times C^{{\rm lk}}\to C^{{\rm lk}}
the projection onto the second component. Let
V
be a finite-rank
C^{\infty}
complex vector bundle over
C^{{\rm lk}},
equipped with a Hermitian metric. Let
E:C^{\infty}(V)\to C^{\infty}(V)
be a self-adjoint second-order linear elliptic operator with eigenvalues
\lambda_{0}\leqslant\penalty\lambda_{1}\leqslant\penalty\lambda_{2}\leqslant\penalty\cdots
which, as is well known, tend to
\infty.
Let
(e_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
be a complete orthonormal system of
L^{2}(V)
where each
e_{j}
is an eigenvector of
E
with eigenvalue
\lambda_{j}.
Fix
m\in{{\mathbb{R}}}
and consider the operator
(r\frac{\partial}{\partial r})^{2}-2mr\frac{\partial}{\partial r}-E:C^{\infty}(\pi^{*}V)\to C^{\infty}(\pi^{*}V).
Let this be an elliptic operator. For
j\in\{0,1,2,\dots\}
with
\lambda_{j}\neq-m^{2}
denote by
\alpha_{j},\beta_{j}\in{{\mathbb{C}}}
the two distinct roots of the polynomial
\xi^{2}-2m\xi-\lambda_{j}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}[\xi].
Let
u\in C^{\infty}(\pi^{*}V)
satisfy the equation
[(r\frac{\partial}{\partial r})^{2}-2mr\frac{\partial}{\partial r}-E]u=0.
Then there exist two sequences
(a_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty},(b_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
of complex numbers such that

u=\sum_{\lambda_{j}\neq-m^{2}}(a_{j}e^{\alpha_{j}\log r}+b_{j}e^{\beta_{j}\log r})e_{j}+\sum_{\lambda_{j}=-m^{2}}(a_{j}+b_{j}\log r)r^{m}e_{j}

(2.11)

which converges in the compact
C^{\infty}
sense. The same result holds also for
u
defined only on some open set in
C^{{\rm reg}}.
∎

Applying Proposition
2.8
to the
p
-form Laplacian, we prove

Corollary 2.9
.

Let
C
be a Riemannian
l
-cone, fix
p\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
and put
m:=1+p-\frac{l}{2}.
Define a self-adjoint elliptic operator
E:\Gamma(\pi^{*}\Lambda^{p-1}_{C^{{\rm lk}}})\oplus\Gamma(\pi^{*}\Lambda^{p}_{C^{{\rm lk}}})\to\Gamma(\pi^{*}\Lambda^{p-1}_{C^{{\rm lk}}})\oplus\Gamma(\Lambda^{p}_{C^{{\rm lk}}})
by
E:=\begin{pmatrix}\Delta+2l-4p&-2{\rm d}^{*}\\
-2{\rm d}&\Delta\end{pmatrix}
where
{\rm d},{\rm d}^{*}
and
\Delta
are computed on
C^{{\rm lk}}.
Let
(e_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
be a complete orthonormal system of
L^{2}(\pi^{*}\Lambda^{p-1}_{C^{{\rm lk}}})\oplus L^{2}(\pi^{*}\Lambda^{p}_{C^{{\rm lk}}})
which consists of eigenvectors of
E
with eigenvalues
\lambda_{0}\leqslant\penalty\lambda_{1}\leqslant\penalty\lambda_{2}\leqslant\penalty\cdots
tending to
\infty.
Then for every
j=0,1,2,\dots
we have
\lambda_{j}\geqslant-m^{2}.
Moreover the following holds.

For
j\in\{0,1,2,\dots\}
with
\lambda_{j}>-m^{2}
denote by
\alpha_{j}>\beta_{j}
the two distinct real roots of the polynomial
\xi^{2}-2m\xi-\lambda_{j}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}[\xi].
Let
\phi
be a section of
\Lambda^{p}_{C^{{\rm reg}}}
over some open set of
C^{{\rm reg}},
satisfying
\Delta\phi=0
with respect to the cone metric. Then there exist two sequences
(a_{j})_{\lambda_{j}\geqslant{-m^{2}}},(b_{j})_{\lambda_{j}\geqslant{-m^{2}}}
of complex numbers such that

\phi=\sum_{\lambda_{j}>-m^{2}}(a_{j}r^{\alpha_{j}}+b_{j}r^{\beta_{j}})e_{j}+\sum_{\lambda_{j}=-m^{2}}(a_{j}+b_{j}\log r)r^{m}e_{j}

(2.12)

which converges in the compact
C^{\infty}
sense.

Proof.

Proposition
2.3
implies
-r^{2}\Delta=(r\frac{\partial}{\partial r})^{2}-2mr\frac{\partial}{\partial r}-E,
to which we can certainly apply Proposition
2.8
.
Notice that for any
j
with
\lambda_{j}<-m^{2}
the two distinct real roots of the polynomial
\xi^{2}-2m\xi-\lambda_{j}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}[\xi]
are not real numbers. Proposition
2.4
implies therefore that no such
j
exists. So
\lambda_{j}\geqslant-m^{2}
for every
j.
The latter part is an immediate consequence of Proposition
2.8
.
∎

We prove that no logarithm terms appear in (
2.12
).

Theorem 2.10
.

In the circumstances of Corollary
2.9
no
j\in\{0,1,2,\dots\}
is such that
\lambda_{j}=-m^{2};
in particular, (
2.12
) becomes a sum of homogeneous harmonic
p
-forms.

Proof.

Suppose contrarily that there exists some
j
with
\lambda_{j}=-m^{2}.
Putting
\phi:=r^{m}e_{j}
we have then
\Delta\phi=\Delta[(\log r)\phi]=0
where
\Delta
is computed on
C^{{\rm reg}}.
Direct computation shows that

\textstyle({\rm d}+{\rm d}^{*})[(\log r)\phi]=\frac{1}{r}{\rm d}r\wedge\phi-\frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\mathbin{\lrcorner}\phi+(\log r)({\rm d}+{\rm d}^{*})\phi

(2.13)

where
{\rm d},{\rm d}^{*}
are computed on
C^{{\rm reg}}.
Applying
{\rm d}+{\rm d}^{*}
to these and looking at the degree-
p
parts, we get

0=\textstyle\Delta[(\log r)\phi]={\rm d}^{*}(\frac{1}{r}{\rm d}r\wedge\phi)-{\rm d}(\frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\mathbin{\lrcorner}\phi)+({\rm d}+{\rm d}^{*})[(\log r)({\rm d}+{\rm d}^{*})\phi].

(2.14)

Applying (
2.13
) to
({\rm d}+{\rm d}^{*})\phi
in place of
\phi,
using the equation
\Delta\phi=0
and looking at the degree-
p
parts, we get

\begin{split}({\rm d}+{\rm d}^{*})[\log r({\rm d}+{\rm d}^{*})\phi]&=\textstyle\frac{1}{r}{\rm d}r\wedge({\rm d}+{\rm d}^{*})\phi-\frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\mathbin{\lrcorner}({\rm d}+{\rm d}^{*})\phi\\
&=\textstyle\frac{1}{r}{\rm d}r\wedge{\rm d}^{*}\phi-\frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\mathbin{\lrcorner}{\rm d}\phi.\end{split}

(2.15)

By (
2.14
) and (
2.15
) we have

\textstyle{\rm d}^{*}(\frac{1}{r}{\rm d}r\wedge\phi)-{\rm d}(\frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\mathbin{\lrcorner}\phi)+\frac{1}{r}{\rm d}r\wedge{\rm d}^{*}\phi-\frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\mathbin{\lrcorner}{\rm d}\phi=0.

(2.16)

Write

\phi:=r^{m}({\rm d}\log r\wedge\phi^{\prime}+\phi^{\prime\prime})

where
\phi^{\prime}
is a
(p-1)
form on
C^{{\rm lk}}
and
\phi^{\prime\prime}
a
p
-form on
C^{{\rm lk}}.
Using (
2.1
) with
\alpha=m
we see that the first term
{\rm d}^{*}(\frac{1}{r}{\rm d}r\wedge\phi)={\rm d}^{*}(r^{m}{\rm d}\log r\wedge\phi^{\prime\prime})
on the left-hand side of (
2.16
) is equal to

(m-2)r^{m-2}\phi^{\prime\prime}-r^{m-3}{\rm d}r\wedge{\rm d}^{*}\phi^{\prime\prime}

(2.17)

where
{\rm d}^{*}
is computed on
C^{{\rm lk}}.
The second term
-{\rm d}(\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}\mathbin{\lrcorner}\phi)=-{\rm d}(r^{m-2}\phi^{\prime})
on the left-hand side of (
2.16
) is equal to

-(m-2)r^{m-3}{\rm d}r\wedge\phi^{\prime}-r^{m-2}{\rm d}\phi^{\prime}.

(2.18)

Using (
2.1
) with
\alpha=m
we see that the third term on the left-hand side of (
2.16
) is equal to

\textstyle\frac{1}{r}{\rm d}r\wedge{\rm d}^{*}\phi={\rm d}r\wedge[r^{m-3}{\rm d}^{*}\phi^{\prime\prime}+(m-2)r^{m-3}\phi^{\prime}].

(2.19)

where
{\rm d}^{*}\phi^{\prime\prime}
is computed on
C^{{\rm lk}}.
The fouth term on the left-hand side of (
2.16
) is equal to

\textstyle-\frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\mathbin{\lrcorner}{\rm d}\phi=-mr^{m-2}\phi^{\prime\prime}+r^{m-2}{\rm d}\phi^{\prime}

(2.20)

All the terms on (
2.17
)–(
2.20
) cancel out except the first term on (
2.17
) and the first term on (
2.20
). So (
2.16
) becomes

-2r^{m-2}\phi^{\prime\prime}=0;
that is,
\phi^{\prime\prime}=0.
Using (
2.1
) with
\alpha=m
we see now that
\Delta\phi^{\prime}+(m-2)^{2}\phi^{\prime}=0
where
\Delta
is computed on
C^{{\rm lk}}.
But
\phi\neq 0
implies
\phi^{\prime}\neq 0
so
-(m-2)^{2}\leqslant\penalty 0
is an eigenvalue of
\Delta,
which must therefore vanish.
Thus
m=2;
that is,
p=\frac{l}{2}+1.

Notice now that the
(l-p)
form Laplacian may be written as
(r\frac{\partial}{\partial r})^{2}-2\mu r\frac{\partial}{\partial r}-{{\mathcal{E}}}u
with
\mu:=1+(l-p)-\frac{l}{2}=1-p+\frac{l}{2}
and
{{\mathcal{E}}}
defined over
C^{{\rm lk}}.
Computation shows
*\phi=:r^{\mu}\epsilon
with
\epsilon=({\rm d}\log r\wedge\epsilon^{\prime}+\epsilon^{\prime\prime})
for some
\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime\prime}
defined on
C^{{\rm lk}}.
Since
*\phi
is harmonic it follows that
\epsilon
is an eigenvector of
{{\mathcal{E}}}
with eigenvalue
-\mu^{2}.
So we can apply the result of the paragraph above with
l-p
in place of
p;
that is,
\mu=2
and
l-p=\frac{l}{2}+1.
But this contradicts
p=\frac{l}{2}+1,
completing the proof.
∎

We prove the following fact about order
-p
homogeneous harmonic
p
-forms.

Corollary 2.11
.

Let
C
be a Riemannian
l
-cone,
p\leqslant\penalty\frac{l}{2}-1
an integer, and
\phi
an order
-p
homogeneous harmonic
p
-form on
C^{{\rm reg}}.
Then
{\rm d}\phi={\rm d}^{*}\phi=0
where
{\rm d}^{*}
is computed with respect to the cone metric on
C^{{\rm reg}}.

Proof.

We compute
\Delta,{\rm d}
and
{\rm d}^{*}
on
C^{{\rm lk}}
except at the end of the proof.
By (
2.1
) with
\beta=0
we have

\Delta\phi^{\prime}=-2(l-2p)\phi^{\prime}+2{\rm d}^{*}\phi^{\prime\prime}\text{ and }\Delta\phi^{\prime\prime}=2{\rm d}\phi^{\prime}

(2.21)

Applying
{\rm d}^{*}
to the first equation, we find
{\rm d}^{*}\Delta\phi^{\prime}=-2(l-2p){\rm d}^{*}\phi^{\prime}.
The left-hand side is equal to
\Delta{\rm d}^{*}\phi^{\prime}
and so
{\rm d}^{*}\phi^{\prime}
is an eigenvector with eigenvalue
-2(l-2p)<0
of
\Delta.
Thus
{\rm d}^{*}\phi^{\prime}=0.

Applying
{\rm d}
to the second equation of (
2.21
) we find
{\rm d}\Delta\phi^{\prime\prime}=0.
So
{\rm d}^{*}{\rm d}{\rm d}^{*}{\rm d}\phi^{\prime\prime}=0.
Doing twice the integration by parts, we get
{\rm d}\phi^{\prime\prime}=0.
Now (
2.21
) becomes

{\rm d}^{*}{\rm d}\phi^{\prime}=-2(l-2p)\phi^{\prime}+2{\rm d}^{*}\phi^{\prime\prime}\text{ and }{\rm d}{\rm d}^{*}\phi^{\prime\prime}=2{\rm d}\phi^{\prime}.

(2.22)

Applying
{\rm d}^{*}
to the second equation and using then the first equation, we find

{\rm d}^{*}{\rm d}{\rm d}^{*}\phi^{\prime\prime}=2{\rm d}^{*}{\rm d}\phi^{\prime}=-4(l-2p)\phi^{\prime}+4{\rm d}^{*}\phi^{\prime\prime}.

(2.23)

Applying
{\rm d}
to both sides and using the second equation of (
2.22
) we get

\begin{split}{\rm d}{\rm d}^{*}{\rm d}{\rm d}^{*}\phi^{\prime\prime}=2{\rm d}^{*}{\rm d}\phi^{\prime}=-4(l-2p){\rm d}\phi^{\prime}+4{\rm d}{\rm d}^{*}\phi^{\prime\prime}\\
=-2(l-2p){\rm d}{\rm d}^{*}\phi^{\prime\prime}+4{\rm d}{\rm d}^{*}\phi^{\prime\prime}=-2(l-2p-2){\rm d}{\rm d}^{*}\phi^{\prime\prime}.\end{split}

(2.24)

As the left-hand side is equal to
\Delta{\rm d}{\rm d}^{*}\phi^{\prime\prime}
the vector
{\rm d}{\rm d}^{*}\phi^{\prime\prime}
is an eigenvector with eigenvalue
-2(l-2p-2)\leqslant\penalty 0
of
\Delta.
If
p<\frac{l}{2}-1
then
{\rm d}{\rm d}^{*}\phi^{\prime\prime}=0.
If
p=\frac{l}{2}-1
then (
2.24
) implies
{\rm d}{\rm d}^{*}{\rm d}{\rm d}^{*}\phi^{\prime\prime}=0
and hence it follows by integration by parts that even in this case we have
{\rm d}{\rm d}^{*}\phi^{\prime\prime}=0.
Doing again integration by parts we find
{\rm d}^{*}\phi^{\prime\prime}=0.
The first equation of (
2.22
) implies in turn that
{\rm d}^{*}{\rm d}\phi^{\prime}=-2(l-2p)\phi^{\prime}.
As
{\rm d}^{*}\phi^{\prime}=0
the left-hand side is equal to
\Delta\phi^{\prime},
so
\phi^{\prime}
is an eigenvector with negative eigenvalue of
\Delta.
Thus
\phi^{\prime}=0.
Now (
2.1
) with
\beta=0
implies
{\rm d}\phi={\rm d}^{*}\phi=0
where
{\rm d},{\rm d}^{*}
are computed on
C^{{\rm reg}}.

∎

In the same way we prove

Corollary 2.12
.

Let
C
be a Riemannian
l
-cone and
p\leqslant\penalty\frac{l}{2}-1
an integer with
b_{p}(C^{{\rm reg}})=0.
Let
\phi
be an order
2+p-l
homogeneous harmonic
p
-form on
C^{{\rm reg}}.
Then
\phi=0.

Proof.

We compute
\Delta,{\rm d}
and
{\rm d}^{*}
on
C^{{\rm lk}}.
By (
2.1
) with
\beta=2+p-l
the equation (
2.21
) holds as it is. Following the subsequent computation we see that
\phi^{\prime}=0
and that
\Delta\phi^{\prime\prime}=0.
But by hypothesis
b_{p}(C^{{\rm lk}})=b_{p}(C^{{\rm reg}})=0
so
\phi^{\prime\prime}=0
too. Thus
\phi=0.

∎

3
Kähler Cones

We begin by defining Kähler cones.

Definition 3.1
.

A
Kähler cone
is a Riemannian cone
C
whose regular part
C^{{\rm reg}}
is given a complex structure
J
with the following properties: the cone metric of
C^{{\rm reg}}
is a Kähler metric on
(C^{{\rm reg}},J);
and for each
t\in(0,\infty),
if we define a diffeomorphism
(0,\infty)\times C^{{\rm lk}}\to(0,\infty)\times C^{{\rm lk}}
by
(a,b)\mapsto(ta,b)
for
(a,b)\in(0,\infty)\times C^{{\rm lk}}
then the corresponding diffeomorphism
C^{{\rm reg}}\to C^{{\rm reg}}
is holomorphic.

We call
(C,J)
a Kähler
n
-cone if
(C^{{\rm reg}},J)
is a complex manifold of complex dimension
n.

It is known that the complex structure of a Kähler cone extends automatically to its vertex. We will recall this shortly after making a definition we shall need.

Definition 3.2
.

We say that the germ
(Y,y)
of a complex analytic space is
quasi-homogeneous
if there exist integers
k;w_{1},\dots,w_{k}\geqslant 1
and a complex analytic embedding
(Y,y)\subseteq({{\mathbb{C}}}^{k},0)
such that
(Y,y)
is invariant under the multiplicative group action
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{*}:={{\mathbb{C}}}\setminus\{0\}\curvearrowright{{\mathbb{C}}}^{k}
defined by
t\cdot(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})=(t^{w_{1}}z_{1},\dots,t^{w_{k}}z_{k}).

Theorem 3.3

(Theorem 3.1 of
[
14
]
)
.

Every Kähler cone
C
has the structure of a normal complex space which agrees with the complex manifold structure of
C^{{\rm reg}}
and whose germ
(C,{\rm vx})
is quasi-homogeneous. ∎

4
Compact Conifolds

We begin by recalling the definition of Sasakian manifolds.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
n\geqslant 1
be an integer and
M
a manifold of dimension
2n-1.
A
contact
form on
M
is a
1
-form
\eta\in C^{\infty}(T^{*}M)
such that the
2n-1
form
\eta\wedge({\rm d}\eta)^{n-1}
is nowhere vanishing. Corresponding to this
\eta
there exists a unique
\xi\in C^{\infty}(TM)
with
\eta(\xi)=1
and
\xi\mathbin{\lrcorner}{\rm d}\eta=0,
called the
Reeb
vector field of
(M,\eta).

A
Sasakian structure
on
M
is the pair of a contact form
\eta
and a section
\Phi\in C^{\infty}(\mathop{\rm End}TM)
such that if we denote by
\xi
the Reeb vector field of
(M,\eta)
then the following hold:
\Phi\xi=0\in C^{\infty}(TM);

\Phi
maps the sub-bundle
\mathop{\rm ker}\eta\subset TM
to itself, defining a compatible almost complex structure upon the symplectic vector bundle
(\mathop{\rm ker}\theta,{\rm d}\eta);
and for
u,v\in C^{\infty}(TM)
we have

[\Phi u,\Phi v]+\Phi^{2}[u,v]-\Phi[\Phi u,v]-\Phi[u,\Phi v]=-2{\rm d}\eta(u,v)\xi\in C^{\infty}(TM).

(4.1)

The data
(M;\eta,\Phi)
is called a
Sasakian manifold.
Its
Sasakian metric
is a Riemannian metric
g
on
M
defined by
g(u,v):=\eta(u)\eta(v)+{\rm d}\eta(u,\Phi v)
for
u,v\in C^{\infty}(TM).

Remark 4.2
.

If
(M;\eta,\Phi)
is a Sasakian manifold then
(0,\infty)\times M
has an almost complex structure
J
with
J(r\frac{\partial}{\partial r})=\xi,

J\xi=-r\frac{\partial}{\partial r}
and
J|_{\mathop{\rm ker}\eta}=\Phi|_{\mathop{\rm ker}\eta}.
The equation (
4.1
) implies that
J
is integrable; for the proof see for instance
[
8
, Theorem 6.5.9]
. On the other hand, using the projection
r:(0,\infty)\times M\to(0,\infty)
we can make
(0,\infty)\times M
into a Riemannian cone. This with
J
defines a Kähler cone.

Conversely, if
C
is a Kähler cone with complex structure
J
on
C^{{\rm reg}}
then
C^{{\rm lk}}
has a contact form
\eta:=-(J{\rm d}r)|_{\{1\}\times C^{{\rm lk}}}.
Denote by
\xi
its Reeb vector field, and define
\Phi\in C^{\infty}(\mathop{\rm End}TC^{{\rm lk}})
by
\Phi\xi:=0
and
\Phi|_{\mathop{\rm ker}\eta}=J|_{\mathop{\rm ker}\eta}.
The pair
(\eta,\Phi)
defines then a Sasakian structure on
C^{{\rm lk}}.
There are thus two-sided operations between Sasakian manifolds and Kähler cones, which are inverses to each other.

We recall the facts we will use about deformations of Sasakian structures.

Definition 4.3
.

Let
(M;\eta,\Phi)
be a Sasakian manifold with Reeb vector field
\xi
and Sasakian metric
g.
Let
\xi^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}(TM)
be such that
g(\xi,\xi^{\prime})>0
at every point of
M
and
\eta,\Phi
are invariant under the flow of
\xi^{\prime}.
Takahashi
[
59
]
proves then that there exists on
M
a Sasakian structure
(\eta^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime})
defined by
\eta^{\prime}:=(\xi^{\prime}\cdot\eta)^{-1}\eta
and
\Phi^{\prime}:=\Phi\circ({\rm id}-\xi^{\prime}\otimes\eta^{\prime}).

Suppose now that
M
is compact so that we can define the minimum
\alpha>0
of
g(\xi,\xi^{\prime}):M\to(0,\infty)
and the maximum
\beta>0
of the same function. Denote by
r:(0,\infty)\times M\to(0,\infty)
the projection. Extend
\xi^{\prime}
to the vector field on
(0,\infty)\times M
invariant under the flow of
r\frac{{\bar{\partial}}}{{\bar{\partial}}r};
and denote by the same
\xi^{\prime}
the extended vector field. Denote by
J,J^{\prime}
the complex structures on
(0,\infty)\times M
corresponding respectively to the Sasakian structures
(\eta,\Phi),(\eta^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime}).
Define then a diffeomorphism
F:(0,\infty)\times M\to(0,\infty)\times M
to be the identity upon
\{1\}\times M
and equivariant under the flows of
-J\xi^{\prime}
on the domain and of
-J^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}=r\frac{{\bar{\partial}}}{{\bar{\partial}}r}
on the co-domain. This is possible because
r^{\beta}\leqslant\penalty F^{*}r\leqslant\penalty r^{\alpha}
wherever
r\leqslant\penalty 1
and
r^{\alpha}\leqslant\penalty F^{*}r\leqslant\penalty r^{\beta}
wherever
r\geqslant 1;
for the proof see Conlon and Hein
[
15
, Proposition II.2]
. They prove also that
F:(0,\infty)\times M\to(0,\infty)\times M
is a bi-holomorphism with respect to
J,J^{\prime};
that is,
F_{*}J=J^{\prime}.

Example 4.4
.

Fix
m\in\{1,2,3,\dots\}
and denote by
z_{1},\dots,z_{m}:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}\to{{\mathbb{C}}}
the co-ordinate functions. The unit sphere
S^{2m-1}\subset{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}
is then defined by the equation
|z_{1}|^{2}+\dots+|z_{m}|^{2}=1.

The Kähler cone metric
\sum_{a=1}^{m}{\rm d}z_{a}\otimes{\rm d}\bar{z}_{a}
on
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}
induces on
S^{2m-1}
a Sasakian structure
(\eta,\Phi)
with
\eta=\frac{i}{2}\sum_{a=1}^{m}(z_{a}{\rm d}\bar{z}_{a}-\bar{z}_{a}{\rm d}z_{a}).
Its Reeb vector field may be written as
\xi:=i\sum_{a=1}^{m}(z_{a}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{a}}-\bar{z}_{a}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}_{a}}).
For
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m}>0
define on
S^{2m-1}
a vector field
\xi^{\prime}:=i\sum_{a=1}^{m}\lambda_{a}(z_{a}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{a}}-\bar{z}_{a}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}_{a}}).
Then
g(\xi,\xi^{\prime}):=\sum_{a=1}^{m}\lambda_{a}|z_{a}|^{2}>0
at every point of
S^{2m-1},
with
\alpha=\min\{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m}\}
and
\beta=\max\{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m}\}
in the notation of Definition
4.3
. The flow of
\xi^{\prime}
may be written as
(t;z_{1},\dots,z_{m})\mapsto(e^{i\lambda_{1}t}z_{1},\dots,e^{i\lambda_{m}t}z_{m})
for
t\in{{\mathbb{R}}}
and
(z_{1},\dots,z_{m})\in{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m},
which is a holomorphic isometry of
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}
and so leaves invariant the Sasakian structure
(\eta,\Phi).
We can therefore use Definition
4.3
and define on
S^{2m-1}
the Sasakian structure
(\eta^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime})
corresponding to
\xi^{\prime}.
Recall also from Definition
4.3
that there is a bi-holomorphism
F:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}\setminus\{0\}\to(0,\infty)\times S^{2m-1}
where
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}\setminus\{0\}
is given the ordinary complex structure
J
and
(0,\infty)\times S^{2m-1}
the deformed complex structure
J^{\prime}
corresponding to
(\eta^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime}).
Define
r:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}\setminus\{0\}\to(0,\infty)
by
r^{2}:=|z_{1}|^{2}+\dots+|z_{m}|^{2}
and define
r_{\lambda}:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}\setminus\{0\}\to(0,\infty)
by
r_{\lambda}:=F^{*}r.
Then
r^{\beta}\leqslant\penalty r_{\lambda}\leqslant\penalty r^{\alpha}
wherever
r\leqslant\penalty 1.
The Kähler form
-\frac{1}{4}{\rm d}(J^{\prime}{\rm d}r^{2})
on
(0,\infty)\times S^{2m-1}
is pulled back by
F
to the Kähler form
-\frac{1}{4}{\rm d}(J{\rm d}r_{\lambda}^{2})=\frac{1}{4}{\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}r_{\lambda}^{2}
on
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}\setminus\{0\},
where
{\rm d}^{c}
is defined with respect to
J.

Since
\xi^{\prime}=i\sum_{a=1}^{m}\lambda_{a}(z_{a}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{a}}-\bar{z}_{a}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}_{a}})
it follows that this vector field extends smoothly to
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}.
The two vector fields
-J\xi^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}
generate then the holomorphic
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-action
{{\mathbb{C}}}\times{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}\to{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}
which maps
(s;z_{1},\dots,z_{m})
to
(e^{\lambda_{1}s}z_{1},\dots,e^{\lambda_{m}s}z_{m}).
Suppose now that
X\subseteq{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}
is a closed normal complex subspace with isolated singularity at
0\in{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}
and invariant under this
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-action. We show then that
X\cap S^{2m-1}\subset X\setminus\{0\}
is a compact submanifold. Restricting the
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-action to
{{\mathbb{R}}}
and differentiating this at
0\in{{\mathbb{R}}}
we get a vector field
-J\xi_{\lambda}=\sum_{a=1}^{m}\lambda_{a}(z_{a}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{a}}+\bar{z}_{a}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}_{a}})
tangent to
X\setminus\{0\}.
Define
f:X\setminus\{0\}\to(0,\infty)
by restricting to
X\setminus\{0\}\subset{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}\setminus\{0\}
the
C^{\infty}
function
|z_{1}|^{2}+\dots+|z_{m}|^{2}:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}\setminus\{0\}\to(0,\infty).
Then
{\rm d}f(\xi_{\lambda})=\sum_{a=1}^{m}\lambda_{a}|z_{a}|^{2}\neq 0.
So
f
is a submersion and
f^{-1}(1)=X\cap S^{2m-1}
a submanifold. Recall from the definition of
F
that the image of
X\setminus\{0\}
under
F:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}\setminus\{0\}\to(0,\infty)\times S^{2m-1}
is
(0,\infty)\times(X\cap S^{2m-1}).
Since
F
is holomorphic with respect to
J,J^{\prime}
it follows moreover that
(0,\infty)\times(X\cap S^{2m-1})
is a complex submanifold of
((0,\infty)\times S^{2m-1},J^{\prime}).
The Kähler cone structure of
(0,\infty)\times S^{2m-1}
induces therefore a Kähler cone structure of
(0,\infty)\times(X\cap S^{2m-1}).
Pulling back this by
F
we get a Kähler cone structure of
X\setminus\{0\}.
As is clear from definition its radius function
X\setminus\{0\}\to(0,\infty)
is induced from
r_{\lambda}:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}\setminus\{0\}\to(0,\infty)
and its Kähler form from
\frac{1}{4}{\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}r_{\lambda}^{2}.

We now state and prove the key lemma. Recall that for
(Y,J)
a complex manifold a
C^{\infty}
function
f:Y\to{{\mathbb{R}}}
is
strictly plurisubharmonic
if for every
v\in C^{\infty}(TY)
we have
{\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}f(v,Jv)>0
at every point of
Y.
The following then holds.

Lemma 4.5
.

Fix
m\in\{1,2,3,\dots\}
and
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m}\in(0,1).
Define
r_{\lambda}:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}\setminus\{0\}\to(0,\infty)
as in Example
4.4
. Let
U\subseteq{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}
be an open neighbourhood of the origin
0\in{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m},
and
p:U\to{{\mathbb{R}}}
a strictly plurisubharmonic
C^{\infty}
function with
p(0)=0
and
\nabla p(0)=0\in T^{*}_{0}{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}.
Then there exist
\epsilon>0
and a strictly plurisubharmonic
C^{\infty}
function
q:U\setminus\{0\}\to{{\mathbb{R}}}
which outside some punctured neighbourhood of
0\in U
agrees with
p
and on some smaller punctured neighbourhood of
0\in U
agrees with
\epsilon r_{\lambda}^{2}.

Proof.

We set
q:=p+\epsilon\phi r_{\lambda}^{2}-\psi(\frac{r^{2}}{\delta^{2}})p
where
\epsilon,\delta
are positive constants;
\phi
a compactly supported
C^{\infty}
function
U\to[0,1]
which is near
0\in U
identically equal to one; and
\psi
a
C^{\infty}
function
[0,\infty)\to[0,1]
supported on
[0,1]\subset[0,\infty)
and which is near
0\in[0,\infty)
identically equal to one. Let
\epsilon
be independent of
\delta
and so small that
p+\epsilon\phi r_{\lambda}^{2}:U\setminus\{0\}\to{{\mathbb{R}}}
is strictly plurisubharmonic. This is possible because the derivatives of
\phi
are supported on a compact set and may therefore by controlled by making
\epsilon
small enough. We prove that
q:U\setminus\{0\}\to{{\mathbb{R}}}
with
\delta
small enough is strictly plurisubharmonic.

We show first that there exists
M>0
independent of
\delta
and so large that
{\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}[\psi(\frac{r^{2}}{\delta^{2}})p]\leqslant\penalty M{\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}r^{2}
at every point of the support of
\phi.
Simple computation shows that at every point of
U
we have

\begin{split}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}\Bigl[\psi\Bigl(\frac{r^{2}}{\delta^{2}}\Bigr)p\Bigr]=\frac{p}{\delta^{4}}\psi^{\prime\prime}\Bigl(\frac{r^{2}}{\delta^{2}}\Bigr){\rm d}r^{2}\wedge{\rm d}^{c}r^{2}+\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}\psi^{\prime}\Bigl(\frac{r^{2}}{\delta^{2}}\Bigr)({\rm d}p\wedge{\rm d}^{c}r^{2}+{\rm d}^{c}p\wedge{\rm d}r^{2})\\
+\frac{p}{\delta^{2}}\psi^{\prime}\Bigl(\frac{r^{2}}{\delta^{2}}\Bigr){\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}r^{2}+\psi\Bigl(\frac{r^{2}}{\delta^{2}}\Bigr){\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}p.\end{split}

(4.2)

We estimate each term on the right-hand side. Since
p(0)=\nabla p(0)=0
it follows that there exists
M_{0}>0
independent of
\delta
and so large that at every point of the support of
\phi
we have

|p|\leqslant\penalty M_{0}r^{2},\,\,\,|{\rm d}p|\leqslant\penalty M_{0}r,\,\,\,{\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}p\leqslant\penalty M_{0}{\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}r^{2}

(4.3)

where
|{\rm d}p|
is the pointwise
\ell^{2}
norm with respect to the flat metric
\sum_{a=1}^{m}{\rm d}z_{a}\otimes{\rm d}\bar{z}_{a},
and the last inequality defined as follows: for
A,B
two real
(1,1)
forms on a complex manifold
(Y,J)
we write
A\leqslant\penalty B
if
A(v.Jv)\leqslant\penalty B(v,Jv)
for every
v\in C^{\infty}(TY).
Since
S^{2m-1}
is compact,
{\rm d}r^{2}\wedge{\rm d}^{c}r^{2}
a real
(1,1)
form on
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}
and
{\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}r^{2}
a positive definite real
(1,1)
form on
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}
it follows that there exists
M_{1}>0
independent of
\delta
and so large that
{\rm d}r^{2}\wedge{\rm d}^{c}r^{2}\leqslant\penalty M_{1}{\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}r^{2}=M_{1}r^{2}{\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}r^{2}
at every point of
S^{2m-1}.
Since
{\rm d}r^{2}\wedge{\rm d}^{c}r^{2}
and
r^{2}{\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}r^{2}
are both homogeneous of order
4
(with respect to the flow on
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}
generated by
r\frac{\partial}{\partial r}
) it follows that the same estimate holds everywhere; that is,
{\rm d}r^{2}\wedge{\rm d}^{c}r^{2}\leqslant\penalty M_{1}r^{2}{\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}r^{2}
at every point of
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}.
This and the first estimate of (
4.3
) imply that at every point of the support of
\phi
we have

\frac{p}{\delta^{4}}\psi^{\prime\prime}\Bigl(\frac{r^{2}}{\delta^{2}}\Bigr){\rm d}r^{2}\wedge{\rm d}^{c}r^{2}\leqslant\penalty M_{0}M_{1}\frac{r^{4}}{\delta^{4}}\Bigl|\psi^{\prime\prime}\Bigl(\frac{r^{2}}{\delta^{2}}\Bigr)\Bigr|{\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}r^{2}\leqslant\penalty M_{0}M_{1}\biggl(\sup_{[0,\infty)}|\psi^{\prime\prime}|\biggr){\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}r^{2}

(4.4)

where the last inequality follows since
\psi^{\prime\prime}
is supported on
[0,1].
We estimate now the second term on the right-hand side of (
4.2
). For
A
a constant real
(1,1)
form on
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}
define
|A|_{\ell^{\infty}}:=\max_{v\in{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}}A(v,Jv)
and denote by
|A|
the
\ell^{2}
norm with respect to the flat metric
\sum_{a=1}^{m}{\rm d}z_{a}\otimes{\rm d}\bar{z}_{a}.
Then
|A|^{2}
is the sum of the squared eigenvalues of
A,
and
|A|_{\ell^{\infty}}\leqslant\penalty|A|.
So

A\leqslant\penalty\frac{i}{2}|A|_{\ell^{\infty}}\sum_{a=1}^{m}{\rm d}z_{a}\wedge{\rm d}\bar{z}_{a}=\frac{1}{4}|A|_{\ell^{\infty}}{\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}r^{2}\leqslant\penalty\frac{1}{4}|A|{\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}r^{2}.

(4.5)

We apply this to
{\rm d}p\wedge{\rm d}^{c}r^{2}+{\rm d}^{c}p\wedge{\rm d}r^{2}
at every point of
U.
Using the middle estimate of (
4.3
) we find indeed that at every point of the support of
\phi
we have

|{\rm d}p\wedge{\rm d}^{c}r^{2}+{\rm d}^{c}p\wedge{\rm d}r^{2}|\leqslant\penalty M_{0}|{\rm d}^{c}r^{2}|+M_{0}r|{\rm d}r^{2}|=2M_{0}|{\rm d}r^{2}|=4M_{0}r.

(4.6)

Hence it follows by (
4.5
) that at every point of the support of
\phi
we have

({\rm d}p\wedge{\rm d}^{c}r+{\rm d}^{c}p\wedge{\rm d}r)\leqslant\penalty M_{0}r{\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}r^{2}

(4.7)

So at every point of the support of
\phi
we have

\frac{1}{\delta}\psi^{\prime}\Bigl(\frac{r^{2}}{\delta^{2}}\Bigr)({\rm d}p\wedge{\rm d}^{c}r+{\rm d}^{c}p\wedge{\rm d}r)\leqslant\penalty M_{0}\frac{r}{\delta}\Bigl|\psi^{\prime}\Bigl(\frac{r^{2}}{\delta^{2}}\Bigr)\Bigr|{\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}r^{2}\leqslant\penalty M_{0}\biggl(\sup_{[0,\infty)}|\psi^{\prime}|\biggr){\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}r^{2}.

(4.8)

We estimate now the remaining the two terms on the right-hand side of (
4.2
). The first estimate of (
4.3
) implies that at every point of the support of
\phi
we have

\frac{p}{\delta^{2}}\psi^{\prime}\Bigl(\frac{r^{2}}{\delta^{2}}\Bigr){\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}r^{2}\leqslant\penalty M_{0}\frac{r^{2}}{\delta^{2}}\Bigl|\psi^{\prime}\Bigl(\frac{r^{2}}{\delta^{2}}\Bigr)\Bigr|{\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}r^{2}\leqslant\penalty M_{0}\biggl(\sup_{[0,\infty)}|\psi^{\prime}|\biggr){\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}r^{2}.

(4.9)

Since
\psi
has values in
[0,1]
it follows by the last estimate of (
4.3
) that

\psi\Bigl(\frac{r^{2}}{\delta^{2}}\Bigr){\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}p\leqslant\penalty M_{0}{\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}r^{2}.

(4.10)

Define now
M>0
by
M:=M_{0}M_{1}(\sup_{[0,\infty)}|\psi^{\prime\prime}|)+2M_{0}(\sup_{[0,\infty)}|\psi^{\prime}|)+M_{0}.
It follows then from (
4.4
), (
4.8
), (
4.9
) and (
4.10
) that at every point of the support of
\phi
we have

{\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}[\psi(\frac{r^{2}}{\delta^{2}})p]\leqslant\penalty M{\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}r^{2}.

We show next that there exists a punctured neighbourhood of
0\in{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}
at every point of which we have
\epsilon r_{\lambda}^{2}-Mr^{2}
is strictly plurisubharmonic. Denote by
g_{\lambda}
the deformed Sasakian metric on
S^{2m-1}
corresponding to
\xi_{\lambda}
as in Example
4.4
, and by
g
the ordinary Sakasian metric on
S^{2m-1}
(that is, the round sphere metric). For
h,h^{\prime}
two Riemannian metrics on a manifold
Y
write
h\geqslant h^{\prime}
if
h(v,v)\geqslant h^{\prime}(v,v)
for every
v\in C^{\infty}(TY).
Let
\nu>0
be so small that
g_{\lambda}\geqslant\nu g_{S^{2m-1}}
at every point of
S^{2m-1}.
Since
\log r_{\lambda}\geqslant\beta\log r
with
\beta=\max\{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m}\}\in(0,1)
it follows then that

r_{\lambda}^{2}[({\rm d}\log r_{\lambda})^{\otimes 2}+g_{\lambda}]\geqslant r^{2\beta}[\beta^{2}({\rm d}\log r)^{\otimes 2}+\nu g_{S^{2m-1}}]\geqslant\min\{\beta^{2},\nu\}r^{2(1-\beta)}\sum_{a=1}^{m}{\rm d}z_{a}\otimes{\rm d}\bar{z}_{a}

at every point of
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}\setminus\{0\}.
The corresponding
(1,1)
forms satisfy the estimate
{\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}r_{\lambda}^{2}\geqslant\min\{\beta^{2},\nu\}r^{2(1-\beta)}{\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}r^{2}
at every point of
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}\setminus\{0\}.
As
\epsilon
and
M
are independent of
\delta
we can make
\delta
so small that
\min\{\beta^{2},\nu\}\delta^{2(1-\beta)}\geqslant\epsilon^{-1}M.
It follows then that at every point of
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}\setminus\{0\}
at which
r\leqslant\penalty\delta,
we have
\epsilon{\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}r_{\lambda}^{2}\geqslant M{\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}r^{2}.

Let
\delta
be so small too that
\phi=1
at those points of
U\setminus\{0\}
at which
r\leqslant\penalty\delta.
Then
q=\epsilon r_{\lambda}^{2}
at the same points; and accordingly, since
\epsilon{\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}r_{\lambda}^{2}\geqslant M{\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}r^{2}
at these points it follows that
q
is strictly plurisubharmonic at them. On the other hand, at the points of
U\setminus\{0\}
with
r>\delta
we have
\psi(\frac{r}{\delta})=0
and know already that
q=p+\epsilon\phi r_{\lambda}^{2}
is strictly plurisubharmonic at these points. Thus
q:U\setminus\{0\}\to{{\mathbb{R}}}
is everywhere strictly plurisubharmonic. Choose finally a punctured neighbourhood of
0\in U
on which
\psi(\frac{r}{\delta})=1.
On this set we have certainly
q=\epsilon r_{\lambda}^{2},
which completes the proof.
∎

We prove a corollary of Lemma
4.5
.

Corollary 4.6
.

Fix
m\in\{1,2,3,\dots\}
and
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m}\in(0,1).
Define
r_{\lambda}:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}\setminus\{0\}\to(0,\infty)
as in Example
4.4
. Let
U\subseteq{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}
be an open neighbourhood of the origin
0\in{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m},
and
\omega
a Kähler form on
U.
Then there exist
\epsilon>0
and a Kähler form on
U\setminus\{0\}
which outside some punctured neighbourhood of
0\in U
agrees with
\omega
and on some smaller punctured neighbourhood of
0\in U
agrees with
\epsilon{\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}r_{\lambda}^{2}.

Proof.

The local
\partial{\bar{\partial}}
lemma implies that there exist an open neighbourhood
V
of
0\in U
and a smooth function
f:V\to{{\mathbb{R}}}
such that
\omega|_{V}={\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}f.
Define
p:V\to{{\mathbb{R}}}
by
p:=f-f(0)-\sum_{a=1}^{m}(\frac{\partial f}{\partial z_{a}}(0)z_{a}+\frac{\partial f}{\partial\bar{z}_{a}}(0)\bar{z}_{a}).
Since
{\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}z_{a}={\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}\bar{z}_{a}=0
for
a=1,\dots,m
it follows then that
\omega|_{V}={\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}p,
to which we can apply Lemma
4.5
with
V
in place of
U.
Let
q:V\setminus\{0\}\to{{\mathbb{R}}}
be the result of this; then
{\rm d}{\rm d}^{c}q
is a Kähler form we want.
∎

We recall now the definition of Kähler complex spaces.

Definition 4.7
.

For a complex space
X
we say that a
C^{\infty}
function
\phi:X\to{{\mathbb{R}}}
is
strictly plurisubharmonic
if every point of
X
has an open neighbourhood
U
embedded in some open set
Y\subseteq{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n}
for which there exists a strictly plurisubharmonic function
\psi:Y\to{{\mathbb{R}}}
with
\psi|_{U}=\phi|_{U}.
We call
X
a
Kähler space
if there exist an open cover
U\cup V\cup\dots=X
and a corresponding family
(\phi_{U}:U\to{{\mathbb{R}}})_{U}
of
C^{\infty}
strictly plurisubharmonic functions such that for each
U
we have
\omega|_{U}=i\partial{\bar{\partial}}\phi_{U}.
We call
\phi_{U},\phi_{V},\dots

Kähler potentials
of
X.

Remark 4.8
.

When we speak simply of a Kähler space
X
we do not make any particular choice of the family
\phi_{U},\phi_{V},\dots
of Kähler potentials. This convention is compatible with the statement of our main results, Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
, which themselves have nothing to do with the choice of Kähler potentials.

We make the definition we will use of compact Kähler conifolds.

Definition 4.9
.

A
compact Kähler conifold
is a compact normal Kähler space whose singularities are isolated and quasi-homogeneous. We call
X
a Kähler
n
-conifold if it has (complex) dimension
n.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler conifold. Denote by
X^{{\rm reg}}
its regular locus and by
X^{\rm sing}
its singular locus. Then there exist a Kähler metric
g
on
X^{{\rm reg}}
and a finite family
(C_{x},g_{x})_{x\in X^{\rm sing}}
of Kähler cones such that the following holds: for every
x\in X^{\rm sing}
there exists a biholomorphism
(X,x)\cong(C_{x},{\rm vx})
under which
g
and
g_{x}
agree.

Proof.

Suppose now conversely that
X
is a compact normal Kähler space whose singularities are isolated and quasi-homogeneous. Choose an open cover
U\cup V\cup\dots=X
and respective Kähler potentials
p_{U},p_{V},\dots
on
U,V,\dots
which define a Kähler form on
X.
For
U
containing a singular point
x\in X^{\rm sing},
choose
q
as in Corollary
4.6
with
x
in place of
0\in{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}
and set
q_{U}:=q.
For
U
not intersecting
X^{\rm sing},
set
q_{U}:=p_{U}.
The Kähler potential
q_{U},q_{V},\dots
define then a Kähler conifold metric on
X^{{\rm reg}}.

∎

Definition 4.11
.

In the circumstances of Lemma
4.10
we call
g
a Kähler conifold metric on
X.
More precisely, this means that there exists
(C_{x},g_{x})_{x\in X^{\rm sing}}
for which the statement about the biholomorphism
(X,x)\cong(C_{x},{\rm vx})
is true for every
x\in X^{\rm sing}.

The following lemma is perhaps of interest in itself although we shall not logically need it for the proof of Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
.

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
X
be a compact normal complex space whose singularities are isolated and which has a Kähler conifold metric. Then
X
is a Kähler space and its singularities are quasi-homogeneous.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.3
, for every
x\in X^{\rm sing}
the germ
(X,x)
is quasi-homogeneous. We show that the compact complex space
X
is a Kähler space. As
X
is normal, if
X
is one-dimensional then it is non-singular and we have nothing to prove. Suppose therefore that
X
has dimension
\geqslant 2.
We use then the following result
[
23
, Lemma 1]
:

Let
(Y,y)
be the germ of a normal complex space of dimension
\geqslant 2,
and
p:Y\setminus\{y\}\to{{\mathbb{R}}}
a strictly plurisubharmonic
C^{\infty}
function. Then there exist a neighborhood
U\subseteq Y
of
y
and a strictly plurisubharmonic
C^{\infty}
function
q:Y\to{{\mathbb{R}}}
such that
q|_{Y\setminus U}=p|_{Y\setminus U}.

(4.11)

This implies that the Kähler potentials which define the cone metrics near
X^{\rm sing}
may be modified so as to define a Kähler form on the whole
X.

∎

Definition
1.1
is now equivalent to the following definition.

Definition 4.13
.

A
compact Calabi–Yau conifold
is a compact Kähler conifold
X
whose canonical sheaf is a rank-one free
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}
module and whose singularities are rational.

Remark 4.14
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler
n
-conifold whose canonical sheaf is a rank-one free
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}
module. The following three conditions are then equivalent:
(i)

X^{\rm sing}
is rational;
(ii)

X^{\rm sing}
is canonical; and
(iii)

X^{\rm sing}
is log-terminal. This is well known and explained for instance in
[
43
, Theorem 5.22 and Corollary 5.24]
. The condition (iii) is equivalent also to the following:
(iv)
the nowhere-vanishing
(n,0)
forms on
X^{{\rm reg}}
(which are unique up to constant) are
L^{2}.
There is in fact also an older result
[
9
, Proposition 3.2]
which proves that (i) and (iv) are equivalent in the present circumstances. Note that the condition (iv) is independent of the choice of a Riemannian metric on
X^{{\rm reg}}
because an
(n,0)
form
\Omega
being
L^{2}
means
\pm i^{n}\int_{X^{{\rm reg}}}\Omega\wedge\overline{\Omega}<\infty
(
\pm
corresponding to the orientation of
X^{{\rm reg}}
).

It is known also that rational singularities are Cohen–Macaulay
[
43
, Theorem 5.10]
. By
[
4
, Corollary 3.3(a)]
the germ
(X,x)
of a Cohen–Macaulay singularity is of depth
\geqslant n;
that is,
H^{q}_{x}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})=0
for every integer
q\leqslant\penalty n-1.

We prove a fact we will use about compact Kähler conifolds. The hypotheses are in fact weaker than being compact Kähler conifolds.

Proposition 4.15
.

Let
X
be a compact normal Kähler
n
-fold whose canonical sheaf is numerically trivial and whose singularities are non-empty, isolated and log-terminal. Then
H^{1}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})=0.

Proof.

We show that no non-zero holomorphic
1
-forms exist on
X^{{\rm reg}}.
As in
[
2
]
there exists a quasi-étale cover
f:Y\to X
which is the product of a torus, irreducible Calabi–Yau varieties and irreducible symplectic varieties; moreover, none of the latter two factors has non-zero
1
-forms on the regular locus. As
X
has isolated singularities,
Y
is a trivial product; that is,
Y
is a complex torus, an irreducible Calabi–Yau variety or an irreducible symplectic variety. Consider first the latter two cases and let
X^{{\rm reg}}
have a holomorphic
1
-form
\phi.
Then
f^{*}\phi
is defined at least outside a co-dimension
2
subset of
Y,
where
f
is étale. At every non-singular point
y\in Y
the sheaf
\Omega^{1}_{Y}
is locally free and
f^{*}\phi
extends to
y.
Thus
f^{*}\phi
is well defined on
Y^{{\rm reg}}
and so, as mentioned above, vanishes.

Let
Y
be now a complex torus. By
[
24
, Lemma 7.4]
there are a complex torus
T
and a finite group action
G\curvearrowright T
which is free in co-dimension one and whose quotient space is isomorphic to
X.
Denote by
\pi:T\to X
the projection map and let
X^{{\rm reg}}
have a holomorphic
1
-form
\phi.
As in the first paragraph the pull-back
\pi^{*}\phi
extends to
T
which is now a
G
-invariant
1
-form. Since
X
has at least one singular point we get at least one point
t\in T
at which
G
has non-trivial stabilizer subgroup
H.
If
\pi^{*}\phi
is non-zero at
t
then the germ of
\pi(t)\in X
is the product of
{{\mathbb{C}}},
corresponding to
\pi^{*}\phi,
and some quotient singularity
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n-1}/H.
But this will not be an isolated singularity. So
\pi^{*}\phi=0
at the point
t;
and as
T
is a torus,
\pi^{*}\phi=0
everywhere. Thus
\phi=0.

Let
Z\to X
be a resolution of singularities morphism. Then
H^{0}(Z,\Omega^{1}_{Z})\subseteq H^{0}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{1}_{X})=0.
As
X
is Kähler so is
Z
and we have
H^{1}(Z,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{Z})\cong H^{0}(Z,\Omega^{1}_{Z})=0.
Since
X^{\rm sing}
is rational it follows that
H^{1}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})\cong H^{1}(Y,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{Y})=0.

∎

5
Harmonic Forms

We begin by defining compact Riemannian conifolds.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
X
be a topological space and
x\in X
any point. Then a
punctured neighbourhood
of
x\in X
is the set
U\setminus\{x\}
where
U
is some (ordinary) neighbourhood of
x\in X.

A
compact Riemannian conifold
consists of a compact metric space
X,
a Riemannian manifold
(X^{{\rm reg}},g)
of dimension
l,
and a finite family
(C_{x},g_{x})_{x\in X^{\rm sing}}
of Riemannian cones such that
X=X^{{\rm reg}}\sqcup X^{\rm sing}
as sets; and for every
x\in X^{\rm sing}
there exist a punctured neighbourhood of
{\rm vx}\in C_{x}^{{\rm reg}},
a punctured neighbourhood of
x\in X^{{\rm reg}},
and a diffeomorphism between these two under which the two Riemannian metrics
g,g_{x}
agree with each other.

We call
X
a Riemannian
l
-conifold if
X
has real dimension
l.

Remark 5.2
.

Although this definition will do for our purpose, the condition that
g
and
g_{x}
should agree locally is stronger than the more standard definition in
[
12
, Definition 4.6]
,
[
31
, Definition 2.2]
,
[
33
, Definition 2.1]
,
[
34
, Definition 3.24]
and others. In the latter definition we require only that
g
should approach with order
\epsilon>0
at
x
the other metric
g_{x};
that is, for
k=0,1,2,\dots
we have
|\nabla^{k}(g-g_{x})|=O(r^{\epsilon})
where
r
is the radius function on
C_{x}^{{\rm reg}}
and
\nabla,|\,\,|
are computed pointwise with respect to the cone metric
g_{x}.

We define now weighted Sobolev spaces.

Definition 5.3
.

Let
X
be a compact Riemannian
l
-conifold. Choose a smooth function
\rho:X^{{\rm reg}}\to(0,\infty)
which near every
x\in X^{\rm sing}
agrees with the radius function on
C_{x}^{{\rm reg}}.
Define for
k=0,1,2,\dots
and
\alpha\in{{\mathbb{R}}}
the weighted Sobolev space
H^{k}(\Lambda^{p}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
to be the set of
\alpha\in L^{2}(\Lambda^{p}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
for which the weak derivatives
\phi,\dots,\nabla^{k}\phi
exist with

\|\phi\|_{H^{k}_{\alpha}}^{2}:=\int_{X^{{\rm reg}}}\sum_{j=0}^{k}\rho^{-l}|\rho^{-j-\alpha}\nabla^{j}\phi|^{2}{\rm d}\mu<\infty

(5.1)

where
|\,\,|,\nabla
and
{\rm d}\mu
are computed with respect to the Riemannian metric of
X^{{\rm reg}}.
For
k=0
put
L^{2}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{p}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}):=H^{0}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{p}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).
Put also
L^{2}(\Lambda^{p}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}):=L^{2}_{-l/2}(\Lambda^{p}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}),
not
L^{2}_{0}(\Lambda^{p}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}),
because for
\phi\in L^{2}(\Lambda^{p}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
we have
\|\phi\|_{L^{2}}:=\|\phi\|_{L^{2}_{-l/2}}=\int_{X^{{\rm reg}}}|\phi|^{2}{\rm d}\mu;
that is,
L^{2}(\Lambda^{p}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
may be regarded as the unweighted
L^{2}
space. We say therefore that a
p
-form
\phi
on
X^{{\rm reg}}
is (plainly)
L^{2}
if
\phi\in L^{2}(\Lambda^{p}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})=L^{2}_{-l/2}(\Lambda^{p}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).
For
\phi,\psi\in L^{2}(\Lambda^{p}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
define the inner product
\phi\cdot\psi:=\int_{X^{{\rm reg}}}(\phi,\psi){\rm d}\mu
where
(\phi,\psi)
is defined pointwise on
X^{{\rm reg}},
using its Riemannian metric.

Suppose now that
X
is a Kähler conifold. Fix
p,q\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
and recall that there is a subsheaf
\Lambda^{pq}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}\subseteq\Lambda^{p+q}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}.
For
k=0,1,2,\dots
and for
\alpha\in{{\mathbb{R}}}
define the weighted Sobolev space
H^{k}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{pq}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}):=L^{2}(\Lambda^{pq}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\cap H^{k}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{p+q}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).

We can also extend the definitions to a function
\alpha:X^{\rm sing}\to{{\mathbb{R}}},
allowing
\phi
to have different orders at different singular points. The modification will be straightforward, which we leave to the reader as an exercise.

We state integration by parts formulae.

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler
n
-conifold. Fix
p,q\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
and
\alpha,\beta:{{\mathbb{R}}}\to X^{\rm sing}
with
\alpha(x)+\beta(x)>1-2n
for every
x\in X^{\rm sing}.
Then for
\phi\in H^{1}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{pq}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
and
\psi\in H^{1}_{\beta}(\Lambda^{p\,q+1}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
we have
{\rm d}\phi\cdot\psi=\phi\cdot{\rm d}^{*}\psi
and
{\bar{\partial}}\phi\cdot\psi=\phi\cdot{\bar{\partial}}^{*}\psi
where
{\rm d}^{*}
and
{\bar{\partial}}^{*}
are computed with respect to the Kähler metric of
X^{{\rm reg}}.

Proof.

Note that both sides of the formula are well defined by the hypotheses. These will be equal by definition for
\phi,\psi
with compact support; and such
\phi,\psi
are dense in the weighted Sobolev spaces. The approximation argument implies therefore that the equality holds for every
\phi,\psi.

∎

We define
(p,q)
form Laplacians and their exceptional values.

Definition 5.5
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler conifold with cone
C_{x}
at each
x\in X^{\rm sing}.
Fix
p,q\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}.
Using the Kähler metric of
X^{{\rm reg}}
define the
(p,q)
form Laplacian
\Delta:\Gamma(\Lambda^{pq}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to\Gamma(\Lambda^{pq}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
by
\Delta;={\rm d}{\rm d}^{*}+{\rm d}^{*}{\rm d}=2{\bar{\partial}}{\bar{\partial}}^{*}+2{\bar{\partial}}^{*}{\bar{\partial}}.
We call
\alpha\in{{\mathbb{C}}}
an
exceptional value
of
\Delta
at
x\in X^{\rm sing}
if there exist non-zero order-
\alpha
homogeneous harmonic
(p,q)
forms on
C_{x}^{{\rm reg}}.

Proposition
2.4
implies

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler conifold and
h
a Hermitian conifold metric on
X.
Fix
p,q\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}.
Then the set of exceptional values of the
(p,q)
form Laplacian
\Delta
is a discrete subset of
{{\mathbb{R}}}.

Applying Proposition
2.3
to
(p,q)
forms we get

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler conifold,
p,q
integers and
\Delta:\Gamma(\Lambda^{pq}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to\Gamma(\Lambda^{pq}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
the
(p,q)
form Laplacian. Then
\Delta
defines for
k\in\{2,3,4,\dots\}
and
\alpha:X^{\rm sing}\to{{\mathbb{R}}}
a bounded linear operator
\Delta:H^{k}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{pq}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to H^{k-2}_{\alpha-2}(\Lambda^{pq}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).
∎

From
[
42
, Theorem 6.2]
we get also

Proposition 5.8
.

In the circumstances of Proposition
5.7
the operator
\Delta:H^{k}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{pq}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to H^{k-2}_{\alpha-2}(\Lambda^{pq}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
is Fredholm if and only if none of
\{\alpha(x)\}_{x\in X^{\rm sing}}
is an exceptional value. ∎

We define the spaces of harmonic
(p,q)
forms.

Definition 5.9
.

In the circumstances of Proposition
5.7
denote by
\mathop{\rm ker}\Delta^{pq}_{\alpha}
the kernel of the operator
\Delta:H^{k}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{pq}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to H^{k-2}_{\alpha-2}(\Lambda^{pq}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).
This is independent of
k
because it consists of harmonic forms, which have the elliptic regularity property.

Remark 5.10
.

Note also that even for
k=0,1
and for
\phi\in H^{k}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{pq}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
we can define the equation
\Delta\phi=0
by means of distributions, and that
\mathop{\rm ker}\Delta^{pq}_{\alpha}
agrees with the set of its solutions.

From
[
42
, Lemma 7.3 and §8]
we get

Proposition 5.11
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler conifold,
p,q
integers and
\Delta:\Gamma(\Lambda^{pq}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to\Gamma(\Lambda^{pq}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
the
(p,q)
form Laplacian. Let a compact interval
[\alpha,\beta]\subset{{\mathbb{R}}}
contain no exceptional values of
\Delta.
Then
\mathop{\rm ker}\Delta^{pq}_{\alpha}=\mathop{\rm ker}\Delta^{pq}_{\beta}.
∎

We recall another standard result about elliptic operators between weighted Sobolev spaces.

Proposition 5.12
.

Let
X,p,q,\Delta
be as in Proposition
5.11
. Let
k\geqslant 2
be an integer and let
\alpha:X^{\rm sing}\to{{\mathbb{R}}}
have no exceptional value of
\Delta.
Then each
\phi\in H^{k-2}_{\alpha-2}(\Lambda^{pq}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
lies in the image of the Fredholm operator
\Delta:H^{k}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{pq}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to H^{k-2}_{\alpha-2}(\Lambda^{pq}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
if and only if
\phi\cdot\psi=0
for every
\psi\in\mathop{\rm ker}\Delta^{pq}_{2-2n-\alpha}.

Proof.

This is proved in
[
33
, Theorem 2.14]
for
p=q=0
and the proof extends immediately to every
p,q.

∎

We prove a few fact we shall need about
L^{2}
harmonic
n
-forms on
2n
-conifolds. Here
L^{2}
is the unweighted
L^{2},
equivalent to the weighted
L^{2}_{-n}.

Proposition 5.13
.

Let
X
be a compact Riemannian
2n
-conifold and
\phi
an
L^{2}
harmonic
n
-form on
X^{{\rm reg}}.
Then
\phi
is in fact of order
>-n;
that is,
\phi\in L^{2}_{\epsilon-n}(\Lambda^{n}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
for some
\epsilon>0.

Proof.

Fix
x\in X^{\rm sing}
and denote by
\phi_{x}
the leading term of
\phi
expanded as in Theorem
2.10
. Write
\phi_{x}=:r^{-n}({\rm d}\log\rho\wedge\phi_{x}^{\prime}+\phi_{x}^{\prime\prime})
where
r
is the radius function on
C_{x}^{{\rm reg}},

\phi_{x}^{\prime}
a homogeneous
n-1
form on
C_{x}^{{\rm lk}},
and
\phi_{x}^{\prime\prime}
a homogeneous
n
-form on
C_{x}^{{\rm lk}}.
Since
\phi
is
L^{2}
it follows that
\phi_{x}
is
L^{2}
over
(0,\delta)\times C_{x}^{{\rm lk}}
for some
\delta>0;
that is,

\phi_{x}\cdot\phi_{x}=\int_{-\infty}^{\log\delta}\int_{C^{{\rm lk}}}(|\phi_{x}^{\prime}|^{2}+|\phi_{x}^{\prime\prime}|^{2}){\rm d}\mu\,{\rm d}\log r<\infty

(5.2)

where
|\,\,|,{\rm d}\mu
are computed on
C_{x}^{{\rm lk}}.
So the integral
\int_{C^{{\rm lk}}}(|\phi_{x}^{\prime}|^{2}+|\phi_{x}^{\prime\prime}|^{2}){\rm d}\mu
is independent of
r,
which with (
5.2
) implies that
\int_{C^{{\rm lk}}}(|\phi_{x}^{\prime}|^{2}+|\phi_{x}^{\prime\prime}|^{2}){\rm d}\mu=0.
Thus
\phi_{x}^{\prime}=\phi_{x}^{\prime\prime}=0
and
\phi_{x}=0.

∎

Remark 5.14
.

We can in fact prove this without using Theorem
2.10
. We shall then need to replace
\phi_{x}
by
\phi_{x}+(\log r)\psi_{x}
where
\psi_{x}
is another homogeneous
n
-form of order
-n.
But
(\log r)\psi_{x}
diverges even faster, which must vanish again by the
L^{2}
condition.

We prove an integration by part formula for harmonic forms on compact Riemannian conifolds.

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
X
be a compact Riemannian
2n
-conifold and
p\leqslant\penalty n-1
an integer. Let
\epsilon>0
be so small that
p-n
is the smallest exceptional value
>-p-\epsilon
of
\Delta:\Gamma(\Lambda^{p}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to\Gamma(\Lambda^{p}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).
Then for every
\phi\in L^{2}_{-p-\epsilon}(\Lambda^{p}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
with
\Delta\phi=0
we have
{\rm d}\phi={\rm d}^{*}\phi=0.

Proof.

For each
x\in X^{\rm sing}
identify punctured neighbourhoods of
x\in X
and
{\rm vx}\in C_{x}.
By Theorem
2.10
there exists on
C_{x}
an order
-p
homogeneous harmonic
p
-form
\psi_{x}
such that
\phi-\psi_{x}
has order
-p+\delta
for some
\delta\in(0,\epsilon).
By Corollary
2.11
we have
{\rm d}\psi_{x}={\rm d}^{*}\psi_{x}=0.
Accordingly,
{\rm d}\phi
and
{\rm d}^{*}\phi
are of order
-p-1+\delta;
and in particular,
\Delta\phi
is of order
-p-2+\delta.
On the other hand, it follows from the definition of
\epsilon
that
\phi
has order
-p-\frac{\delta}{2}.
Recalling
p\leqslant\penalty n-1
and using Proposition
5.4
we see that the integration by parts formula
{\rm d}\phi\cdot{\rm d}\phi+{\rm d}^{*}\phi\cdot{\rm d}^{*}\phi=\phi\cdot\Delta\phi
is valid. But
\Delta\phi=0
and the left-hand side is non-negative. So
{\rm d}\phi={\rm d}^{*}\phi=0.

∎

We recall the basic facts we will use about tangent sheaves of normal complex spaces.

Definition 5.16
.

If
X
is a normal complex space then
\Theta_{X}
denotes its
tangent sheaf,
that is, the
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}
module dual to
\Omega^{1}_{X}.

Remark 5.17
.

The sheaf
\Theta_{X}
is as is well known a reflexive sheaf, which has the following properties. Denote by
\iota:X^{{\rm reg}}\to X
the embedding of the regular locus. The natural
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}
module homomorphism
\Theta_{X}\to\iota_{*}\Theta_{X^{{\rm reg}}}
is then an isomorphism. Moreover,
H^{1}_{X^{\rm sing}}(X,\Theta_{X^{{\rm reg}}})=0.

We finally state the theorem we will prove in the next section.

Definition 5.18
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler conifold and give it a Kähler conifold metric. Take
p,q\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
and
\alpha:X^{\rm sing}\to{{\mathbb{R}}}.
Then
\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}+{\bar{\partial}}^{*})^{pq}_{\alpha}
consists of
(p,q)
forms
\phi\in L^{2}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{pq}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
with
{\bar{\partial}}\phi={\bar{\partial}}^{*}\phi=0.

Theorem 5.19
.

Let
X
be a compact Calabi–Yau
n
-conifold with
H^{2}_{X^{\rm sing}}(X,\Omega^{n-2}_{X})=0
and give
X
a Kähler conifold metric. Then there exists a
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-vector space isomorphism

H^{1}(X,\Theta_{X})\cong\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}+{\bar{\partial}}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}.

6
Proof of Theorem
5.19

We make a definition we will use to prove Theorem
5.19
.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
Y
be a topological space and
{{\mathcal{F}}}
a sheaf on it. For
q\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
denote by
{}_{c}H^{q}(Y,{{\mathcal{F}}})
the image of the natural map
H_{c}^{q}(Y,{{\mathcal{F}}})\to H^{q}(Y,{{\mathcal{F}}})
from the compact support cohomology group to the plain cohomology group. If
Y
is embedded in another space
X,
and
{{\mathcal{F}}}
induced from a sheaf
{{\mathcal{E}}}
on
X
then we write
{}_{c}H^{q}(Y,{{\mathcal{E}}}):={}_{c}H^{q}(Y,{{\mathcal{F}}}).

We prove a lemma about Definition
6.1
.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
X
be a topological space,
{{\mathcal{E}}}
a
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-vector space sheaf on
X,
and
q
an integer. Let
Y\subseteq X
be a finite subset which has a fundamental system
\{U\}
of neighbourhoods with
H^{q}(U,{{\mathcal{E}}})=H^{q+1}(U,{{\mathcal{E}}})=0
for each
U.
The
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-vector space
{}_{c}H^{q}(X\setminus Y,{{\mathcal{E}}})
is then isomorphic to the image of the natural map
H^{q}(X,{{\mathcal{E}}})\to H^{q}(X\setminus Y,{{\mathcal{E}}}).

Proof.

By hypothesis, for each
U
the natural map
H^{q}(U\setminus Y,{{\mathcal{E}}})\to H^{q+1}_{Y}(U,{{\mathcal{E}}})=H^{q+1}_{Y}(X,{{\mathcal{E}}})
is an isomorphism. On the other hand, there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{q}(X,{{\mathcal{E}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{q}(X\setminus Y,{{\mathcal{E}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{q+1}_{Y}(X,{{\mathcal{E}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{c}^{q}(X\setminus Y,{{\mathcal{E}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{q}(X\setminus Y,{{\mathcal{E}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{q}(U\setminus Y,{{\mathcal{E}}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

(6.1)

with exact rows. The vector space
{}_{c}H^{q}(X\setminus Y,{{\mathcal{E}}}),
which is by definition the image of
\beta
above, is now equal to the image of
\alpha
in the same diagram.
∎

We prove a corollary of Lemma
6.2
.

Corollary 6.3
.

Let
X
be a compact normal complex space whose singularities are isolated. Then there exists a
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-vector space isomorphism
{}_{c}H^{1}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Theta_{X})\cong H^{1}(X,\Theta_{X}).

Proof.

As the Stein neighbourhoods of
X^{\rm sing}
are a fundamental system and
{{\mathcal{E}}}
a coherent sheaf on
X,
we can apply Lemma
6.2
to
{{\mathcal{E}}}=\Theta_{X},

q=1
and
Y=X^{\rm sing};
that is,
{}_{c}H^{1}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Theta_{X})
agrees with the image of the natural map
H^{1}(X,\Theta_{X})\to H^{1}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Theta_{X}).
But
\Theta_{X}
is a reflexive sheaf and
H^{1}_{X^{\rm sing}}(X,\Theta_{X})=0.
The map
H^{1}(X,\Theta_{X})\to H^{1}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Theta_{X})
is therefore injective and hence we get the isomorphism we want.
∎

We make another definition we will use to prove Theorem
5.19
.

Definition 6.4
.

Let
X
be a topological space and
{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\bullet}=({{\mathcal{E}}}^{0}\to{{\mathcal{E}}}^{1}\to\cdots)
a chain complex of
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-vector space sheaves on
X.
Define for
p=0,1,2,\dots
a decreasing filtration
F^{p}{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\bullet}\subseteq\dots\subseteq F^{0}{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\bullet}={{\mathcal{E}}}^{\bullet}
as follows: for
q<p
the degree-
q
part of
F^{p}{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\bullet}
vanishes and for
q\geqslant p
its degree part is equal to
{{\mathcal{E}}}^{p}.
The inclusion
F^{p}{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\bullet}\subseteq{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\bullet}
induces for
q\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
a map
