H^{q}(X,F^{p}{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\bullet})\to H^{q}(X,{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\bullet})
between the hypercohomology groups, whose image we denote by
F^{p}H^{q}(X,{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\bullet}).
For
p\geqslant 1
the inclusion
F^{p}{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\bullet}\subseteq F^{p-1}{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\bullet}
induces an inclusion
F^{p}H^{q}(X,{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\bullet})\subseteq F^{p-1}H^{q}(X,{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\bullet})
which defines thus a decreasing filtration of
H^{q}(X,{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\bullet}).
Denote by
\gr^{p}H^{q}(X,{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\bullet})
the quotient vector space
F^{p}H^{q}(X,{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\bullet})/F^{p+1}H^{q}(X,{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\bullet}).

Suppose now that
X
is a complex space and denote by
\iota:X^{{\rm reg}}\to X
the embedding of the regular locus. From the de Rham complex
\Omega^{\bullet}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}
we get for
q\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
a filtered vector space
H^{q}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{\bullet}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).
The quasi-isomorphism
\underline{{{\mathbb{C}}}}\to\Omega^{\bullet}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}
induces a
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-vector space isomorphism
H^{q}(X^{{\rm reg}},{{\mathbb{C}}})\cong H^{q}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{\bullet}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).

We give the subspace
{}_{c}H^{q}(X^{{\rm reg}},{{\mathbb{C}}})\subseteq H^{q}(X^{{\rm reg}},{{\mathbb{C}}})
the filtration induced from that of
H^{q}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{\bullet}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).
For
p\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
the quotient space
\gr^{p}{}_{c}H^{q}(X^{{\rm reg}},{{\mathbb{C}}})
is then well defined.

We recall a result we will use shortly about harmonic
n
-forms on compact Riemannian conifolds.

Theorem 6.5

((0.16) of
[
41
]
)
.

Let
X
be a compact Riemannian
2n
-conifold and denote by
\mathop{\rm ker}({\rm d}+{\rm d}^{*})^{n}_{-n}
the
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-vector space of
L^{2}
closed and co-closed
n
-forms on
X^{{\rm reg}}.
The natural projection
\mathop{\rm ker}({\rm d}+{\rm d}^{*})^{n}_{-n}\to H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},{{\mathbb{C}}})
which assigns to every
\phi\in\mathop{\rm ker}({\rm d}+{\rm d}^{*})^{n}_{-n}
its de Rham class
[\phi]\in H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},{{\mathbb{C}}})
is then an isomorphism onto
{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},{{\mathbb{C}}}).
∎

We come now the first step to the proof of Theorem
5.19
.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
n\geqslant 3
be an integer and
X
a compact Calabi–Yau
n
-conifold with
X^{\rm sing}
non-empty. Give
X
a Kähler conifold metric. Let
\phi
be an
(n,1)
form of order
>-n-1
on
X^{{\rm reg}}
with
{\bar{\partial}}\phi={\bar{\partial}}^{*}\phi=0.
Then there exists on
X^{{\rm reg}}
an
(n,0)
form
\chi
of order
>-n
and with
{\bar{\partial}}\chi=\phi.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.15
we have
H^{1}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})=0.
By hypothesis we have
H^{1}_{X^{\rm sing}}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})=H^{2}_{x}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})=0.
So
H^{1}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{n}_{X})\cong H^{1}(X^{{\rm reg}},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})\cong H^{1}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})=0.
In particular,
\phi
is
{\bar{\partial}}
exact and we can write
\phi={\bar{\partial}}\chi
with
\chi
an
(n,0)
form on
X^{{\rm reg}}.
Then
{\bar{\partial}}^{*}{\bar{\partial}}\chi=0;
that is,
\chi
is a harmonic
(n,0)
form. Write it as a countable sum of homogeneous harmonic forms near each
x\in X^{\rm sing}.
Let
\phi
have order
\epsilon-1-n
with
\epsilon>0.
Since
\phi={\bar{\partial}}\chi
it follows then that each homogeneous term
\chi^{\prime}
of order
<\epsilon-n
of
\chi
is holomorphic. Recall now from Remark
4.14
that there exists on
X^{{\rm reg}}
an
L^{2}
holomorphic
(n,0)
form
\Omega.
We can then write
\chi^{\prime}=f\Omega
with
f
some holomorphic function on a punctured neighbourhood of
x\in X.
As
X
is normal
f
extends holomorphically to
x;
and in particular, it is bounded. But
\Omega
is
L^{2};
and by Proposition
5.13
, making
\epsilon
smaller if we need, we can suppose that
\Omega
is of order
\epsilon-n.
This implies that
f=0
and that
\chi^{\prime}=0.
That is, every homogeneous holomorphic term of order
<\epsilon-n
must vanish, which completes the proof.
∎

We make a more careful study of
\gr^{n-1}{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{\bullet}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).

Theorem 6.7
.

Let
n\geqslant 3
be an integer and
X
a compact Calabi–Yau
n
-conifold with
X^{\rm sing}
non-empty. Give
X
a Kähler conifold metric. Denote by
\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}+{\bar{\partial}}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}
the space of
L^{2}
harmonic
(n-1,1)
forms on
X^{{\rm reg}}.
Then there exists an injective
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-linear map
\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}+{\bar{\partial}}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}\to\gr^{n-1}{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},{{\mathbb{C}}}).

Proof.

We show first that for every
\phi\in\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}+{\bar{\partial}}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}
there exists on
X^{{\rm reg}}
an
(n,0)
form
\psi
of order
>-n
such that
{\bar{\partial}}\psi=\partial\phi.
Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
be so small that
\phi
has order
\epsilon-n.
Then
{\bar{\partial}}^{*}\partial\phi
is an
(n,0)
form of order
\epsilon-n-2.
Put
\alpha:=\epsilon-n
and take
\chi\in\mathop{\rm ker}\Delta^{n-1\,1}_{2-\alpha-2n}=\mathop{\rm ker}\Delta_{2-\epsilon-n}.
Then
{\rm d}\chi
and
{\rm d}^{*}\chi
have order
>-n
so integration by parts shows
{\rm d}\chi\cdot{\rm d}\chi+{\rm d}^{*}\chi\cdot{\rm d}^{*}\chi=\chi\cdot\Delta\chi=0
and
{\rm d}\chi={\rm d}^{*}\chi=0.
Hence it follows that
{\bar{\partial}}\chi=0
and that
\chi\cdot{\bar{\partial}}^{*}\partial\phi=0.
So by Proposition
5.12
there exists an
(n,0)
form
{\tau}
of order
\alpha=\epsilon-n
and with
\frac{1}{2}\Delta{\tau}={\bar{\partial}}^{*}\partial\phi.
Then
\partial\phi-{\bar{\partial}}{\tau}
is
{\bar{\partial}}
closed and co-closed. By Lemma
6.6
there exists on
X^{{\rm reg}}
an
(n,0)
form
\sigma
of order
>n
and with
\partial\phi-{\bar{\partial}}{\tau}={\bar{\partial}}\sigma.
Put
\psi:=\sigma+{\tau},
which is of order
\epsilon-n
and satisfies
{\bar{\partial}}\psi=\partial\phi.

Since
\phi
is an
(n-1,1)
form and
\psi
an
(n,0)
form it follows that
{\rm d}(\phi-\psi)={\bar{\partial}}\phi+\partial\phi-{\bar{\partial}}\psi=0.
Also
\phi-\psi
has order
>-n
and is
L^{2}.
By Theorem
6.5
its de Rham cohomology class
[\phi-\psi]
lies in
{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},{{\mathbb{C}}}).
Recalling again that
\phi
is an
(n-1,1)
form and
\psi
an
(n,0)
form, we can also define
[\phi-\psi]\in\gr^{n-1}{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},{{\mathbb{C}}}).
Notice that
[\phi-\psi]\in{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},{{\mathbb{C}}})
may depend on the choice of
\psi
but that
[\phi-\psi]\in{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},{{\mathbb{C}}})
does not. The map
\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}+{\bar{\partial}}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}\to\gr^{n-1}{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},{{\mathbb{C}}})
is thus well defined. This is of course
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-linear. We show now that it is injective.

Let
\phi\in\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}+{\bar{\partial}}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}
be an element of the kernel of the map
\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}+{\bar{\partial}}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}\to\gr^{n-1}{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{\bullet}_{X^{{\rm reg}}});
that is, there exists an
(n,0)
form
\psi
of order
>-n,
with
\partial\psi={\bar{\partial}}\phi
and with
[\phi-\psi]=0\in\gr^{n-1}{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},{{\mathbb{C}}}).
Then there exists another
(n,0)
form
\psi^{\prime}
with
{\bar{\partial}}\psi^{\prime}=0
and
[\phi-\psi-\psi^{\prime}]=0\in H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},{{\mathbb{C}}}).

As in Remark
4.14
there exists on
X^{{\rm reg}}
an
L^{2}
holomorphic
(n,0)
form
\Omega.
Write
\psi^{\prime}=f\Omega
with
f
some holomorphic function
X^{{\rm reg}}\to{{\mathbb{C}}}.
As
X
is normal
f
extends holomorphically to
X.
In particular, it is bounded; and accordingly,
f\Omega=\psi^{\prime}
is
L^{2}.
Proposition
5.13
implies that
\psi^{\prime}
has order
>-n.

Thus
\phi-\psi-\psi^{\prime}
has order
>-n;
and accordingly,
{\rm d}^{*}(\phi-\psi-\psi^{\prime})
has order
>-n-1.
Take
\epsilon>0
and put
\beta:=1+\epsilon-n.
Take
\chi\in\mathop{\rm ker}\Delta^{n-1}_{2-\beta-n}=\mathop{\rm ker}\Delta^{n-1}_{1-n-\epsilon}.
Letting
\epsilon>0
be small enough and using Corollary
2.11
we see that
{\rm d}\chi
and
{\rm d}^{*}\chi
have order
>-n.
Integration by parts shows therefore that
{\rm d}\chi={\rm d}^{*}\chi=0
and that
\chi\cdot{\rm d}^{*}(\phi-\psi-\psi^{\prime})=0.
By Proposition
5.12
there exists on
X^{{\rm reg}}
an
n-1
form
\theta
of order
\beta=1+\epsilon-n
and with
\Delta\theta={\rm d}^{*}(\phi-\psi-\psi^{\prime}).
Then
{\rm d}{\rm d}^{*}\theta={\rm d}^{*}(\phi-\psi-\psi^{\prime}-{\rm d}\theta).
Applying
{\rm d}^{*}
we get
{\rm d}^{*}{\rm d}{\rm d}^{*}\theta=0;
that is,
{\rm d}^{*}\theta
is a harmonic
n-2
form of order
\epsilon-n.
By Corollary
2.7
however
{\rm d}^{*}\theta
is in fact of order
2-n.
Now
{\rm d}{\rm d}^{*}\theta\cdot{\rm d}{\rm d}^{*}\theta={\rm d}^{*}\theta\cdot\Delta{\rm d}^{*}\theta=0.
So
{\rm d}{\rm d}^{*}\theta=0.
Or equivalently
{\rm d}^{*}(\phi-\psi-\psi^{\prime}-{\rm d}\theta)=0.
Thus
\phi-\psi-\psi^{\prime}-{\rm d}\theta\in\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}+{\bar{\partial}}^{*})^{n}_{-n}.
Since
[\phi-\psi-\psi^{\prime}]=0\in{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},{{\mathbb{C}}})\cong\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}+{\bar{\partial}}^{*})^{n}_{-n}
it follows that
\phi-\psi-\psi^{\prime}-{\rm d}\theta=0;
that is,
\phi-\psi-\psi^{\prime}={\rm d}\theta.
We prove now the following lemma.

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
p,q\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
be such that
p+q=n-1.
Let
\chi
be a
(p,q)
form on
X^{{\rm reg}}
of order
>1-n
and with
{\bar{\partial}}\chi=0.
Then there exists on
X^{{\rm reg}}
a
(p,q-1)
form
\zeta
of order
2-n
and such that
\chi-{\bar{\partial}}\zeta
is
{\rm d}
closed and co-closed.

Proof.

Notice that
{\bar{\partial}}^{*}\chi
is a
(p,q-1)
form of order
>-n.
Take
\epsilon\in(0,1)
and put
\alpha:=2-\epsilon-n.
Take
\zeta\in\mathop{\rm ker}\Delta_{2-\alpha-2n}^{p\,q-1}=\mathop{\rm ker}\Delta^{p\,q-1}_{\epsilon-n}.
Integration by parts shows that
{\bar{\partial}}\zeta={\bar{\partial}}^{*}\zeta=0.
So
\zeta\cdot{\bar{\partial}}^{*}\chi={\bar{\partial}}\zeta\cdot\chi=0.
Letting
\epsilon
be a non-exceptional value, we see from Proposition
5.12
that there exists on
X^{{\rm reg}}
some
(p,q-1)
form
\zeta
of order
\alpha=2-\epsilon-n
and with
\frac{1}{2}\Delta\zeta={\bar{\partial}}^{*}\chi.
Then
{\bar{\partial}}{\bar{\partial}}^{*}\zeta={\bar{\partial}}^{*}(\chi-{\bar{\partial}}\zeta)
and applying
{\bar{\partial}}^{*}
we get
{\bar{\partial}}^{*}{\bar{\partial}}{\bar{\partial}}^{*}\zeta=0.
So
{\bar{\partial}}^{*}\theta
is a harmonic
n-3
form of order
1-\epsilon-n.
By Corollary
2.7
however
{\bar{\partial}}^{*}\zeta
has in fact order
3-n.
Now
{\bar{\partial}}{\bar{\partial}}^{*}\zeta\cdot{\bar{\partial}}{\bar{\partial}}^{*}\zeta={\bar{\partial}}^{*}\theta\cdot\Delta{\bar{\partial}}^{*}\zeta=0.
So
{\bar{\partial}}{\bar{\partial}}^{*}\zeta=0.
Or equivalently
{\bar{\partial}}^{*}(\chi-{\bar{\partial}}\zeta)=0.
Now
\chi-{\bar{\partial}}\zeta
is
{\bar{\partial}}
closed and co-closed, and in particular harmonic. It is also of order
\alpha-1=1-\epsilon-n.
By Lemma
5.15
therefore
\chi-{\bar{\partial}}\zeta
is
{\rm d}
closed and co-closed.
∎

For
(p,q)
with
p+q=n-1
denote by
\theta^{pq}
the
(p,q)
component of the
n-1
form
\theta.
Since
{\rm d}\theta=\phi-\psi-\psi^{\prime}
has only
(n,0)
and
(n-1,1)
components it follows that
{\bar{\partial}}\theta^{n-2\,1}=0.
Applying Lemma
6.8
to
\theta^{n-2\,1}
we find some
(n-2,0)
form
\zeta
of order
2-n
such that
\theta^{n-2\,1}-{\bar{\partial}}\zeta
is
{\rm d}
-closed. In particular,
\partial\theta^{n-2\,1}=\partial{\bar{\partial}}\zeta=-{\bar{\partial}}\partial\zeta.
Put
\eta:=\theta^{n-1\,0}-\partial\zeta,
which is of order
1-n.
Then
{\rm d}\eta={\rm d}\theta^{n-1\,0}+\partial{\bar{\partial}}\zeta={\rm d}\theta^{n-1\,0}+\partial\theta^{n-2\,1}=\phi-\psi-\psi^{\prime}.
Looking at the
(n-1,1)
components of both sides it follows that
{\bar{\partial}}\eta=\phi.
But
\phi
has order
>-n
so
\phi\cdot\phi={\bar{\partial}}^{*}\phi\cdot\eta=0
because
{\bar{\partial}}^{*}\phi=0.
Thus
\phi=0,
which proves that the map
\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}+{\bar{\partial}}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}\to\gr^{n-1}{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{\bullet}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
is injective.
∎

We prove a corollary of Theorem
6.7
.

Corollary 6.9
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler
n
-conifold and give it a Kähler conifold metric. The natural projection
\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}+{\bar{\partial}}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}\to H^{1}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{n-1}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
is then injective.

Proof.

Let
\phi\in\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}+{\bar{\partial}}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}
have vanishing cohomology class in
H^{1}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{n-1}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).
So there exists on
X^{{\rm reg}}
an
(n-1,0)
form
\chi
with
{\bar{\partial}}\chi=\phi.
As in the proof of Theorem
6.7
choose on
X^{{\rm reg}}
an
(n,0)
form
\psi
of order
>-n
such that
{\rm d}(\phi+\psi)=0.
Now
\phi+\psi-{\rm d}\chi
is an
(n,0)
form, which means that the map
\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}+{\bar{\partial}}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}\to\gr^{n-1}{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},{{\mathbb{C}}})
produced in Theorem
6.7
maps
\phi
to
0.
But this map is injective so
\phi=0
as we have to prove.
∎

The image of the natural projection
\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}+{\bar{\partial}}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}\to H^{1}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{n-1}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
has also the following property.

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler
n
-conifold and give it a Kähler conifold metric. Then
{}_{c}H^{1}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{n-1}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
lies in the image of the natural projection
\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}+{\bar{\partial}}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}\to H^{1}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{n-1}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).

Proof.

Take any element of
{}_{c}H^{1}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{n-1}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
represented on
X^{{\rm reg}}
by some compactly supported
(n-1,1)
form
\phi
with
{\bar{\partial}}\phi=0.
Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
be so small as in Lemma
5.15
and put
\alpha=1+\epsilon-n.
Then by Lemma
5.15
every
\chi\in\Delta^{n-1\,0}_{2-\alpha-2n}=\Delta^{n-1\,0}_{1-\epsilon-n}
satisfies
{\rm d}\chi=0
and in particular
{\bar{\partial}}\chi=0.
So
{\bar{\partial}}^{*}\phi\cdot\chi=0.
By Proposition
5.12
therefore there exists
\psi\in H^{2}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{n-1\,0}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
with
\frac{1}{2}\Delta\psi={\bar{\partial}}^{*}\phi.
Or equivalently,
{\bar{\partial}}^{*}{\bar{\partial}}\psi={\bar{\partial}}^{*}(\phi-{\bar{\partial}}\psi).
Applying
{\bar{\partial}}^{*}
we get
\Delta{\bar{\partial}}^{*}\psi=0;
that is,
{\bar{\partial}}^{*}\psi
is a harmonic
n-2
form of order
\alpha-1=\epsilon-n.
By Corollary
2.7
it has in fact order
2-n.
Now
{\bar{\partial}}{\bar{\partial}}^{*}\psi\cdot{\bar{\partial}}{\bar{\partial}}^{*}\psi={\bar{\partial}}^{*}\psi\cdot\Delta{\bar{\partial}}^{*}\psi=0.
So
{\bar{\partial}}{\bar{\partial}}^{*}\psi=0.
Or equivalently,
{\bar{\partial}}^{*}(\phi-{\bar{\partial}}\psi)=0;
that is,
\phi-{\bar{\partial}}\psi
is a
{\bar{\partial}}
closed and co-closed
(n-1,1)
form of order
\epsilon-n
as we have to prove.
∎

We finally prove

Theorem 6.11
.

Let
X
be a compact Calabi–Yau
n
-conifold with
H^{2}_{X^{\rm sing}}(X,\Omega^{n-2}_{X})=0
and give
X
a Kähler conifold metric. Then there exist
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-vector space isomorphisms

H^{1}(X,\Theta_{X})\cong\gr^{n-1}{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{\bullet}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\cong\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}+{\bar{\partial}}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}.

(6.2)

Proof.

The
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}
module sheaf isomorphism
\Omega^{n}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}\cong{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}
implies an
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}
module sheaf isomorphism
\Theta_{X^{{\rm reg}}}\cong\Omega^{n-1}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}.
Corollary
6.3
implies therefore a
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-vector space isomorphism
{}_{c}H^{1}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{n-1}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\cong H^{1}(X,\iota_{*}\Omega^{n-1}_{X}).

By Corollary
6.9
and Lemma
6.10
there is an injective map
{}_{c}H^{1}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{n-1}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}+{\bar{\partial}}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}.
Composing this with the injective map produced in Theorem
6.7
we get an injective map
{}_{c}H^{1}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{n-1}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to\gr^{n-1}{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{\bullet}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).
We show that this is surjective too. Take any element of
\gr^{n-1}{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{\bullet}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
and represent it on
X^{{\rm reg}}
by the sum
\phi=\phi^{n0}+\phi^{n-1\,1}
of an
(n,0)
form and an
(n-1,1)
form. As
[\phi]\in{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},{{\mathbb{C}}})
there exists near each
x\in X^{\rm sing},
on a punctured neighbourhood
U_{x}
of it, some
n-1
form
\psi
with
{\rm d}\psi=\phi.
More explicitly, using the radius function
r
we can write
\phi={\rm d}r\wedge\phi^{\prime}+\phi^{\prime\prime}
and simple computation shows that
{\rm d}(\int_{0}^{r}\phi^{\prime}{\rm d}r)=\phi.
We can thus take
\psi:=\int_{0}^{r}\phi^{\prime}{\rm d}r.
Since
\phi^{\prime}=\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\mathbin{\lrcorner}\phi
is the sum of an
(n-1,0)
form and an
(n-2,1)
form it follows that so is
\psi,
which we write as
\psi^{n-1\,0}+\psi^{n-2\,1}.
Since
{\rm d}\psi
has vanishing
(n-2,2)
component it follows that
{\bar{\partial}}\psi^{n-2\,1}=0.
Recall from hypothesis that
H^{1}(U_{x},\Omega^{n-2}_{X})\cong H^{2}_{x}(X,\Omega^{n-2}_{X})=0.
Then we can find on
U_{x}
an
(n-2,0)
form
\chi
such that
{\bar{\partial}}\chi=\phi^{n-2\,1}.
Now on
U_{x}
we have

\phi^{n-1\,1}={\bar{\partial}}\psi^{n-1\,0}+\partial\psi^{n-2\,1}={\bar{\partial}}\psi^{n-1\,0}-{\bar{\partial}}\partial\chi.

(6.3)

So
\phi^{n-1\,1}
defines an element of
{}_{c}H^{1}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{n-1}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).
This element maps to
[\phi]
under the map
{}_{c}H^{1}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{n-1}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to\gr^{n-1}{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{\bullet}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}),
which is thus surjective.

The map
{}_{c}H^{1}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{n-1}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to\gr^{n-1}{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{\bullet}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
is now a
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-vector space isomorphism which is the composite of the injective maps
{}_{c}H^{1}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{n-1}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}+{\bar{\partial}}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}
and
\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}+{\bar{\partial}}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}\to\gr^{n-1}{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{\bullet}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).
The latter two maps are therefore isomorphisms too, which completes the proof.
∎

7
Harmonic
n-1
Forms

The following is an analogue of Lemma
6.10
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler
n
-conifold and give it a Kähler conifold metric. Take
p,q\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
with
p+q=n-1.
Then
{}_{c}H^{q}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{p}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
lies in the image of the natural projection
\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}+{\bar{\partial}}^{*})^{pq}_{1-n}\to H^{q}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{p}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).

Proof.

Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
be small enough and put
\alpha:=-\epsilon+2-n.
We show first that every
\chi\in\mathop{\rm ker}\Delta^{p\,q-1}_{2-\alpha-2n}=\mathop{\rm ker}\Delta^{p\,q-1}_{-n+\epsilon}
satisfies
{\bar{\partial}}\chi=0.
Corollary
2.7
and Proposition
5.11
imply that
\chi\in\mathop{\rm ker}\Delta^{p\,q-1}_{2-n-\delta}
for any
\delta>0.
Expanding
\chi
near
X^{\rm sing}
as in Theorem
2.10
we see that
\chi
has order
2-n.
Integration by parts therefore makes sense so that
\chi\cdot{\rm d}^{*}{\rm d}\chi={\rm d}\chi\cdot{\rm d}\chi+{\rm d}^{*}\chi\cdot{\rm d}^{*}\chi.
So
{\rm d}\chi=0.
As
\chi
is a pure
(p,q)
form, we have
{\bar{\partial}}\chi=0.

Take an element of
H^{q}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{p}_{X})
represented on
X^{{\rm reg}}
by a compactly supported
(p,q)
form
\phi
with
{\bar{\partial}}\phi=0.
Letting
\chi
be as above we have
0=\phi\cdot{\bar{\partial}}\chi={\bar{\partial}}^{*}\phi\cdot\chi.
But
\chi
is an arbitrary element of
\mathop{\rm ker}\Delta^{p\,q-1}_{2-\alpha-2n},
so
{\bar{\partial}}^{*}\phi
is orthogonal to
\mathop{\rm ker}\Delta^{p\,q-1}_{2-\alpha-2n}.
Proposition
5.12
implies therefore that
{\bar{\partial}}^{*}\phi
lies in the image of the Fredholm operator
\Delta:H^{2}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{p\,q-1}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to L^{2}_{\alpha-2}(\Lambda^{p\,q-1}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).
Write
{\bar{\partial}}^{*}\phi=\frac{1}{2}\Delta\psi
with
\psi\in H^{2}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{p\,q-1}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).
Then
{\bar{\partial}}^{*}(\phi-{\bar{\partial}}\psi)={\bar{\partial}}{\bar{\partial}}^{*}\psi
and so
{\bar{\partial}}^{*}\psi
is harmonic. As
\alpha-1=-\epsilon+1-n>-n
it follows from Corollary
2.7
that
{\bar{\partial}}^{*}\psi
has order
2-n.
Integration by parts therefore makes sense and we have
{\bar{\partial}}{\bar{\partial}}^{*}\psi=0.
So
{\bar{\partial}}^{*}(\phi-{\bar{\partial}}\psi)={\bar{\partial}}{\bar{\partial}}^{*}\psi=0
and
\phi-{\bar{\partial}}\psi
is harmonic of order
1-n.
By Corollary
2.11
then
{\rm d}(\phi-{\bar{\partial}}\psi)
and
{\rm d}^{*}(\phi-{\bar{\partial}}\psi)
have order
-n.
Integration by parts implies therefore
{\rm d}(\phi-{\bar{\partial}}\psi)={\rm d}^{*}(\phi-{\bar{\partial}}\psi)=0.
Thus
\phi-{\bar{\partial}}\psi
is a representative we want of the cohomology class of
\phi.

∎

Under the hypothesis of Theorem
1.2
we can improve the result above.

Corollary 7.2
.

Let
X,n,p
and
q
be as in Lemma
7.1
. Suppose that for each
x\in X^{\rm sing}
we have
b_{n-2}(C_{x}^{{\rm reg}})=0
or
b_{n-1}(C_{x}^{{\rm reg}})=0.
Then there exists
\epsilon>0
such that
{}_{c}H^{q}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{p}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
lies in the image of the natural projection
\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}+{\bar{\partial}}^{*})^{pq}_{1+\epsilon-n}\to H^{q}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{p}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).

Proof.

We modify the proof of Lemma
7.1
. Let
\epsilon>0
be small enough and define
\alpha:X^{\rm sing}\to{{\mathbb{R}}}
by saying that for
x\in X^{\rm sing}
with
b_{n-2}(C_{x}^{{\rm reg}})=0
we should have
\alpha(x)=\epsilon+2-n
and for every other
x\in X^{\rm sing}
(with
b_{n-1}(C_{x}^{{\rm reg}})=0
) we should have
\alpha(x)=-\epsilon+2-n.
We show that every
\chi\in\mathop{\rm ker}\Delta^{p\,q-1}_{2-\alpha-2n}
satisfies
{\bar{\partial}}\chi=0.
For
x\in X^{\rm sing}
with
b_{n-2}(C_{x}^{{\rm reg}})=0
the order of
\chi
is
-n-\epsilon,
and Corollaries
2.7
and
2.12
imply that it is in fact
2-n.
For
x\in X^{\rm sing}
with
b_{n-1}(C_{x}^{{\rm reg}})=0
the order of
\chi
is
-n+\epsilon
and Corollary
2.7
implies that it is in fact
2-n.
In both cases
\chi
has order
2-n
and as in the proof of Lemma
7.1
we can verify
{\bar{\partial}}\chi=0.

Let
\phi,\psi
be as in the proof of Lemma
7.1
. Then
\psi
has order
\alpha,
and
\phi-{\bar{\partial}}\psi
is a harmonic form of order
\alpha-1.
For
x\in X^{\rm sing}
with
b_{n-2}(C_{x}^{{\rm reg}})=0
we have
\alpha(x)=\epsilon+2-n>2-n.
So
\phi-{\bar{\partial}}\psi
has order
>1-n
at
x.
On the other hand, for
x\in X^{\rm sing}
with
b_{n-1}(C_{x}^{{\rm reg}})=0
we expand
\phi-{\bar{\partial}}\psi
at
x
into the sum of homogeneous harmonic forms. As
\alpha(x)-1=-\epsilon+1-n
with
\epsilon
small enough the leading term has order
1-n
which, by Corollary
2.11
, vanishes. So
\phi-{\bar{\partial}}\psi
has order
>1-n,
which completes the proof.
∎

We make a more careful study of
(1,n-2)
forms. We prove a lemma we will use for the next theorem.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
X
be a compact Riemannian
l
-conifold,
p
an integer and
\phi
a
C^{\infty}

p
-form on
X^{{\rm reg}}
of order
>-p.
Then every
x\in X^{\rm sing}
has a punctured neighbourhood
U^{{\rm reg}}
on which
\phi
is
{\rm d}
-exact.

Proof.

Denote by
C_{x}
the model cone at
x
of
X.
Write
\phi={\rm d}\log r\wedge\phi^{\prime}+\phi^{\prime\prime}
where
\phi^{\prime}
is the pull-back of some
p-1
form on
C^{{\rm lk}}_{x},
and
\phi^{\prime\prime}
that of some
p
-form on
C^{{\rm lk}}_{x}.
Making
U^{{\rm reg}}
small enough we can suppose that it is diffeomorphic to
(0,\delta)\times C^{{\rm lk}}_{x}
for some
\delta>0.
Take any
p
-cycle
A
on
C^{{\rm lk}}_{x}.
Then
\int_{\{r\}\times A}\phi=\int_{\{r\}\times A}\phi^{\prime\prime}
is independent of
r\in(0,\delta).
But since
\phi
is of order
>-p
it follows that so is
\phi^{\prime\prime}
and hence that
\int_{\{r\}\times A}\phi^{\prime\prime}
converges to
0
as
r
tends to
0.
Thus
\int_{\{r\}\times A}\phi=0,
which implies that
\phi
is
{\rm d}
-exact on
U^{{\rm reg}}.

∎

For
(1,n-2)
forms we improve Corollary
7.2
as follows.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler
n
-conifold whose singularities are of depth
\geqslant n.
Suppose that for each
x\in X^{\rm sing}
we have
b_{n-2}(C_{x}^{{\rm reg}})=0
or
b_{n-1}(C_{x}^{{\rm reg}})=0.
Give
X
a Kähler conifold metric. Then there exists
\epsilon>0
such that
{}_{c}H^{n-2}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{1}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
agrees with the image of the natural projection
\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}+{\bar{\partial}}^{*})^{1\,n-2}_{1+\epsilon-n}\to H^{n-2}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{1}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).

Proof.

Let
\phi
be a harmonic
(1,n-2)
form on
X^{{\rm reg}}
of order
>1-n.
Integration by parts shows that
{\rm d}\phi={\rm d}^{*}\phi=0.
Lemma
7.3
implies that every
x\in X^{\rm sing}
has a punctured neighbourhood on which we can write
\phi={\rm d}\psi
with
\psi
some
n-2
form. As
\phi
is a
(1,n-2)
form we can write also
\phi=\partial\psi^{\prime}+{\bar{\partial}}\psi^{\prime\prime}
where
\psi^{\prime}
is some
(0,n-2)
form with
{\bar{\partial}}\psi^{\prime}=0,
and
\psi^{\prime\prime}
some
(1,n-3)
form; this may be verified in the same way as in the paragraph containing (
6.3
). Let
U
be a Stein neighbourhood of
x\in X^{\rm sing}
(which is an ordinary neighbourhood and contains therefore
x\in X^{\rm sing}
). Since
(X,x)
has depth
\geqslant n
it follows then that
H^{n-2}(U\setminus\{x\},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})\cong H^{n-1}_{x}(U,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})=0.
So
\psi^{\prime}={\bar{\partial}}\chi
where
\chi
is some
(0,n-3)
form on
U\setminus\{x\}.
Thus
\phi=\partial{\bar{\partial}}\chi+{\bar{\partial}}\psi^{\prime\prime}={\bar{\partial}}(-\partial\chi+\psi^{\prime\prime})
is
{\bar{\partial}}
exact. Using cut-off functions, we see that the
{\bar{\partial}}
cohomology class of
\phi
lies in
{}_{c}H^{n-2}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{1}_{X}).

For
\epsilon>0
the image of the natural projection
\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}+{\bar{\partial}}^{*})^{1\,n-2}_{1+\epsilon-n}\to H^{n-2}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{1}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
thus lies in
{}_{c}H^{n-2}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{1}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).
This with Corollary
7.2
completes the proof.
∎

8
Deformation Functors

We make a basic definition we will use in what follows.

Definition 8.1
.

For
{{\mathbb{K}}}={{\mathbb{R}}}
or
{{\mathbb{C}}}
a
local
{{\mathbb{K}}}
-algebra
is a
{{\mathbb{K}}}
-algebra
A
with unique maximal ideal
{\mathfrak{m}}A
such that the natural maps
{{\mathbb{K}}}\to A\to A/{\mathfrak{m}}A
induce an isomorphism
{{\mathbb{K}}}\cong A/{\mathfrak{m}}A.
An
Artin
local
{{\mathbb{K}}}
-algebra is a local
{{\mathbb{K}}}
-algebra
A
which is an
Artin
ring; that is, every descending chain of ideals in it should be finite.

Remark 8.2
.

It is well-known that Artin rings are Noetherian rings. Moreover, for a local
{{\mathbb{K}}}
-algebra
A
the following three conditions are equivalent:
(i)

A
is an Artin ring;
(ii)

A
is a Noetherian ring, and there exists an integer
n\geqslant 1
such that
({\mathfrak{m}}A)^{n}=0;
and
(iii)

A
is a finite-dimensional
{{\mathbb{K}}}
-vector space. The proof is as follows. If (i) holds then by the descending chain condition there exists an integer
n\geqslant 1
such that
({\mathfrak{m}}A)^{n}=({\mathfrak{m}}A)^{n+1}.
Nakayama’s lemma implies therefore
({\mathfrak{m}}A)^{n}=0.
Using the
A
-module exact sequence
0\to({\mathfrak{m}}A)^{k}\to({\mathfrak{m}}A)^{k-1}\to({\mathfrak{m}}A)^{k-1}/({\mathfrak{m}}A)^{k}\to 0
for
k=1,\dots,n
we see also that (iii) holds. Conversely, it is clear that (iii) implies the descending chain condition which is equivalent to (i). The three conditions are thus equivalent.

We prove a lemma we will use in Definition
8.12
.

Lemma 8.3
.

If
A
is an Artin local
{{\mathbb{R}}}
-algebra then the tensor product
A\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}{{\mathbb{C}}},
which is naturally a
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebra, is an Artin local
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebra.

Proof.

The composite of the natural maps
A\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}{{\mathbb{C}}}\to(A/{\mathfrak{m}}A)\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}{{\mathbb{C}}}\cong{{\mathbb{C}}}
has kernel
{\mathfrak{m}}A\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}{{\mathbb{C}}},
which is therefore a maximal ideal of
A\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}{{\mathbb{C}}}.
We show that its complement consists of invertible elements. Take
a\in(A\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}{{\mathbb{C}}})\setminus({\mathfrak{m}}A\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}{{\mathbb{C}}})
and write
a=:a^{\prime}\otimes 1+a^{\prime\prime}\otimes i
with
a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime}\in A.
Put
a^{\prime}=:b^{\prime}+c^{\prime}
and
a^{\prime\prime}=:b^{\prime\prime}+c^{\prime\prime}
where
b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime}\in{{\mathbb{C}}}
and
c^{\prime},c^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathfrak{m}}A.
Since
a\notin({\mathfrak{m}}A\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}{{\mathbb{C}}})
it follows that
b:=b^{\prime}\otimes 1+b^{\prime\prime}\otimes i\in{{\mathbb{C}}}\setminus\{0\}.
Since
c^{\prime},c^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathfrak{m}}A
are nilpotent it follows that so is
c:=c^{\prime}\otimes 1+c^{\prime\prime}\otimes i\in A\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}{{\mathbb{C}}}.
As
b\neq 0
we can define
\frac{c}{b}\in A\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}{{\mathbb{C}}},
and
c
nilpotent implies
\frac{c}{b}
nilpotent. Consequently
1+\frac{c}{b}
is invertible and accordingly so is
b+c=a.

The ring
A\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}{{\mathbb{C}}}
is thus a local ring with unique maximal ideal
{\mathfrak{m}}A\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}{{\mathbb{C}}}.
Since
A
is a finite-dimensional
{{\mathbb{R}}}
-vector space it follows that
A\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}{{\mathbb{C}}}
is a finite-dimensional
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-vector space, which must therefore be an Artin ring as we have to prove.
∎

Definition 8.4
.

Let
{{\mathbb{K}}}={{\mathbb{R}}}
or
{{\mathbb{C}}}.
We denote by
({\rm Art})_{{\mathbb{K}}}
the category whose objects are Artin local
{{\mathbb{K}}}
-algebras and whose morphisms are
{{\mathbb{K}}}
-algebra homomorphisms. A
small extension
homomorphism in
({\rm Art})_{{\mathbb{K}}}
is a surjective
{{\mathbb{K}}}
-algebra homomorphism
A\to B
whose kernel is a non-zero principal ideal
(\epsilon)\subseteq A
such that the product ideal
(\epsilon){\mathfrak{m}}A\subseteq A
vanishes.

Definition 8.5
.

Denote by
({\rm sets})
the category with objects sets and morphisms maps. Call a functor
D:({\rm Art})_{{\mathbb{C}}}\to({\rm sets})
a
deformation
functor if
D({{\mathbb{C}}})
consists of a single element. For a deformation functor
D:({\rm Art})_{{\mathbb{C}}}\to({\rm sets})
we consider the following conditions:

(H1)

Let
A,B,C
be Artin local
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebra,
A\to C
a
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebra homomorphism and
B\to C
a small extension homomorphism in
({\rm Art})_{{\mathbb{C}}}.
The induced map
D(A\times_{C}B)\to D(A)\times_{D(C)}D(B)
is then surjective.

(H2)

Let
A
be an Artin local
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebra and take
B:={{\mathbb{C}}}[t]/t^{2}.
The induced map
D(A\times_{{\mathbb{C}}}B)\to D(A)\times_{D({{\mathbb{C}}})}D(B)
is then bijective.

It is known that (H1) and (H2) imply the following condition:

If
A\to B
is a small extension homomorphism in
({\rm Art})_{{\mathbb{C}}}
then the additive group
D({{\mathbb{C}}}[t]/t^{2})
acts transitively upon the non-empty fibres of
D(A)\to D(B).

(8.1)

This is proved as follows. Denote by
\pi:A\to A/{\mathfrak{m}}A\cong{{\mathbb{C}}}
the natural projection and by
(\epsilon):=\mathop{\rm ker}(A\to B)
the non-zero principal ideal of
A.
There is then a
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebra isomorphism
A\times_{{\mathbb{C}}}({{\mathbb{C}}}[t]/t^{2})\cong A\times_{B}A
defined by
(a,\pi a+\lambda t)\mapsto(a,a+\lambda\epsilon)
for
a\in A
and
\lambda\in{{\mathbb{C}}}.
Using this and the condition (H1) we get a bijection
D(A)\times_{D({{\mathbb{C}}})}D({{\mathbb{C}}}[t]/t^{2})\cong D(A\times_{B}A).
On the other hand, (H2) implies that the induced map
D(A\times_{B}A)\to D(A)\times_{D(B)}D(A)
is surjective. Combining these two maps we get a surjection
D(A)\times_{D({{\mathbb{C}}})}D({{\mathbb{C}}}[t]/t^{2})\to D(A)\times_{D(B)}D(A)
which defines the transitive action we want.

It is also easy to show that if (H2) holds then
D({{\mathbb{C}}}[t]/t^{2})
has a natural
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-vector space structure. In this case consider the following condition:

(H3)

D({{\mathbb{C}}}[t]/t^{2})
is a finite-dimensional
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-vector space.

Schlessinger
[
55
, Theorem 2.11(1)]
proves that (H1)–(H3) hold if and only if
D
has a hull
[
55
, Definition 2.7]
.

Define for
k=0,1,2,\dots
two
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebras
A_{k}:={{\mathbb{C}}}[t]/t^{k+1}
and
A_{k}[\epsilon]:={{\mathbb{C}}}[t,\epsilon]/(t^{k+1},\epsilon^{2}).
Consider the natural projection
A_{k}[\epsilon]\to A_{k}
and the induced map
D(A_{k}[\epsilon])\to D(A_{k}).
For
\xi\in D(A_{k})
denote by
T^{1}(\xi)
the set of
\eta\in D(A_{k}[\epsilon])
which maps to
\xi
under
D(A_{k}[\epsilon])\to D(A_{k}).
Following
[
28
, §1.5]
consider the condition called (H5). If (H1)–(H3) and (H5) hold then for
k=0,1,2,\dots
and
\xi\in D(A_{k})
there is on
T^{1}(\xi)
a natural
A_{k}
module structure.

An
obstruction space of

D
a
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-vector space
T^{2}
with the following two properties:
(i)
for every small extension
0\to(\epsilon)\to A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\to}}B\to 0
in
({\rm Art})_{{\mathbb{C}}}
there exists a sequence
D(A)\xrightarrow{D(f)}D(B)\to T^{2}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}(\epsilon)
which is exact in the sense that the image of the former map
D(f)
agrees with the fibre over
0\in T^{2}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}(\epsilon)
of the latter map; and
(ii)
if there is in
({\rm Art})_{{\mathbb{C}}}
a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\epsilon)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\epsilon^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

(8.2)

whose rows are small extension homomorphisms then there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}(\epsilon)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(A^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(B^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}(\epsilon^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm id}\otimes\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(g)}

whose rows are the exact sequences in (i) just mentioned. We call the map
D(A)\to T^{2}(X)\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}(\epsilon)
the
obstruction map of
(T^{2}(X),f).

We recall a version we will use of
T^{1}
lift theorems; for the original versions see
[
37
,
49
]
, and for the more complex version we will use to prove Theorem
1.2
see Lemma
8.19
.

Theorem 8.6

(Theorem 1.8 of
[
28
]
)
.

Let
({\rm Art})_{{\mathbb{C}}}\to({\rm sets})
be a deformation functor satisfying
(H1)–(H3)
and
(H5)
and having an obstruction space. For
k=1,2,3,\dots
denote by
\pi_{k}:A_{k}\to A_{k-1}
the natural projection. Suppose that for
k=1,2,3,\dots
and
\xi\in D(A_{k}),
if we put
\eta:=D(\pi_{k})(\xi)\in D(A_{k-1})
then the natural map
T^{1}(\xi)\to T^{1}(\eta)
is surjective. The maps
D(\pi_{1}),D(\pi_{2}),D(\pi_{3}),\dots
are then all surjective. ∎

Remark 8.7
.

More precisely, the following holds. Take
k=1,2,3,\dots
and
\xi\in D(A_{k}).
Define a
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebra homomorphism
\theta_{k}:A_{k}\to A_{k-1}[\epsilon]
by
t\mapsto t+\epsilon
module ideals (so that if we define
\eta
as in Theorem
8.6
above then
D(\theta_{k})(\xi)\in T^{1}(\eta)
). Denote by
\varpi_{k}:A_{k}[\epsilon]\to A_{k-1}[\epsilon]
the natural projection. Then
\xi
lies in the image of
D(\pi_{k+1}):D(A_{k+1})\to D(A_{k})
if and only if
D(\theta_{k})(\xi)
lies in the image of
D(\varpi_{k}):D(A_{k}[\epsilon])\to D(A_{k-1}[\epsilon]).

We turn now to the examples of deformation functors. We begin by recalling the definition of
A
-ringed spaces.

Definition 8.8
.

Let
A
be a commutative ring with unit. Then an
A
-ringed space
is the pair
(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})
where
X
is a topological space and
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}
a sheaf on
X
of
A
-algebras. A
morphism
from an
A
-ringed space
(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})
to another
A
-ringed space
(Y,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{Y})
is the pair of a continuous map
X\to Y
and an
A
-algebra sheaf homomorphism
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{Y}\to f_{*}{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}.

Remark 8.9
.

Complex spaces are thus
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-ringed spaces. Morphisms of complex spaces are by definition the morphisms of
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-ringed spaces.

We recall the standard definitions about deformations of complex spaces.

Definition 8.10
.

Let
X
be a compact complex space. A
deformation of
X
is the data
({{\mathcal{X}}},S,o,f,\phi)
where
{{\mathcal{X}}},S
are complex analytic spaces,
o\in S
a point of the underlying topological space,
f:{{\mathcal{X}}}\to S
a proper flat morphism of complex spaces, and
\phi:{{\mathcal{X}}}\times_{S}\{o\}\cong X
a complex space isomorphism. For
X
compact, we require
f
to be proper. We omit
o,f,\phi
when they are clear from the context.

Fix a complex space
S
and a point
o\in S.
Let
f:{{\mathcal{X}}}\to S
and
g:{{\mathcal{Y}}}\to S
be deformations of
X.
Then an
isomorphism
from
f:{{\mathcal{X}}}\to S
to
g:{{\mathcal{Y}}}\to S
is a complex space isomorphism
{{\mathcal{X}}}\to{{\mathcal{Y}}}
which induces over
o\in S
the identity map
X\cong{{\mathcal{X}}}\times_{S}\{o\}\to{{\mathcal{Y}}}\times_{S}\{o\}\cong X.

There is always a deformation of
X
defined by
X\times S
with the projection
X\times S\to S,
which we call the
trivial
deformation of
X.

For an Artin local
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebra
B
we denote by
\spec B
the complex space whose underlying topological space consists of one point and whose stalk over it is exactly
B.
A deformation
over

B
of
X
is a deformation
({{\mathcal{X}}},S,o,f,\phi)
with
S=\spec B.
We denote this by
{{\mathcal{X}}}/B
for short.

Remark 8.11
.

It follows from definition that
{{\mathcal{X}}}
has the same underlying space as
X
and that
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
is a sheaf of
B
-algebras. Thus
{{\mathcal{X}}}
is a
B
-ringed space
(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}).
Isomorphisms of two deformations over
B
are the
B
-ringed space isomorphisms.

We define also deformations over real parameter spaces. This will be crucial to defining real differential forms including Kähler forms; for more details see Definitions
9.4
and
9.7
.

Definition 8.12
.

Let
X
be a complex space and
A
an object of
({\rm Art})_{{\mathbb{R}}}.
Recall from Lemma
8.3
that
B:=A\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}{{\mathbb{C}}}
is an Artin local
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebra. A
deformation
over
A
of
X
is a deformation over
B
of
X.
We denote this by
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A.
So
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A={{\mathcal{X}}}/B
in notation, and we choose the more convenient one according to the context.

We give now the first key example of deformation functors. We give only a short account of the relevant facts; for more details see for instance
[
46
]
.

Example 8.13
.

Let
X
be a compact reduced complex space. Denote by
D:({\rm Art})_{{\mathbb{C}}}\to({\rm sets})
the deformation functor which assigns to every Artin local
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebra
A
the set of isomorphism classes of deformations over
A
of
X.
It is known that
D
satisfies (H1)–(H3) and (H5). It has also an obstruction space
T^{2}(X):=\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{2}_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}}(L_{X},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})
where
L_{X}\in D^{-}(\Mod{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})
is the cotangent complex of
X.
We can therefore apply Theorem
8.6
to the deformation functor
D.

It is known that for
k=0,1,2,\dots,
if
X_{k}/A_{k}
is a deformation of
X
then its
T^{1}
module
T^{1}(X_{k}/A_{k})
is isomorphic to
\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{1}_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X_{k}}}(\Omega^{1}_{X_{k}},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X_{k}}).
Thus, if for
k=1,2,3,\dots
the natural map
T^{1}(X_{k}/A_{k})\to T^{1}(X_{k-1}/A_{k-1})
is always surjective then so are
D(\pi_{1}),D(\pi_{2}),D(\pi_{3}),\dots
as in Theorem
8.6
. By
[
18
,
26
,
39
]
there exists a Kuranishi space
\Def(X),
the base space of semi-universal deformations of
X.
If
D(\pi_{1}),D(\pi_{2}),D(\pi_{3}),\dots
are surjective then every tangent vector to
\Def(X)
may be lifted to a formal path; that is,
\Def(X)
is non-singular.

Deformations of complex space germs are defined in the same way as in Definition
8.10
. One difference is that a deformation
({{\mathcal{X}}},x)\to(S,o)
of the germ
(X,x)
is no longer a proper map. But otherwise the modification is straightforward. The corresponding deformation functors have properties similar to those of Example
8.13
, as we recall briefly now; for more details see for instance
[
30
]
.

Example 8.14
.

Let
(X,x)
be the germ of a reduced complex space. Denote by
D:({\rm Art})_{{\mathbb{C}}}\to({\rm sets})
the deformation functor which assigns to every Artin local
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebra
A
the set of isomorphism classes of deformations over
A
of
X.
It is known that
D
satisfies (H1)–(H3) and (H5). It has also an obstruction space
T^{2}:=\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{2}_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,x}}(L_{X,x},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,x})
where
L_{X,x}\in D^{-}(\Mod{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,x})
is the cotangent complex of
(X,x).
We can therefore apply Theorem
8.6
to the deformation functor
D.

It is known that for
k=0,1,2,\dots,
if
X_{k}/A_{k}
is a deformation of
(X,x)
then its
T^{1}
module is isomorphic to

\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{1}_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X_{k},x}}((\Omega^{1}_{X_{k}/A_{k}})_{x},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X_{k},x}).
Thus, if for
k=1,2,3,\dots
the natural map
T^{1}(X_{k}/A_{k})\to T^{1}(X_{k-1}/A_{k-1})
is always surjective then so are
D(\pi_{1}),D(\pi_{2}),D(\pi_{3}),\dots
as in Theorem
8.6
. This will imply that the Kuranishi space, which exists by
[
17
,
63
]
, is non-singular.

We recall also the basic facts about locally trivial deformations; for more details see for instance
[
2
, Corollary 2.6 and Remark 2.7]
.

Example 8.15
.

Let
X
be a compact complex space. Denote by
D:({\rm Art})_{{\mathbb{C}}}\to({\rm sets})
the deformation functor which assigns to every Artin local
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebra
A
the set of isomorphism classes of locally trivial deformations over
A
of
X.
It is known that
D
satisfies (H1)–(H3) and (H5). It has also an obstruction space
T^{2}(X):=H^{2}(X,\Theta_{X}).
We can therefore apply Theorem
8.6
to
D.

Suppose now that
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
is a locally trivial deformation of
X
so its isomorphism class defines an element of
D(A).
There exists then a surjective
A
-module homomorphism
H^{1}(X,\Theta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})\to T^{1}({{\mathcal{X}}}/A).
This will be an isomorphism if
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
has no non-trivial automorphisms that, restricted to
X,
become the identity.

Let
k\geqslant 1
be an integer,
X_{k}/A_{k}
a deformation of
X,
and
X_{k-1}/A_{k-1}
the deformation of
X
defined by
X_{k-1}:=\spec A_{k-1}\times_{\spec A_{k}}X_{k}.
There is then a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(X,\Theta_{X_{k}/A_{k}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{1}(X_{k}/A_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(X,\Theta_{X_{k-1}/A_{k-1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{1}(X_{k-1}/A_{k-1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

(8.3)

where the horizontal maps are those introduced above and the vertical maps those induced by
\pi_{k}:A_{k}\to A_{k-1}.
Suppose now that the left vertical map
\alpha
is surjective. Since the bottom horizontal map
\beta
is surjective as mentioned above it follows then that
\beta\circ\alpha
is surjective. Accordingly, so is
\gamma.
The functor
D
thus satisfies the hypothesis of Theorem
8.6
; and consequently,
D(\pi_{1}),D(\pi_{2}),D(\pi_{3}),\dots
are surjective. This will imply that the Kuranishi space, which exists by
[
20
, Corollary 0.3]
, is non-singular.

As in
[
29
]
we cannot expect that our deformation functors are always unobstructed. Following
[
45
, Theorem 2.2]
therefore we make

Definition 8.16
.

Let
X
be a compact reduced complex space whose singularities are isolated. For
x\in X^{\rm sing}
denote by
D_{x}:({\rm Art})_{{\mathbb{C}}}\to({\rm sets})
the deformation functor which assigns to every Artin local
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebra
A
the set of isomorphism classes of deformations over
A
of the germ
(X,x).
It is known that
D_{x}
has an obstruction space
T^{2}(X):=\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{2}_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,x}}(L_{X,x},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,x})
where
L_{X,x}\in D^{-}(\Mod{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,x})
is the cotangent complex of
(X,x).
Define
D_{\rm loc}:({\rm Art})_{{\mathbb{C}}}\to({\rm sets})
by
D_{\rm loc}(A):=\prod_{x\in X^{\rm sing}}D_{x}(A)
for
A
an object of
({\rm Art})_{{\mathbb{C}}}.
Put
T^{2}_{\rm loc}(X):=\bigoplus_{x\in X^{\rm sing}}T^{2}(X,x).
Notice that for each
k=0,1,2,\dots
the map
\pi_{k+1}:A_{k+1}\to A_{k}
is a small extension homomorphism in
({\rm Art})_{{\mathbb{C}}},
with kernel the principal ideal
(t^{k+1})\subseteq A_{k+1}.
We define then a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(A_{k+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(A_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}(X)\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}(t^{k+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\rm loc}(A_{k+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\rm loc}(A_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}_{\rm loc}(X)\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}(t^{k+1}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(\pi_{k+1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\rm loc}(\pi_{k+1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\rm loc}}

(8.4)

where
\alpha
is the obstruction map of
(T^{2}(X),\pi_{k+1})
and
\alpha_{\rm loc}
that of
(T^{2}_{\rm loc}(X),\pi_{k+1}).
Define the leftmost vertical map
D(A_{k+1})\to D_{\rm loc}(A_{k})
by taking an element of
D(A_{k+1}),
representing it by a deformation
X_{k}/A_{k}
of
X,
taking the germ at
X^{\rm sing}
of
X_{k}/A_{k},
and taking its isomorphism class (which is independent of the choice of the representative
X_{k}/A_{k}
). Define in the same way the middle vertical map
D(A_{k})\to D_{\rm loc}(A_{k}).
We define now the rightmost vertical map
\beta:T^{2}(X)\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}(\epsilon)\to T^{2}_{\rm loc}(X)\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}\epsilon.
Denote by
f:X^{\rm sing}\to X
the inclusion map and notice that there is a natural isomorphism
T^{2}_{\rm loc}(X)\cong\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{2}_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}}(L_{X},f_{*}f^{*}{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}).
The natural map
{\rm id}\to f_{*}f^{*}
induces therefore a map
\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{2}_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}}(L_{X},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})\to\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{2}_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}}(L_{X},f_{*}f^{*}{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}).
But the domain
\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{2}_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}}(L_{X},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})
of the latter map is exactly
T^{2}(X)
and hence we get a map
T^{2}(X)\to T^{2}_{\rm loc}(X).
Tensoring this with
(\epsilon)
we get a map
T^{2}(X)\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}(\epsilon)\to T^{2}_{\rm loc}(X)\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}\epsilon
which we call
\beta.

We say that the obstruction to deforming
X

concentrates upon
its singularities if for each
k=0,1,2,\dots
the map
\beta|_{\mathop{\rm im}\alpha}:\mathop{\rm im}\alpha\to T^{2}_{\rm loc}(X)\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}(\epsilon)
is injective.

Remark 8.17
.

Suppose that the obstruction to deforming
X
concentrates upon its singularities and that every
(X,x)
has unobstructed deformations. We show then that the whole
X
has unobstructed deformations. Let
k\geqslant 0
be an integer and take any element
\xi\in D(A_{k}).
As the deformations of each
(X,x)
are unobstructed, in (
8.4
) the left bottom horizontal map
D_{\rm loc}(\pi_{k}):D_{\rm loc}(A_{k+1})\to D_{\rm loc}(A_{k})
is surjective. In particular, the composite map
D(A_{k})\to D_{\rm loc}(A_{k})\to T^{2}_{\rm loc}(X)\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}(t^{k+1})
maps
\xi
to zero. But by Definition
8.16
the map
\beta|_{\mathop{\rm im}\alpha}
is injective, so in (
8.4
) the right top horizontal map
D(A_{k})\to T^{2}(X)\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}(t^{k+1})
maps
\xi
to zero. Thus
\xi
may be lifted to
D(A_{k+1})
in (
8.4
). The map
D(\pi_{k+1}):D(A_{k+1})\to D(A_{k})
is therefore surjective. As this holds for every
k=0,1,2,\dots
the deformations of
X
are unobstructed.

The condition in Definition
8.16
is rather hard to verify as it is. Following
[
28
, Theorem 2.2]
therefore we make

Definition 8.18
.

Let
X
be a compact reduced complex space whose singularities are Cohen–Macaulay. Let
k\geqslant 1
be an integer,
X_{k}/A_{k}
a deformation of
X,
and
X_{k-1}/A_{k-1}
the deformation of
X
defined by
X_{k-1}:=\spec A_{k-1}\times_{\spec A_{k}}X_{k}.
Suppose that if we denote by
\iota:X^{{\rm reg}}\to X
the inclusion of the regular locus then

\iota_{*}\Omega^{n}_{X_{k}/A_{k}}
is a rank-one free
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X_{k}}
module.

(8.5)

Consider the deformation functor of Example
8.13
and its
T^{1}
modules. We define then an
A_{k}
module exact sequence

T^{1}(X_{k}/A_{k})\to T^{1}(X_{k-1}/A_{k-1})\to\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{2}_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}}(\Omega^{1}_{X},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}).

(8.6)

Consider the
A_{k}
module short exact sequence
0\to A_{k-1}\to A_{k}\to{{\mathbb{C}}}\to 0
where the first arrow is the multiplication by
t
modulo ideals and the second arrow the natural projection. Tensoring these with the sheaf
\Omega^{1}_{X_{k}/A_{k}}
we get an exact sequence
\Omega^{1}_{X_{k-1}/A_{k-1}}\to\Omega^{1}_{X_{k}/A_{k}}\to\Omega^{1}_{X}\to 0.
As
\Omega^{1}_{X_{k}/A_{k}}
is flat over
X^{{\rm reg}}
the kernel of the first arrow, which we call
{\tau},
is supported on
X^{\rm sing}.
Since
X^{\rm sing}
has dimension
\leqslant\penalty n-2
it follows that
H^{n-1}(X,\mathop{\rm ker}{\tau})=H^{n}(X,\mathop{\rm ker}{\tau})=0
and hence that the natural map
H^{n-1}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X_{k-1}/A_{k-1}})\to H^{n-1}(X,\mathop{\rm im}{\tau})
is an isomorphism. Using this we get an
A_{k}
module exact sequence

H^{n-2}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X})\to H^{n-1}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X_{k-1}/A_{k-1}})\to H^{n-1}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X_{k}/A_{k}}).

(8.7)

Since
A_{k}
is a Gorenstein ring and an injective
A_{k}
module it follows that the functor
\hom_{A_{k}}({\bullet},A_{k})
is exact; and in particular, taking the dual of (
8.7
) we get an exact sequence

\begin{split}\hom_{A_{k}}(H^{n-1}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X_{k}/A_{k}}),A_{k})\to\hom_{A_{k}}(H^{n-1}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X_{k-1}/A_{k-1}}),A_{k})\\
\to\hom_{A_{k}}(H^{n-2}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X}),A_{k}).\end{split}

(8.8)

Note now that for
M
an
A_{j}
module with
j<k
there is a natural isomorphism
\hom_{A_{k}}(M,A_{k})\cong\hom_{A_{j}}(M,A_{j}).
The sequence (
8.8
) may then be re-written as

\begin{split}\!\!\!\!\!\hom_{A_{k}}(H^{n-1}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X_{k}/A_{k}}),A_{k})\to\hom_{A_{k-1}}(H^{n-1}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X_{k-1}/A_{k-1}}),A_{k-1})\\
\to\hom_{{\mathbb{C}}}(H^{n-2}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X}),{{\mathbb{C}}}).\end{split}

(8.9)

The condition (
8.5
) implies now that the relative canonical sheaves of
X_{0},\dots,X_{k}
are all free of rank one. The three
A_{k}
modules of (
8.9
) are then isomorphic by Serre duality to those three of (
8.6
). Using this we define the arrows of (
8.6
) to be those of (
8.9
).

Suppose now that
X^{\rm sing}
is isolated. For
x\in X^{\rm sing},
if we denote by
f:\{x\}\to X
the inclusion map then using the natural map
{\rm id}\to f_{*}f^{*}
we get for
k=0,1,2,\dots
a map
\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{1}_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X_{k},x}}(\Omega^{1}_{X_{k}/A_{k}},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X_{k}})\to\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{1}_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X_{k},x}}((\Omega^{1}_{X_{k}/A_{k}})_{x},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X_{k},x})
or equivalently a map
T^{1}(X_{k}/S_{k})\to T^{1}_{\rm loc}(X_{k}/A_{k})
where the latter denotes the
T^{1}
module for the deformation functor
D_{\rm loc}.
There is also a map from
\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{2}_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X_{k},x}}(\Omega^{1}_{X_{k}/A_{k}},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X_{k}})
to
\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{2}_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X_{k},x}}((\Omega^{1}_{X_{k}/A_{k}})_{x},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X_{k},x}).
There is now a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!T^{1}(X_{k}/A_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{1}(X_{k-1}/A_{k-1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{2}_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}}(\Omega^{1}_{X},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!T_{\rm loc}^{1}(X_{k}/A_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{\rm loc}^{1}(X_{k-1}/A_{k-1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\!\!\displaystyle\bigoplus_{x\in X^{\rm sing}}\!\!\!\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{2}_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,x}}(\Omega^{1}_{X,x},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,x}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}

(8.10)

The following is a more complex version of
T^{1}
lift theorems. Although this is known to experts, we give it a proof for the sake of clarity; in
[
45
, Theorem 2.2]
, for instance, the result is stated without proof.

Lemma 8.19
.

Let
X
be a compact reduced complex space whose singularities are Cohen–Macaulay and isolated; the latter implies that Definition
8.16
makes sense. Let (
8.5
) hold so that Definition
8.18
makes sense, and suppose that in (
8.10
) the map
\delta|_{\mathop{\rm im}\gamma}:\mathop{\rm im}\gamma\to\bigoplus_{x\in X^{\rm sing}}\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{2}_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,x}}(\Omega^{1}_{X,x},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,x})
is injective. The obstruction to deforming
X
then concentrates upon its singularities.

Proof.

Recall that there is a
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebra homomorphism
\theta_{k}:A_{k}\to A_{k-1}[\epsilon]
defined by
t\mapsto t+\epsilon
module ideals. Consider also the
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebra homomorphism
A_{k}[\epsilon]\to A_{k-1}[\epsilon]\times_{A_{k-1}}A_{k}
made of the projections
A_{k}[\epsilon]\to A_{k-1}[\epsilon]
and
A_{k}[\epsilon]\to A_{k}.
There is then an
A_{k+1}
module commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(t^{k+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{k+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(t^{k}\epsilon)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{k}[\epsilon]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{k-1}[\epsilon]\times_{A_{k-1}}A_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{k+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{k+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{k}\times{\rm id}}

(8.11)

whose rows are small extensions in
({\rm Art})_{{\mathbb{C}}}.
The leftmost vertical map
(t^{k+1})\to(t^{k}\epsilon)
is a
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-vector space isomorphism which map
t^{k+1}
to
(k+1)t^{k}\epsilon.
By the defining property of the obstruction spaces there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(A_{k+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(A_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}(X)\otimes(t^{k+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(A_{k}[\epsilon])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(A_{k}\times_{A_{k-1}}A_{k-1}[\epsilon])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}(X)\otimes(t^{k}\epsilon)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\rm loc}(A_{k}[\epsilon])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\rm loc}(A_{k}\times_{A_{k-1}}A_{k-1}[\epsilon])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}_{\rm loc}(X)\otimes(t^{k}\epsilon).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

(8.12)

Let
\xi\in D(A_{k})
be any element with
\beta\circ\alpha(\xi)=0.
Denote by
\eta\in D(A_{k}\times_{A_{k-1}}A_{k-1}[\epsilon]))
its image under
D(A_{k})\to D(A_{k}\times_{A_{k-1}}A_{k-1}[\epsilon]).
Let
X_{k}/A_{k}
represent the image of
\eta
under
D(A_{k}\times_{A_{k-1}}A_{k-1}[\epsilon])\to D(A_{k})
and let
Y_{k-1}/A_{k-1}[\epsilon]
represent the image of
\eta
under
D(A_{k}\times_{A_{k-1}}A_{k-1}[\epsilon])\to D(A_{k-1}[\epsilon]).
Then
X_{k-1}:=\spec A_{k-1}\times_{\spec A_{k}}X_{k}
is isomorphic as an
A_{k-1}
ringed space to
\spec A_{k-1}\times_{\spec A_{k-1}[\epsilon]}Y_{k-1}.
We have thus an element
[Y_{k-1}]\in T^{1}(X_{k-1}/A_{k-1})
represented by
Y_{k-1}.
There is on the other hsnd a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(A_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}(X)\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}(t^{k+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(A_{k}\times_{A_{k-1}}A_{k}[\epsilon])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}(X)\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}(t^{k}\epsilon)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\rm loc}(A_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}_{\rm loc}(X)\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}(t^{k+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\rm loc}(A_{k}\times_{A_{k-1}}A_{k}[\epsilon])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}_{\rm loc}(X)\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}(t^{k}\epsilon).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

(8.13)

Since
\beta\circ\alpha(\xi)=0
it follows that in this commutative diagram the composite map
D(A_{k})\to T^{2}_{\rm loc}(X)\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}(t^{k}\epsilon)
maps
\xi\in D(A_{k})
to
0\in T^{2}_{\rm loc}(X)\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}(t^{k}\epsilon).
Denote by
\zeta\in D_{\rm loc}(A_{k}\times_{A_{k-1}}A_{k-1}[\epsilon])
the image in (
8.13
) of
\xi\in D(A_{k}).
This appears also in (
8.12
). Since
\zeta
maps in (
8.13
) to
0\in T^{2}_{\rm loc}(X)\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}(t^{k}\epsilon)
it follows that so does
\zeta
in (
8.12
). In (
8.12
) therefore
\zeta
lifts to some element
\omega\in D_{\rm loc}(A_{k}[\epsilon]).

We look now at the commutative diagram (
8.10
). The image of
[Y_{k-1}]\in T^{1}(X_{k-1}/A_{k-1})
maps to an element of
T^{1}_{\rm loc}(X_{k-1}/A_{k-1})
which is the image of
\omega\in D_{\rm loc}(A_{k}[\epsilon]).
Thus
\delta\circ\gamma[Y_{k-1}]=0.
The current hypothesis (that of Lemma
8.19
) implies therefore
\gamma[Y_{k-1}]=0.
So
[Y_{k-1}]
lifts to some element of
T^{1}(X_{k}/A_{k}).
In (
8.12
) accordingly
\eta\in D(A_{k}\times_{A_{k-1}}A_{k-1}[\epsilon])
lifts to some element of
D(A_{k}[\epsilon]).
So
\eta
maps to zero under
D(A_{k}\times_{A_{k-1}}A_{k-1}[\epsilon])\to T^{2}(X)\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}(t^{k}\epsilon).
But the vertical map
T^{2}(X)\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}(t^{k+1})\to T^{2}(X)\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}(t^{k}\epsilon)
is an isomorphism, and
\xi\in D(A_{k})
therefore maps to
0\in T^{2}(X)\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}(t^{k+1})
as we have to prove.
∎

Remark 8.20
.

Obstruction maps are in general hard to compute as they are. On the other hand,
T^{1}
and Ext modules are less functorial but easier to compute. The effect of Lemma
8.19
is that computing Ext modules is sufficient for our current purpose. Something similar is done for instance in
[
53
, Proposition 2.6]
.

9
Relative Differential Forms

The next four definitions, Definitions
9.1
–
9.4
, are devoted to defining sheaves of holomorphic forms,
C^{\infty}
forms and real analytic forms.

Definition 9.1
.

Suppose first that
X
is a complex manifold with structure sheaf
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}.
Denote by
C^{\infty}_{X}
the sheaf on
X
of
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-valued
C^{\infty}
functions, which is therefore a
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebra sheaf. Denote by
C^{\omega}_{X}\subseteq C^{\infty}_{X}
the
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebra subsheaf on
X
made from
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-valued real analytic functions. Denote by
\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}
the
{{\mathbb{Z}}}
-graded
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}
module sheaf on
X
of holomorphic forms, and by
\Lambda^{\bullet}_{X}
the
{{\mathbb{Z}}}
-graded
C^{\infty}_{X}
module sheaf on
X
of
C^{\infty}
forms. There is also a real analytic version of
\Lambda^{\bullet}_{X}
for which however we do not introduce any particular symbol (because we shall not have to use it directly).

Both
\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}
and
\Lambda^{\bullet}_{X}
are sheaves of differential graded algebras over
{{\mathbb{C}}},
equipped with the de Rham differentials
{\rm d}_{X}:\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}\to\Omega^{{\bullet}+1}_{X}
and
{\rm d}_{X}:\Lambda^{\bullet}_{X}\to\Lambda^{{\bullet}+1}_{X},
together with the wedge product maps
\wedge:\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}\otimes_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}}\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}\to\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}
and
\wedge:\Lambda^{\bullet}_{X}\otimes_{C^{\infty}_{X}}\Lambda^{\bullet}_{X}\to\Lambda^{\bullet}_{X}.
For
p,q\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
denote by
\Lambda^{pq}_{X}
the sheaf on
X
of
C^{\infty}

(p,q)
forms so that for
r\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
we have
\Lambda^{r}_{X}=\bigoplus_{p+q=r}\Lambda^{pq}_{X}.
For
p,q\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
the differential
{\rm d}_{X}:\Lambda^{p+q}_{X}\to\Lambda^{p+q+1}_{X}
induces two
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-vector space sheaf homomorphisms
\Lambda^{pq}_{X}\to\Lambda^{p+1\,q}_{X}
and
\Lambda^{pq}_{X}\to\Lambda^{p\,q+1}_{X}
which we denote by
\partial_{X}
and
{\bar{\partial}}_{X}.
Since
{\rm d}_{X}^{2}=0
it follows that
\partial_{X}^{2}={\bar{\partial}}_{X}^{2}=\partial_{X}{\bar{\partial}}_{X}+{\bar{\partial}}_{X}\partial_{X}=0.
There are also real analytic versions of
(\Lambda^{\bullet}_{X},{\rm d}_{X},\wedge)
and
\Lambda^{{\bullet}{\bullet}}_{X}.

Suppose now that
X
is embedded as an open set in some
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n}.
Let
A
be an Artin local
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebra and define an
A
-ringed space
{{\mathcal{X}}}:=(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}})
by
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}:={{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}A.
Put
C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{X}}}:=C^{\infty}_{X}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}A
and
C^{\omega}_{{\mathcal{X}}}:=C^{\omega}_{X}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}A,
which are also
A
-algebra sheaves on
X.
There is on
X
a
{{\mathbb{Z}}}
-graded
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
module sheaf
\Omega^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
defined by
\Omega^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:=\Omega^{p}_{X}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}A
for
p\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}.
There is on
X
a
{{\mathbb{Z}}}
-graded
C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
module sheaf
\Lambda^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
defined by
\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:=\Lambda^{p}_{X}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}A
for
p\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}.
Define a degree-one
A
-module sheaf homomorphism
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:\Omega^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Omega^{{\bullet}+1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
by
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:={\rm d}_{X}\otimes{\rm id}_{A}.
Define by the same formula a degree-one
A
-module sheaf homomorphism
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:\Lambda^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Lambda^{{\bullet}+1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}.
In either case
({\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})^{2}=0;
that is,
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
is a differential. There are also for
p,q\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
an
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
module homomorphism
\wedge:\Omega^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\otimes_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}}\Omega^{q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Omega^{p+q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
and a
C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
module homomorphism
\wedge:\Omega^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\otimes_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}}\Omega^{q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Omega^{p+q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}.
The triples
(\Omega^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A},{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A},\wedge)
and
(\Lambda^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A},{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A},\wedge)
are both differential graded
A
-algebra sheaves. For
p,q\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
put
\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:=\Lambda^{pq}_{X}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}A
so that for
r\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
we have
\Lambda^{r}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}=\bigoplus_{p+q=r}\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}.
For
p,q\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
the differential
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:\Lambda^{p+q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Lambda^{p+q+1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
induces two
A
-module sheaf homomorphisms
\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Lambda^{p+1\,q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
and
{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Lambda^{p\,q+1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
which we denote by
\partial_{X}
and
{\bar{\partial}}_{X}.
Since
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{2}=0
it follows that
\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{2}={\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{2}=\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}+{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}=0.
There are also real analytic versions of
(\Lambda^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A},{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A},\wedge)
and
\Lambda^{{\bullet}{\bullet}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}.

We define next the model sheaf of holomorphic forms.

Definition 9.2
.

Let
X
be a complex space embedded in an open set
Y\subseteq{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n},
and
{{\mathcal{X}}}
an
A
-ringed space embedded in
{{\mathcal{Y}}}:=Y\times\spec A
by an ideal sheaf
{{\mathcal{I}}}\subseteq{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}};
that is, if we denote by
{{\mathcal{Q}}}
the quotient sheaf of
{{\mathcal{I}}}\subseteq{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}
then
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}:={{\mathcal{Q}}}|_{X}.
We define on
X
a
{{\mathbb{Z}}}
-graded
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
module sheaf
\Omega^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
equipped with a degree-one
A
-module sheaf homomorphism
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:\Omega^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Omega^{{\bullet}+1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
such that
({\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})^{2}=0.
We do this by an induction on
p.
For
p<0
set
\Omega^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}=0
and the differential
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:\Omega^{p-1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Omega^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
must vanish. For
p\geqslant 0
consider the
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}
submodule sheaf
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/A}{{\mathcal{I}}}\wedge\Omega^{p-1}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/A}+{{\mathcal{I}}}\Omega^{p}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/A}\subseteq\Omega^{p}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/A}
whose quotient sheaf we denote by
{{\mathcal{Q}}}^{p}.
Set
\Omega^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:={{\mathcal{Q}}}^{p}|_{X}.
The differential
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/A}:\Omega^{p-1}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/A}\to\Omega^{p}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/A}
induces then an
A
-module sheaf homomorphism
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:\Omega^{p-1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Omega^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
with
({\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})^{2}=0.
Now for
p,q\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
the wedge product map
\wedge:\Omega^{p}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/A}\otimes_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}}\Omega^{q}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/A}\to\Omega^{p+q}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/A}
induces an
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
module homomorphism
\wedge:\Omega^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\otimes_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}}\Omega^{q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Omega^{p+q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
which satisfies the Leibniz rule with respect to
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}.
The triple
(\Omega^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A},{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A},\wedge)
is thus a sheaf of differential graded
A
-algebras.

We define also the model sheaf of
C^{\infty}
forms.

Definition 9.3
.

Let
A
be an Artin local
{{\mathbb{R}}}
-algebra and recall from Lemma
8.3
that
B:=A\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}{{\mathbb{C}}}
is an Artin local
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebra. Let
X
be a complex space embedded in an open set
Y\subseteq{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n},
and
{{\mathcal{X}}}
a
B
-ringed space embedded in
{{\mathcal{Y}}}:=Y\times\spec B
by an ideal sheaf
{{\mathcal{I}}}\subseteq{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}.
Put
\Lambda^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/A}:=\Lambda^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}=\Lambda^{\bullet}_{Y}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}B,
which we identify naturally with
\Lambda^{\bullet}_{X}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}A.
The complex conjugate map
\Lambda^{\bullet}_{Y}\to\Lambda^{\bullet}_{Y}
and the identity map
A\to A
induce then an
{{\mathbb{R}}}
-algebra sheaf homomorphism
\Lambda^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/A}\to\Lambda^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/A}
which we call the
complex conjugate map.
Denote by
\overline{{{\mathcal{I}}}}
the image under this of
{{\mathcal{I}}}\subseteq{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}\subseteq C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}=\Lambda^{0}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/A}.
There is then an ideal sheaf
{{\mathcal{J}}}:={{\mathcal{I}}}+\overline{{{\mathcal{I}}}}\subseteq C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}
whose quotient we denote by
{{\mathcal{Q}}}.
The restriction
C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{X}}}:={{\mathcal{Q}}}|_{X}
defines on
X
a
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebra sheaf.

We define on
X
a
{{\mathbb{Z}}}
-graded
C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
module sheaf
\Lambda^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
equipped with a degree-one
B
-module sheaf homomorphism
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:\Lambda^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/B}\to\Lambda^{{\bullet}+1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/B}
such that
({\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})^{2}=0.
We do this in the same way as in Definition
9.2
with
\Lambda^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}
in place of
\Omega^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/A}
and with
{{\mathcal{J}}}
in place of
{{\mathcal{I}}}.
This produces at the same time for
p,q\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
the wedge product map
\wedge:\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\otimes_{C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{X}}}}\Lambda^{q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Lambda^{p+q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
is defined in the same way. The triple
(\Lambda^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A},{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A},\wedge)
is thus a sheaf of differential graded
B
-algebras.

For
p,q\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
denote by
\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
the image of
\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/A}|_{X}
under the projection
\Lambda^{p+q}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/A}|_{X}\to\Lambda^{p+q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}.
Each
\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
is then a
C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
submodule of
\Lambda^{p+q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
so that for
r\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
we have
\Lambda^{r}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}=\bigoplus_{p+q=r}\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}.
For
p,q\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
the differential
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:\Lambda^{p+q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Lambda^{p+q+1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
induces two
B
-module sheaf homomorphisms
\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Lambda^{p+1\,q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
and
{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Lambda^{p\,q+1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
which we denote by
\partial_{X}
and
{\bar{\partial}}_{X}.
Since
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{2}=0
it follows that
\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{2}={\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{2}=\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}+{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}=0.
Also for
p,q\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
the complex conjugate map
\Lambda^{p+q}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/A}\to\Lambda^{p+q}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/A}
induces an
{{\mathbb{R}}}
-algebra sheaf homomorphism
\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Lambda^{qp}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
which we call the
complex conjugate map.

There are also real analytic versions of
(\Lambda^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A},{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A},\wedge),

\Lambda^{{\bullet}{\bullet}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
and their complex conjugate maps.

We finally glue together the local models above.

Definition 9.4
.

Let
X
be a complex space,
A
an Artin local
{{\mathbb{R}}}
-algebra and
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a deformation of
X.
Put
B:=A\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}{{\mathbb{C}}}
and recall from Definition
8.12
that
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
is a deformation
{{\mathcal{X}}}/B
of
X.
Choose an open cover
X=U\cup V\cup\dots
such that each
{{\mathcal{U}}}:=(U,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}|_{U})
is embedded as a
B
-ringed space into
Y\times\spec B
for some open set
Y\subseteq{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n}.
These
U,V,\dots
exist by
[
30
, Chapter 2, Proposition 1.5]
. Applying Definition
9.2
to
{{\mathcal{U}}},{{\mathcal{V}}},\dots
we get on
U,V,\dots
the sheaves
\Omega^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{U}}}/A},\Omega^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{V}}}/A},\dots,
which we can glue together. The result is an
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
module sheaf on
X
which we denote by
\Omega^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}.
The gluing process defines also a differential and a wedge product map, which we denote by
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
and
\wedge
respectively. The triple
(\Omega^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A},{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A},\wedge)
is thus a sheaf on
X
of differential graded
A
-algebras. We define on
X
another differential graded
A
-algebra sheaf
(\Lambda^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A},{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A},\wedge)
in the same way with Definition
9.3
in place of Definition
9.2
.

For
p,q\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
define a
C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
module sheaf
\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
by gluing together the local models
\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{U}}}/A},\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{V}}}/A},\dots
corresponding to
U,V,\dots
respectively. Each
\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
is then a
C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
submodule of
\Lambda^{p+q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
so that for
r\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
we have
\Lambda^{r}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}=\bigoplus_{p+q=r}\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}.
For
p,q\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
the differential
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:\Lambda^{p+q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Lambda^{p+q+1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
induces two
B
-module sheaf homomorphisms
\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Lambda^{p+1\,q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
and
{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Lambda^{p\,q+1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
which we denote by
\partial_{X}
and
{\bar{\partial}}_{X}.
Since
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{2}=0
it follows that
\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{2}={\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{2}=\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}+{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}=0.

Also for
p,q\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
the complex conjugate maps for the local models are glued up into an
{{\mathbb{R}}}
-algebra sheaf homomorphism
\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Lambda^{qp}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
which we call the
complex conjugate map.
For
p\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
denote by
\mathop{\rm Re}\Lambda^{pp}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\subseteq\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
the subsheaf invariant under the complex conjugate map
\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}.

There are also real analytic versions of
(\Lambda^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A},{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A},\wedge)
and
\Lambda^{{\bullet}{\bullet}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}.

For
A={{\mathbb{R}}}
we write
X={{\mathcal{X}}}/{{\mathbb{R}}}
to define
(\Omega^{\bullet}_{X},{\rm d}_{X},\wedge),

(\Lambda^{\bullet}_{X},{\rm d}_{X},\wedge)
and
\Lambda^{{\bullet}{\bullet}}_{X}.
We also write
{\rm d}={\rm d}_{X},

\partial=\partial_{X}
and
{\bar{\partial}}={\bar{\partial}}_{X},
omitting the index
X.
A
C^{\infty}
function
X\to{{\mathbb{R}}}
means a section of
\mathop{\rm Re}\Lambda^{00}_{X}=\mathop{\rm Re}C^{\infty}_{X}.

Remark 9.5
.

For
A={{\mathbb{R}}}
the definitions above, Definitions
9.1
–
9.4
, are equivalent to those of
[
22
, §1.1]
. There is another way of making the same definitions, which is to use the diagonal map
X\to X\times_{\spec A}X
as in
[
5
, §1]
.

We write more explicitly the sheaves
\Omega^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
and
\Lambda^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
for
X
a complex manifold.

Remark 9.6
.

Let
X
be a complex manifold,
A
an Artin local
{{\mathbb{R}}}
-algebra and
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a deformation of
X.
Put again
B:=A\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}{{\mathbb{C}}}
and recall now from
[
19
, Theorem 3.21]
that there exists an open cover
U\cup V\cup\dots=X
such that each
(U,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}|_{U})
is isomorphic as a deformation of
U
to the trivial deformation
U\times\spec B.
The sheaf
\Omega^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/B}
is then defined by gluing together the local models
\Omega^{p}_{U}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}A,\Omega^{p}_{V}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}A,\dots
for
U,V,\dots\subseteq X.
The sheaf
\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
is defined by gluing together the local models
\Lambda^{p}_{U}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}A,\Lambda^{p}_{U}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}A,\dots
for
U,V\dots\subseteq X.

These expressions imply that we can use the ordinary Dolbealt lemma for the complex manifold
X;
that is, if
X
is of complex dimension
n
then for
p=0,1,2,\dots
the sequence

0\to\Omega^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\xrightarrow{{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}}\Lambda^{p0}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\xrightarrow{{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}}\dots\xrightarrow{{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}}\Lambda^{pn}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to 0

(9.1)

is exact. Getting rid of the first non-zero term
\Omega^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
and applying the global section functor
\Gamma
we get a complex

0\to\Gamma(\Lambda^{p0}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})\xrightarrow{{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}}\dots\xrightarrow{{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}}\Gamma(\Lambda^{pn}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})\to 0.

(9.2)

Since each
\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
is a fine sheaf (admitting partitions of unity) it follows that for
q=0,1,2,\dots
the sheaf cohomology group
H^{q}(X,\Omega^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})
is isomorphic to the
q^{\rm th}
cohomology group of (
9.2
). For
A={{\mathbb{R}}}
this reduces to the ordinary Dolbeault isomorphism.

We make now the definition of Kähler forms on infinitesimal deformations.

Definition 9.7
.

Recall that a Kähler form on a complex space
X
is an element
\omega\in\Gamma(\mathop{\rm Re}\Lambda^{11}_{X})
for which there exist an open cover
U\cup V\cup\dots=X
and a corresponding family
(\phi_{U}:U\to{{\mathbb{R}}})_{U}
of
C^{\infty}
strictly plurisubharmonic functions such that for each
U
we have
\omega|_{U}=i\partial{\bar{\partial}}\phi_{U}.

Let
A
be an Artin local
{{\mathbb{R}}}
-algebra and
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a deformation of
X.
Then a
Kähler form
on
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
is a section
\omega_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\in\Gamma(\mathop{\rm Re}\Lambda^{11}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})
for which there exist an open cover
U\cup V\cup\dots=X
and a corresponding family
(\phi_{U}\in\mathop{\rm Re}C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{X}}}(U))_{U}
such that for each
U
we have
\omega_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{U}=i\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi_{U}
and the restriction map
\mathop{\rm Re}C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{X}}}(U)\to\mathop{\rm Re}C^{\infty}_{X}(U)=C^{\infty}(U,{{\mathbb{R}}})
maps
\phi_{U}
to some strictly plurisubharmonic function.

Remark 9.8
.

Denote by
{{\mathcal{K}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
the cokernel of the map
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\to\mathop{\rm Re}C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
which maps a local section
f
to
\frac{1}{2}(f+\bar{f}).
The family
(\phi_{U}\in\mathop{\rm Re}C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{X}}}(U))
corresponding to a Kähler form on
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
is then a section of
{{\mathcal{K}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}.
Conversely, every section of
{{\mathcal{K}}}_{X}
is obtained from such a family except that the restrictions to
X
need not be strictly plurisubharmonic. Put
{{\mathcal{K}}}_{X}:={{\mathcal{K}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
when
A={{\mathbb{R}}}.

We state now the key result we shall need about Kähler forms. Notice that if
X
is a complex space then the inclusion of its constant sheaf
\underline{{{\mathbb{R}}}}
into the structure sheaf
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}
induces an
{{\mathbb{R}}}
-linear map
H^{2}(X,{{\mathbb{R}}})\to H^{2}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}).

Theorem 9.9

(Theorem 6.3 of
[
5
]
)
.

Let
X
be a Kähler space for which the map
H^{2}(X,{{\mathbb{R}}})\to H^{2}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})
is surjective. Then for every Artin local
{{\mathbb{R}}}
-algebra
A
and every deformation
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
of
X
there exist Kähler forms on
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A.

Remark 9.10
.

Bingener
[
5
]
deals not only with the infinitesimal deformations as above but also with the deformations over a complex space germ
(S,o)
of positive dimension. But we shall not have to do so for our purpose, for which the weaker statement above will do.

We give now a direct proof of Theorem
9.9
because this is simpler than the original one. Using the hypothesis and the
{{\mathbb{R}}}
-vector space sheaf isomorphism
i:{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}\to{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}
which multiplies by
i=\sqrt{-1}
we see that the inclusion
i\underline{{{\mathbb{R}}}}\to{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}
induces also a surjective map
H^{2}(X,i{{\mathbb{R}}})\to H^{2}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}).
That is, the corresponding map
H^{2}(X,({{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}/i\underline{{{\mathbb{R}}}}))\to H^{3}(X,i\underline{{{\mathbb{R}}}})
is injective. Suppose now that
0\to(\epsilon)\to A\to B\to 0
is a small extension in
({\rm Art})_{{\mathbb{R}}}.
Since the map
{{\mathbb{R}}}\cong(\epsilon)\to A
is injective it follows that so is the
{{\mathbb{R}}}
-linear map
H^{3}(X,i{{\mathbb{R}}})\to H^{3}(X,iA).
Composing this with the injection
H^{2}(X,({{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}/i\underline{{{\mathbb{R}}}}))\to H^{3}(X,i\underline{{{\mathbb{R}}}})
we see that the map
H^{2}(X,({{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}/i\underline{{{\mathbb{R}}}}))\to H^{3}(X,i\underline{A})
is injective. Using the commutative diagrams,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\!\!\!\!\!0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i\underline{{{\mathbb{R}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}/i\underline{{{\mathbb{R}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(X,({{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}/i\underline{{{\mathbb{R}}}}))}
