{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{3}(X,i{{\mathbb{R}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\!\!\!\!\!0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i\underline{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}/i\underline{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,\!\!\!}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(X,({{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}/i\underline{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{3}(X,iA)}

(9.3)

we see that the map
H^{2}(X,({{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}/i\underline{{{\mathbb{R}}}}))\to H^{2}(X,({{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}/i\underline{A}))
is also injective. Introducing now the
B
-ringed space
{{\mathcal{Y}}}:=(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\otimes_{A}B)
we get a commuative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}/i\underline{{{\mathbb{R}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{\rm Re}C^{\infty}_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{K}}}_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}/i\underline{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{\rm Re}C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{X}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{K}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}/i\underline{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{\rm Re}C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{K}}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

(9.4)

Since
\mathop{\rm Re}C^{\infty}_{X}
and
\mathop{\rm Re}C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
have vanishing higher cohomology groups, we get isomorphisms
H^{1}(X,{{\mathcal{K}}}_{X})\cong H^{2}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}/if^{-1}\underline{{{\mathbb{R}}}})
and
H^{1}(X,{{\mathcal{K}}}_{X})\cong H^{2}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}/if^{-1}\underline{A}).
The map
H^{2}(X,({{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}/i\underline{{{\mathbb{R}}}}))\to H^{2}(X,({{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}/i\underline{A}))
being injective implies now the map
H^{1}(X,{{\mathcal{K}}}_{X})\to H^{1}(X,{{\mathcal{K}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}})
being injective. The map
H^{0}(X,{{\mathcal{K}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}})\to H^{0}(X,{{\mathcal{K}}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}})
is accordingly surjective. This means that every Kähler form on
{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B
extends to
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A.
The induction therefore completes the proof. ∎

There is a useful criterion for the hypothesis of Theorem
9.9
.

Theorem 9.11

(Proposition 5 of
[
47
]
)
.

Let
X
be a compact normal Kähler space whose singularities are rational. The map
H^{2}(X,{{\mathbb{R}}})\to H^{2}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})
is then surjective, so the conclusion of Theorem
9.9
holds. ∎

We make a definition we will use often in what follows.

Definition 9.12
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler conifold,
A
an Artin local
{{\mathbb{R}}}
-algebra and
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a locally trivial deformation of
X.
Then a
Kähler conifold metric on
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
is a Kähler form
\omega
on
(X^{{\rm reg}},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
such that the following holds: for every
x\in X^{\rm sing}
there exist a punctured neighbourhood
U
of
x\in X
and some Kähler cone metric
g_{x}
on
(X,x)
such that if we denote by
\omega_{x}
the Kähler form of
g_{x}
then
\omega|_{U}\in\Gamma(\Lambda^{11}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{U})
corresponds to
\omega_{x}\otimes 1\in\Gamma(\Lambda^{11}_{U})\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}A
under an isomorphism
\Lambda^{11}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{U}\cong\Lambda^{11}_{U}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}A
(which does exist for
U
small enough, as
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
is locally trivial).

Using Theorem
9.11
we generalize Corollary
4.6
as follows.

Corollary 9.13
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler conifold whose singularities are rational. Let
A
be an Artin local
{{\mathbb{R}}}
-algebra and
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a locally trivial deformation of
X.
Then there exists on
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a Kähler conifold metric.

Proof.

Using Theorem
9.11
choose an open cover
U\cup V\cup\dots=X
and a corresponding family
(p_{U},p_{V},\dots)
which define a Kähler form on
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A.
Put
B:=A\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}{{\mathbb{C}}}.
For
U
containing a singular point
x\in X^{\rm sing}
embed the
B
-ringed space
(U,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}|_{U})
into
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}\times\spec B.
Extend
p_{U}
to some open set in
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}
as a
C^{\infty}
function with values in
B.
Put
p^{\prime}:=\displaystyle{p_{U}-p_{U}(0)-\sum_{a=1}^{m}(\frac{\partial p_{U}}{\partial z_{a}}(0)z_{a}+\frac{\partial p_{U}}{\partial\bar{z}_{a}}(0)\bar{z}_{a})}.
On the other hand, let
\epsilon r_{\lambda}:U\to{{\mathbb{R}}}
be as in Lemma
4.5
. Regard this as a
B
-valued function and as a smooth function on
U\times\spec B.
Choose also a cut-off function
\psi
as in the proof of Lemma
4.5
. Define a
C^{\infty}
function
q_{U}:U\times\spec B\to{{\mathbb{R}}}
by

q_{U}:=p^{\prime}+\epsilon\phi r_{\lambda}^{2}-\psi\Bigl(\frac{r^{2}}{\delta^{2}}\Bigr)p^{\prime}

(9.5)

Then
q_{U}=p_{U}
at the points far enough from
x.
Since
\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}z_{1}=\dots=\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}z_{m}=0
and
\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\bar{z}_{1}=\dots=\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\bar{z}_{m}=0
it follows that
i\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}q_{U}=i\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}p^{\prime}=i\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}p_{U}.
We can therefore glue together
q_{U}
and the other Kähler potentials. That is, for
U
not intersecting
X^{\rm sing},
set
q_{U}:=p_{U}.
The family
(q_{U},q_{V},\dots)
defines then a section over
X^{{\rm reg}}
of the sheaf
{{\mathcal{K}}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}.
Its image under the restriction map
{{\mathcal{K}}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to{{\mathcal{K}}}_{X}
defines a Kähler conifold metric on
X^{{\rm reg}},
as in the proof of Lemma
4.5
. The family
(q_{U},q_{V},\dots)
defines thus a Kähler conifold metric on
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A.

∎

10
Tensor Calculus

We generalize several standard notions from Kähler geometry. Let
X
be a complex manifold,
A
an Artin local
{{\mathbb{R}}}
-algebra and
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a deformation of
X.
A
local coordinate system
on
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
is the data
(U;z^{1},\dots,z^{n})
where
U\subseteq X
is an open set isomorphic to an open set in
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n}
and such that there exists an
A
-algebra sheaf isomorphism
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}|_{U}\cong{{\mathcal{O}}}_{U}\times_{{\mathbb{R}}}A;
and
\zeta^{1},\dots,\zeta^{n}
are the coordinates on
U
embedded in
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n}.
For
a=1,\dots,n
we write
z^{a}:=\zeta^{a}\otimes 1
which is a section of
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}|_{U}.
So if
\phi
is a section of
\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
with
p,q\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
then we can write

\phi=\frac{1}{p!q!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1},\dots,a_{p}=1,\dots,n\\
b_{1},\dots,b_{q}=1,\dots,n\end{subarray}}\phi_{a_{1}\dots a_{p}\bar{b}_{1}\dots\bar{b}_{q}}{\rm d}z^{a_{1}}\wedge\dots\wedge{\rm d}z^{a_{p}}\wedge{\rm d}\overline{z^{b_{1}}}\wedge\dots\wedge{\rm d}\overline{z^{b_{q}}}

(10.1)

with
\phi_{a_{1}\dots a_{p}\bar{b}_{1}\dots\bar{b}_{q}}\in C^{\infty}(U,{{\mathbb{C}}})\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}A.

Suppose now that
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
is given a Kähler form
\omega.
In each local coordinate system
(U;z^{1},\dots,z^{n})
write
\omega=\frac{i}{2}\sum_{a,b=1}^{n}g_{a\bar{b}}{\rm d}z^{a}\wedge{\rm d}z^{b}
with
g_{a\bar{b}}=g_{\bar{b}a}\in C^{\infty}(U,{{\mathbb{R}}})\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}A.
Denote by
g^{a\bar{b}}=g^{\bar{b}a}
the inverse matrix of
g_{a\bar{b}},
both
n{\times}n
with entries in
A.
Define for
p,q\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
an
C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
bi-linear sheaf homomorphism
g_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\times\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
by saying that if
\phi,\psi\in\Gamma(\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})
then

g_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}(\phi,\psi):=\sum g^{a_{1}\bar{c}_{1}}\dots g^{a_{p}\bar{c}_{p}}g^{\bar{b}_{1}d_{1}}\dots g^{\bar{b}_{q}d_{q}}\phi_{a_{1}\dots a_{p}\bar{b}_{1}\dots\bar{b}_{q}}\overline{\psi}_{\bar{c}_{1}\dots\bar{c}_{p}d_{1}\dots d_{q}}

(10.2)

where
\sum
is over
a_{1},\dots,a_{p};b_{1},\dots,b_{q};c_{1},\dots,c_{p};d_{1},\dots,d_{q}=1,\dots,n.
Since
\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
is a locally free
C^{\infty}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}}
module and admits partitions of unity it follows that (
10.2
) for
\phi,\psi\in\Gamma(\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})
determines the sheaf homomorphism
g_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}.

The same computation as for ordinary Kähler manifolds shows that there exists a unique
A
-module sheaf homomorphism
\nabla_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:\Lambda^{10}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Lambda^{1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\otimes_{C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{X}}}}\Lambda^{10}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
with the following properties.

(i)

If
\phi\in\Gamma(\Lambda^{10}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})
and
f\in\Gamma(C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{X}}})
then
\nabla_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}(f\phi)={\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}f\otimes\phi+f\nabla_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi.

(ii)

If
\phi,\psi\in\Gamma(\Lambda^{10}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})
then
{\rm d}[g_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}(\phi,\psi)]=g_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}(\nabla_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi,\psi)+g_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}(\phi,\nabla_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\psi).

(iii)

Using
\Lambda^{1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}=\Lambda^{10}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\oplus\Lambda^{01}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
define the projections
\Lambda^{1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Lambda^{01}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
and
\Lambda^{1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\otimes_{C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{X}}}}\Lambda^{10}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Lambda^{01}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\otimes_{C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{X}}}}\Lambda^{10}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}.
The composite of the latter with
\nabla_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
is then equal to
{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}.

The properties (i)–(iii) imply also that we can write
\nabla_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
more explicitly in each local coordinate system
(U;z^{1},\dots,z^{n}).
For
a,b,c=1,\dots,n
put

\Gamma^{c}_{ab}:=\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}g^{c\bar{k}}\frac{\partial g_{a\bar{k}}}{\partial z^{b}}\Bigl(=-\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{\partial g^{c\bar{k}}}{\partial z^{b}}g_{a\bar{k}}\Bigr).

(10.3)

For
\phi\in\Lambda^{10}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}(U)
put
\displaystyle\nabla_{a}\phi_{b}=\frac{\partial\phi_{b}}{\partial z^{a}}-\sum_{c=1}^{n}\Gamma^{c}_{ab}\phi_{c}.
Then

\nabla_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi=:\sum_{a,b=1}^{n}\nabla_{a}\phi_{b}{\rm d}z^{a}\otimes{\rm d}z^{b}+\sum_{a,b=1}^{n}{\bar{\partial}}\phi_{b}\otimes{\rm d}z^{b}.

(10.4)

This is the Levi-Civita connection in the following sense. Making
U
smaller if we need, we can suppose that there exists
f\in C^{\infty}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}}(U)
with
\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial z^{a}\partial z^{\bar{b}}}=g_{a\bar{b}}.
This implies
\Gamma^{c}_{ab}=\Gamma^{c}_{ba}.

There exists also an
A
-module sheaf homomorphism
\nabla_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:\Lambda^{01}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Lambda^{1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\otimes_{C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{X}}}}\Lambda^{01}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
characterized by the same conditions (i)—(iii) with
\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
in place of
{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
at the end of (iii). The generalized Christoffel symbols are defined by
\Gamma^{\bar{c}}_{\bar{a}\bar{b}}:=\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}g^{\bar{c}k}\frac{\partial g_{k\bar{a}}}{\partial z^{\bar{b}}}
which is also equal to the complex conjugate
\overline{\Gamma^{c}_{ab}}.

For
p,q\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
extend
\nabla_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
to an operator
\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Lambda^{1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\otimes_{C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{X}}}}\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
by the Leibniz rule. In the local coordinate expression, for
c=1,\dots,n
define
\nabla_{c},\nabla_{\bar{c}}:\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}(U)\to\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}(U)
by

\begin{split}\!\!\!\nabla_{c}\phi_{a_{1}\dots a_{p}\bar{b}_{1}\dots\bar{b}_{q}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{c}}\phi_{a_{1}\dots a_{p}\bar{b}_{1}\dots\bar{b}_{q}}-\sum_{j=1}^{p}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\Gamma^{k}_{ca_{j}}\phi_{a_{1}\dots a_{j-1}ka_{j+1}\dots a_{p}\bar{b}_{1}\dots\bar{b}_{q}},\\
\!\!\!\nabla_{\bar{c}}\phi_{a_{1}\dots a_{p}\bar{b}_{1}\dots\bar{b}_{q}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{\bar{c}}}\phi_{a_{1}\dots a_{p}\bar{b}_{1}\dots\bar{b}_{q}}-\sum_{j=1}^{q}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\Gamma^{\bar{k}}_{\bar{c}\bar{b}_{j}}\phi_{a_{1}\dots a_{p}\bar{b}_{1}\dots\bar{b}_{j-1}\bar{k}\bar{b}_{j+1}\dots\bar{b}_{q}}.\end{split}

(10.5)

These are then the components of
\nabla_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi
for
{\rm d}z^{c}\otimes{\rm d}z^{a_{1}}\wedge\dots\wedge{\rm d}z^{a_{p}}\wedge{\rm d}z^{\bar{b}_{1}}\wedge\dots\wedge{\rm d}z^{\bar{b}_{q}}
and
{\rm d}z^{\bar{c}}\otimes{\rm d}z^{a_{1}}\wedge\dots\wedge{\rm d}z^{a_{p}}\wedge{\rm d}z^{\bar{b}_{1}}\wedge\dots\wedge{\rm d}z^{\bar{b}_{q}}
respectively.

Define now an
A
-module sheaf homomorphism
\bar{\partial}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Lambda^{p-1\,q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
by

(\bar{\partial}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}\phi)_{a_{1}\dots a_{p}b_{1}\dots b_{q-1}}:=(-1)^{p-1}\sum_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{n}g^{\bar{\beta}\alpha}\nabla_{\alpha}\phi_{a_{1}\dots a_{p}\bar{\beta}\bar{b}_{1}\dots\bar{b}_{q-1}}

(10.6)

in the local coordinate expression. Define an
A
-module sheaf homomorphism
\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
by

\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:=2(\bar{\partial}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\bar{\partial}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}+\bar{\partial}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\bar{\partial}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}).

(10.7)

This is the obvious generalization of the Laplacian. The key properties we shall need are the following.

Notice that the Kähler form on
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
induces a Kähler form on
X.
Denote by
\Delta_{X}:\Lambda^{pq}_{X}\to\Lambda^{pq}_{X}
the Laplacian with respect to the induced Kähler form on
X.
On the other hand, there is a restriction map
\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Lambda^{pq}_{X}.
The diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{pq}_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{pq}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{X}}

(10.8)

then commutes.

As
\nabla_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
is the Levi-Civita connection in the sense above we can compute
\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
and
\bar{\partial}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
in terms of
\nabla_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A},
as we do for ordinary Kähler manifolds; that is, if
\phi\in\Gamma(\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})
then in the local coordinate expression we have

\begin{split}(\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi)_{a_{1}\dots a_{p+1}\bar{b}_{1}\dots\bar{b}_{q}}&=\sum_{j=1}^{p+1}(-1)^{j-1}\nabla_{a_{j}}\phi_{a_{1}\dots\hat{a}_{j}\dots a_{p+1}\bar{b}_{1}\dots\bar{b}_{q}},\\
(\bar{\partial}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi)_{a_{1}\dots a_{p}\bar{b}_{1}\dots\bar{b}_{q+1}}&=\sum_{j=1}^{q+1}(-1)^{p+j-1}\nabla_{\bar{b}_{j}}\phi_{a_{1}\dots a_{p}\bar{b}_{1}\dots\hat{b}_{j}\dots\bar{b}_{q+1}}.\end{split}

(10.9)

The latter implies readily that
\bar{\partial}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Lambda^{p\,q-1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
is the formal adjoint of
\bar{\partial}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:\Lambda^{p-1\,q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
with respect to the measure
\omega^{n}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A};
that is, for every section
\phi\in\Gamma(\Lambda^{p-1\,q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})
and every compactly supported section
\psi\in\Gamma_{c}(\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})
we have

\int_{X}g_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}({\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}\phi,\psi)\omega^{n}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}=\int_{X}g_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}(\phi,\bar{\partial}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\psi)\omega^{n}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}.

(10.10)

In the same way, define an
A
-module sheaf homomorphism
\partial^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Lambda^{p-1\,q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
by

(\partial^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi)_{a_{1}\dots a_{p-1}\bar{b}_{1}\dots\bar{b}_{q}}:=-\sum_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{n}g^{\alpha\bar{\beta}}\nabla_{\bar{\beta}}\phi_{\alpha a_{1}\dots\dots a_{p-1}\bar{b}_{1}\dots\bar{b}_{q}}.

(10.11)

This is then the formal adjoint of
\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}.

Put
\overline{\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}}:=\partial^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}+\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\partial^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
and
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}=\partial^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}+\bar{\partial}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}.
Generalizing the standard computation for Kähler manifolds, we prove that

\square:={\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}+{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}{\rm d}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}=2\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}=2\overline{\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}}.

(10.12)

Proof of (
10.12
).

Define a
C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
module homomorphism
\omega_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\wedge:\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Lambda^{p+1\,q+1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
to be the left multiplication by
\omega_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}.
Define a
C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
module homomorphism
\Lambda:\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Lambda^{p-1\,q-1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
to be the pointwise adjoint of
\omega_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\wedge;
or equivalently, in the local coordinate expression, if
\phi
is a section of
\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
then set

(\Lambda\phi)_{a_{1}\dots a_{p-1}\bar{b}_{1}\dots\bar{b}_{q-1}}:=i(-1)^{p}\sum_{a,b=1}^{n}g^{\bar{b}a}\phi_{aa_{1}\dots a_{p-1}\bar{b}\bar{b}_{1}\dots\bar{b}_{q-1}}.

(10.13)

We show that
[\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A},\Lambda]=-i\bar{\partial}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
as
A
-module sheaf homomorphisms from
\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
to
\Lambda^{p\,q-1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}.
If
\phi
is a local section of
\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
then

\begin{split}(\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\Lambda\phi)_{a_{1}\dots a_{p}\bar{b}_{1}\dots\bar{b}_{q-1}}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}(-1)^{j-1}\nabla_{a_{j}}(\Lambda\phi)_{a_{1}\dots\hat{a}_{j}\dots a_{p}\bar{b}_{1}\dots\bar{b}_{q-1}}\\
=i(-1)^{p}\sum_{a,b=1}^{n}g^{\bar{b}a}\sum_{j=1}^{p}(-1)^{j-1}\nabla_{a_{j}}\phi_{aa_{1}\dots\hat{a}_{j}\dots a_{p}\bar{b}\bar{b}_{1}\dots\bar{b}_{q-1}}.\end{split}

(10.14)

On the other hand,

\displaystyle(\Lambda\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi)_{a_{1}\dots a_{p}\bar{b}_{1}\dots\bar{b}_{q-1}}=i(-1)^{p-1}\sum_{a,b=1}^{n}g^{\bar{b}a}(\partial\phi)_{aa_{1}\dots a_{p}\bar{b}\bar{b}_{1}\dots\bar{b}_{q-1}}

\displaystyle=i(-1)^{p-1}\sum_{a,b=1}^{n}g^{\bar{b}a}(\nabla_{a}\phi_{a_{1}\dots a_{p}\bar{b}\bar{b}_{1}\dots\bar{b}_{q-1}}+(-1)^{j}\nabla_{a_{j}}\phi_{aa_{1}\dots\hat{a}_{j}\dots a_{p}\bar{b}\bar{b}_{1}\dots\bar{b}_{q-1}}).

This with (
10.14
) implies

(\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\Lambda\phi-\Lambda\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi)_{a_{1}\dots a_{p}\bar{b}_{1}\dots\bar{b}_{q-1}}=-i(-1)^{p-1}\sum_{a,b=1}^{n}g^{\bar{b}a}\nabla_{a}\phi_{a_{1}\dots a_{p}\bar{b}\bar{b}_{1}\dots\bar{b}_{q-1}}

which is equal to
-i(\bar{\partial}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi)_{a_{1}\dots a_{p}\bar{b}_{1}\dots\bar{b}_{q-1}}
as claimed. We compute now
\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:=\bar{\partial}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\bar{\partial}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}+\bar{\partial}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\bar{\partial}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}.
The identity
[\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A},\Lambda]=-i\bar{\partial}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
implies

\begin{split}-i\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}&=\bar{\partial}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}[\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A},\Lambda]+[\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A},\Lambda]\bar{\partial}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\\
&=\bar{\partial}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\Lambda-\bar{\partial}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\Lambda\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}+\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\Lambda\bar{\partial}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}-\Lambda\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\bar{\partial}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}.\end{split}

(10.15)

Since
\Lambda
is a real operator it follows also that
-i\partial^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}=-[\bar{\partial}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A},\Lambda]
and hence that

\begin{split}-i\overline{\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}}&=-\bar{\partial}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}[\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A},\Lambda]-[\bar{\partial}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A},\Lambda]\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\\
&=\bar{\partial}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\Lambda+\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\Lambda\bar{\partial}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}-\bar{\partial}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\Lambda\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}-\Lambda\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\bar{\partial}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\end{split}

(10.16)

whose right-hand side is equal to that of (
10.15
). Thus
\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}=\overline{\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}}.
On the other hand,

\begin{split}\square=(\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}+\bar{\partial}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})(\partial^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}+\bar{\partial}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})+(\partial^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}+\bar{\partial}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})(\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}+\bar{\partial}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})\\
\!\!\!\!\!\!=\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}+\overline{\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}}+(\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\bar{\partial}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}+\bar{\partial}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})+(\bar{\partial}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\partial^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}+\partial^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\bar{\partial}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}).\end{split}

(10.17)

Using again the identity
[\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A},\Lambda]=-i\bar{\partial}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
we find

-i(\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\bar{\partial}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}+\bar{\partial}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})=\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}(\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\Lambda-\Lambda\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})+(\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\Lambda-\Lambda\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}=0;

that is, the second last term of (
10.17
) vanishes. Taking the complex conjugates we see also that the last term of (
10.17
) vanishes. The equation (
10.17
) implies therefore
\square=\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}+\overline{\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}}=2\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A},
proving (
10.12
).
∎

Also
\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
is an elliptic operator with

\displaystyle(\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi)_{a_{1}\dots a_{p}\bar{b}_{1}\dots\bar{b}_{q}}

\displaystyle=-g^{\overline{\beta}\alpha}\nabla_{\alpha}\nabla_{\overline{\beta}}\phi_{a_{1}\dots a_{p}\bar{b}_{1}\dots\bar{b}_{q}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{q}(-1)^{j-1}g^{\overline{\beta}\alpha}[\nabla_{\alpha},\nabla_{\bar{b}_{j}}]\phi_{a_{1}\dots a_{p}\overline{\beta}\bar{b}_{1}\dots\bar{b}_{j-1}\bar{b}_{j+1}\dots\bar{b}_{q}}.

The proof is similar to that for ordinary Kähler manifolds.

11
C^{\infty}
Deformations

We introduce now a notion of deforming
C^{\infty}
manifolds.

Definition 11.1
.

Let
X
be a
C^{\infty}
manifold. If
A
is an Artin local
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebra then a
deformation
over
A
of
X
is an
A
-algebra sheaf
{{\mathcal{F}}}
on
X
equipped with a
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebra sheaf isomorphism
{{\mathcal{F}}}\otimes_{A}(A/{\mathfrak{m}}A)\cong C^{\infty}_{X}
and such that

every point of
X
has an open neighbourhood
U
on which there exists an
A
-algebra sheaf isomorphism
{{\mathcal{F}}}|_{U}\cong C^{\infty}_{U}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}A.

(11.1)

The last
A
-algebra sheaf
C^{\infty}_{U}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}A
may be regarded as the sheaf on
U
of
A
-valued
C^{\infty}
functions.

Let
{{\mathcal{G}}}
be another deformation over
A
of
X.
Then an
isomorphism
from
{{\mathcal{F}}}
to
{{\mathcal{G}}}
is an
A
-algebra sheaf isomorphism
\phi:{{\mathcal{F}}}\to{{\mathcal{G}}}
such that if we denote by
\pi_{{\mathcal{F}}}:{{\mathcal{F}}}\to C^{\infty}_{X}
and
\pi_{{\mathcal{G}}}:{{\mathcal{G}}}\to C^{\infty}_{X}
the natural projections then
\pi_{{\mathcal{G}}}\circ\phi=\pi_{{\mathcal{F}}}.
We say that
{{\mathcal{F}}}
and
{{\mathcal{G}}}
are
isomorphic
if there exists an isomorphism from one to the other, which is clearly an equivalence relation. The functor
\Delta:({\rm Art})_{{\mathbb{C}}}\to({\rm sets})
assigns to each Artin local
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebra
A
the set
\Delta(A)
of isomorphism classes of deformations over
A
of
X.

The
A
-algebra sheaf
C^{\infty}_{X}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}A
is certainly a deformation over
A
of
X,
which we call the
trivial
deformation over
A
of
X.
It is clear that
\Delta({{\mathbb{C}}})
consists of a single element represented by the trivial deformation of
X.

We show that
\Delta
satisfies a condition stronger than (H1) and (H2) in Definition
8.5
.

Proposition 11.2
.

let
A,B,C
be Artin local
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebras and
A\to C,B\to C
any
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebra homomorphisms; the induced map
\Delta(A\times_{C}B)\to\Delta(A)\times_{\Delta(C)}\Delta(B)
is then bijective.

Proof.

We define explicitly the inverse map
\Delta(A)\times_{\Delta(C)}\Delta(B)\to\Delta(A\times_{C}B).
Take therefore an element of
\Delta(A)\times_{\Delta(C)}\Delta(B)
and represent it by
({{\mathcal{F}}},{{\mathcal{G}}})
where
{{\mathcal{F}}}
is a deformation over
A
of
X,
and
{{\mathcal{G}}}
a deformation over
B
of
X
such that
{{\mathcal{F}}}\otimes_{A}C\cong{{\mathcal{G}}}\otimes_{B}C.
Denote by
{{\mathcal{E}}}
the fibre product of
{{\mathcal{F}}},{{\mathcal{G}}}
over
{{\mathcal{F}}}\otimes_{A}C\cong{{\mathcal{G}}}\otimes_{B}C.
We show that
{{\mathcal{E}}}
is a deformation over
D:=A\times_{C}B.
It is clear that there is a
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebra isomorphism isomorphism
{{\mathcal{E}}}\otimes_{A}{{\mathbb{C}}}\cong{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}.
Since
{{\mathcal{F}}},{{\mathcal{G}}}
satisfy the condition (
11.1
) it follows that every point of
X
has an open neighbourhood
U
on which there exists an
A
-algebra sheaf isomorphism
{{\mathcal{F}}}|_{U}\cong C^{\infty}_{U}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}D.
So
{{\mathcal{E}}}
represents an element of
\Delta(A\times_{C}B)
and defines the inverse map we want.
∎

We show that
\Delta
satisfies a condition stronger than (H3) in Definition
8.5
.

Theorem 11.3
.

\Delta({{\mathbb{C}}}[t]/t^{2})
consists of a single element represented by the trivial deformation of
X.

Proof.

Choose an open cover
U\cup V\cup\dots=X
such that
{{\mathcal{F}}}
is made from
C^{\infty}_{U}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}A,C^{\infty}_{V}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}A,\dots
by gluing them together. For each
U
denote by
\sigma_{U}:{{\mathcal{F}}}|_{U}\to C^{\infty}_{U}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}A
the
A
-algebra sheaf isomorphism on
U;
and for each
U,V
define
\sigma_{UV}:C^{\infty}_{U\cap V}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}A\to C^{\infty}_{U\cap V}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}A
by
\sigma_{UV}:=(\sigma_{U}|_{U\cap V})\circ(\sigma_{V}^{-1}|_{U\cap V}),
which we call the
transition function
for
U,V
of
{{\mathcal{F}}}.
Define
{\tau}_{UV}:C^{\infty}_{U\cap V}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}A\to C^{\infty}_{U\cap V}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}A
by
{\tau}_{UV}:=\sigma_{UV}-{\rm id}.
Simple computation shows that this is an
A
-linear derivation. The exact sequence
0\to C^{\infty}_{U\cap V}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}(t)\to C^{\infty}_{U\cap V}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}A\to C^{\infty}_{U\cap V}\to 0
shows that
{\tau}_{UV}
may be regarded as a
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-linear derivation
C^{\infty}_{U\cap V}\to C^{\infty}_{U\cap V}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}(t).
Thus
{\tau}_{UV}
defines over
U\cap V
a section of the sheaf
{{\mathcal{D}}}_{X}:=\der_{{\mathbb{C}}}(C^{\infty}_{X},C^{\infty}_{X}).
Varying
U,V
we get a Čech
1
-cochain
{\tau}:=({\tau}_{UV})_{U,V}
of
{{\mathcal{D}}}_{X}.
Since
(\sigma_{UV})
is the family of transition functions it follows that
{\tau}
is a cocycle. On the other hand, as
{{\mathcal{D}}}_{X}
is a
C^{\infty}_{X}
module sheaf admitting partitions of unity, there exists a
0
-cocycle
(\theta_{U})
whose coboundary is equal to
{\tau}.
Define for each
U
an
A
-algebra sheaf isomorphism
\zeta_{U}:C^{\infty}_{U\cap V}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}A\to C^{\infty}_{U\cap V}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}A
by
\zeta_{U}:={\rm id}+\theta_{U}.
Then for each
U,V
we have
\zeta_{U}|_{U\cap V}\circ\sigma_{UV}=\zeta_{V}|_{U\cap V}.
Since
\sigma_{UV}
is the transition function over
U\cap V
of
{{\mathcal{F}}}
we get, varying
(U,V),
an
A
-algebra sheaf isomorphism
{{\mathcal{F}}}\cong C^{\infty}_{X}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}A.

∎

Remark 11.4
.

The sheaf
{{\mathcal{D}}}_{X}
is slightly different from the sheaf of
C^{\infty}
vector fields; see also Remark
11.7
below.

Corollary 11.5
.

For every Artin local
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebra
A
the set
\Delta(A)
consists of a single element represented by the trivial deformation of
X.

Proof.

We prove by an induction on the length of
A
that
\Delta(A)
consists of a single element. We know that this holds for
A={{\mathbb{C}}}
and suppose therefore that
A\to B
a small extension homomorphism in
({\rm Art})_{{\mathbb{C}}}
with
\Delta(B)
consisting of a single element. Recall then from (
8.1
) that the zero vector space
\Delta({{\mathbb{C}}}[t]/t^{2})
acts transitively upon the unique fibre of
\Delta(A)\to\Delta(B).
Thus
\Delta(A)
consists also of a single element, which completes the induction. It is clear that the single element of
\Delta(A)
is represented by the trivial deformation of
X.

∎

We return now to the study of a complex manifold
X
and its deformations.

Corollary 11.6
.

Let
X
be a complex manifold and let
{{\mathbb{K}}}
be
{{\mathbb{R}}}
or
{{\mathbb{C}}}.
Let
A
be an Artin local
{{\mathbb{K}}}
-algebra and
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a deformation of
X.
Then there exists a differential graded
A
-algebra sheaf isomorphism
(\Lambda^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A},{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A},\wedge)\cong(\Lambda^{\bullet}_{X}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}A,{\rm d}_{X}\otimes{\rm id}_{A},\wedge).

Proof.

Suppoes
{{\mathbb{K}}}={{\mathbb{R}}}
and put
B:=A\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}{{\mathbb{C}}}.
Recall from Definition
9.4
that
C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
is a deformation over
B
of the
C^{\infty}
manifold which underlies
X.
Applying Corollary
11.5
to this
C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
we get an
B
-algebra sheaf isomorphism
C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\cong C^{\infty}_{X}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}B\cong C^{\infty}_{X}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}A.
More explicitly, after choosing an open cover
U\cup V\cup\dots=X
we can reproduce the sheaf
C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
from the local models
C^{\infty}_{U}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}A,C^{\infty}_{V}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}A,\dots,
gluing them together under the identity functions. We can then reproduce the sheaf
\Lambda^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
from the local models
\Lambda^{\bullet}_{U}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}A,\Lambda^{\bullet}_{V}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}A,\dots,
gluing them together under the identity functions. There is thus an isomorphism
\Lambda^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\cong\Lambda^{\bullet}_{X}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}A
which is compatible with the differentials and wedge products. The case
{{\mathbb{K}}}={{\mathbb{C}}}
may be treated in the same way.
∎

Remark 11.7
.

We can prove Corollary
11.6
also by using real analytic functions, which we explain briefly now. It is easy to modify Definition
11.1
and Proposition
11.2
. The real analytic version of Theorem
11.3
will be slightly different. The stalks of real analytic functions will be Noetherian rings and the sheaf
\Theta_{X}:=\der_{{\mathbb{C}}}(C^{\omega}_{X},C^{\omega}_{X})
of derivations will agree with the locally free sheaf of real analytic vector fields. Recall now from
[
25
, p461]
that the real analytic manifold
X
is embeddable into a Stein complex space
Y
so that
X\subseteq Y
has a fundamental system of Stein open neighbourhoods. Cartan
[
11
, Théorème 1]
proves then that if
{{\mathcal{F}}}
is a coherent
C^{\omega}_{X}
module sheaf on
X
then for
p=1,2,3,\dots
we have
H^{p}(X,{{\mathcal{F}}})=0.
After we apply this to
{{\mathcal{F}}}_{X}=\Theta_{X}
and
p=1
we can follow the proof of Theorem
11.3
. It is also easy to modify Corollary
11.5
. Hence we get the real analytic version of the isomorphism
\Lambda^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\cong\Lambda^{\bullet}_{X}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}A.
Tensoring the local models with the sheaves of
C^{\infty}
functions, we come back to the same conclusion as in Corollary
11.6
.

12
Proof of Theorem
1.2

We make now the definitions we will use about linear differential operators over manifolds.

Definition 12.1
.

Let
X
be a
C^{\infty}
manifold (which need not be compact) and
E
a
C^{\infty}
complex vector bundle over
X.
Denote by
\Gamma(E)
the set of
C^{\infty}
sections of
E,
by
\Gamma_{c}(E)
the set of compactly supported
C^{\infty}
sections of
E,
by
L^{1}_{\rm loc}(E)\subseteq{{\mathcal{D}}}^{\prime}(E)
the set of locally
L^{1}
sections of
E,
and by
{{\mathcal{D}}}^{\prime}(E)
the set of distribution sections of
E.
The last means that each element of
{{\mathcal{D}}}^{\prime}(E)
is a continuous
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-linear map from
\Gamma_{c}(E)
to
{{\mathbb{C}}}
where
\Gamma_{c}(E)
is given the compact
C^{\infty}
topology.

Suppose now that
X
is given a Riemannian metric and
E
a Hermitian metric. Denote by
L^{2}(E)\subseteq L^{1}_{\rm loc}(E)
the set of
\xi\in L^{1}_{\rm loc}(E)
with
\int_{X}|\xi|^{2}{\rm d}\mu<\infty
where
|\xi|
is the pointwise norm with respect to the Hermitian metric on
E,
and
{\rm d}\mu
the volume measure of the Riemannian metric on
X.
The Cauchy–Schwarz inequality implies that for
\xi,\eta\in L^{2}(E)
the pointwise pairing
\xi\cdot\eta
relative to the Hermitian metric on
E
defines a globally
L^{1}
function
X\to{{\mathbb{C}}},
whose integral defines the inner product
(\xi,\eta)_{L^{2}}:=\int_{X}\xi\cdot\eta\,{\rm d}\mu.
It is well known that
L^{2}(E)
is a Hilbert space and
\Gamma_{c}(E)
a dense subspace of
L^{2}(E).

Let
F
be another complex vector bundle over
X,
and
P:\Gamma(E)\to\Gamma(F)
a linear differential operator (with
C^{\infty}
coefficients). Note that
P
extends to a linear operator
{{\mathcal{D}}}^{\prime}(E)\to{{\mathcal{D}}}^{\prime}(F)
which we denote by the same
P.
Denote by
\mathop{\rm ker}P\subseteq L^{2}(E)
the kernel of the operator
P
restricted to
L^{2}(E);
that is,
\mathop{\rm ker}P:=\{\xi\in L^{2}(E):P\xi=0\}.
This is a closed subspace of the Hilbert space
L^{2}(E).

Suppose now that
F
is given a Hermitian metric and define then the formal adjoint operator
P^{*}:\Gamma_{c}(F)\to\Gamma_{c}(E)
by saying that for every
\xi\in\Gamma(E)
and
\eta\in\Gamma_{c}(F)
we have
(P^{*}\eta,\xi)_{L^{2}}=(\eta,P\xi)_{L^{2}}.
This
P^{*}
is also a linear differential operator (with
C^{\infty}
coefficients and of the same order as
P
).

We make another definition we will use to prove Theorem
1.2
.

Definition 12.2
.

Let
X
be a complex manifold which is given a Kähler metric. Let
{{\mathbb{K}}}
be
{{\mathbb{R}}}
or
{{\mathbb{C}}},

A
an Artin local
{{\mathbb{K}}}
-algebra and
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a deformation of
X.
Using Corollary
11.6
choose a differential graded
A
-algebra sheaf isomorphism
(\Lambda^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A},{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})\cong(\Lambda^{\bullet}_{X}\otimes_{{\mathbb{K}}}A,{\rm d}_{X}\otimes{\rm id}_{A}).
For
p,q\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
the restriction map
\Lambda^{p+q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Lambda^{p+q}_{X}
is surjective because it is induced from the projection
A\to A/{\mathfrak{m}}A={{\mathbb{K}}}.
On the other hand, the
{{\mathbb{K}}}
-algebra homomorphism
{{\mathbb{K}}}\to A
induces a
C^{\infty}_{X}
module sheaf homomorphism

\Lambda^{p+q}_{X}=\Lambda^{p+q}_{X}\otimes_{{\mathbb{K}}}{{\mathbb{K}}}\to\Lambda^{p+q}_{X}\otimes_{{\mathbb{K}}}A\cong\Lambda^{p+q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}.

(12.1)

Since the map
{{\mathbb{K}}}\to A
splits the projection
A\to{{\mathbb{K}}}
it follows that (
12.1
) splits the restriction map
\Lambda^{p+q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Lambda^{p+q}_{X}.

Regard the
C^{\infty}_{X}
module
\Lambda^{p+q}_{X}
as a complex vector bundle and give it the Hermitian metric induced from the Kähler metric of
X.

Using the isomorphism
C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\cong C^{\infty}_{X}\otimes_{{\mathbb{K}}}A
regard the
C^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
module
\Lambda^{p+q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
as a
C^{\infty}_{X}
module with
C^{\infty}_{X}
acting trivially upon the
A
factor. The isomorphism
\Lambda^{p+q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\cong\Lambda^{p+q}_{X}\otimes_{{\mathbb{K}}}A
defines then a
C^{\infty}_{X}
module isomorphism and accordingly a vector bundle isomorphism. Here
A
is regarded as a finite-dimensional
{{\mathbb{K}}}
-vector space. We give this a metric (that is, a positive definite symmetric
{{\mathbb{K}}}
-bilinear form) which is compatible with the splitting
A={{\mathbb{K}}}\oplus{\mathfrak{m}}A.
The latter condition means that the restriction to the
{{\mathbb{K}}}
factor agrees with the product structure of
{{\mathbb{K}}}
(which makes sense because the products in
{{\mathbb{K}}}
may be regarded as inner products and accordingly as a metric). As
\Lambda^{p+q}_{X}
is given already a Hermitian metric, using the metric on
A
we get a Hermitian metric on
\Lambda^{p+q}_{X}\otimes_{{\mathbb{K}}}A.

Using these Hermitian metrics, define
L^{2}(\Lambda^{p+q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})
and
L^{2}(\Lambda^{p+q}_{X})
as in Definition
12.1
. The restriction map
\Lambda^{p+q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Lambda^{p+q}_{X}
induces then a map
L^{2}(\Lambda^{p+q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})\to L^{2}(\Lambda^{p+q}_{X})
which we denote by
R.
Since
\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\subseteq\Lambda^{p+q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
we get also the map
R:L^{2}(\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})\to L^{2}(\Lambda^{pq}_{X}).

Remark 12.3
.

No Kähler forms on
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
are relevant to Definition
12.2
.

We prove a lemma we will use shortly for the proof of Theorem
12.7
below.

Lemma 12.4
.

Let
(X,x)
be the germ of a complex space of dimension
n
and of depth
\geqslant n.
Let
A
be an Artin local
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebra and
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a deformation of
X.
Let
\phi\in\Gamma(\Lambda^{1\,n-2}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
be
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
exact on a punctured neighbourhood of
x\in X^{\rm sing}.
Then
\phi
is
{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
exact on a punctured neighbourhood of
x\in X^{\rm sing}.

Proof.

We prove by an induction on the length of
A
that
H^{n-1}_{x}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}})=0.
By the depth condition this is true for
A={{\mathbb{C}}}.
Let
0\to(\epsilon)\to A\to B\to 0
be a small extension in
({\rm Art})_{{\mathbb{C}}},
and
{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B
the deformation of
X
defined by
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}:={{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\otimes_{A}B.
The
A
-module sheaf short exact sequence
0\to{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}\to{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\to{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}\to 0
induces the long exact sequence containing
0=H^{n-1}_{x}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})\to H^{n-1}_{x}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}})\to H^{n-1}_{x}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}).
By the induction hypothesis, however,
H^{n-1}_{x}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}})
vanishes; and accordingly, so does
H^{n-1}_{x}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}).

Write
\phi={\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\psi
with
\psi\in\Gamma(\Lambda^{n-2}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).
Using the argument in the proof of Theorem
6.11
and noting that
\phi
is a
(1,n-2)
form, we can in fact write
\phi=\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\psi^{\prime}+{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\psi^{\prime\prime}
where
\psi^{\prime}
is some
(0,n-2)
form with
{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\psi^{\prime}=0,
and
\psi^{\prime\prime}
some
(1,n-3)
form. Let
U
be a Stein neighbourhood of
x\in X^{\rm sing}.
Then
H^{n-2}(U\setminus\{x\},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}})\cong H^{n-1}_{x}(U,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}})=0.
So
\psi^{\prime}={\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\chi
where
\chi
is some
(0,n-3)
form on
U\setminus\{x\}.
Thus
\phi=\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\chi+{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\psi^{\prime\prime}={\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}(-\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\chi+\psi^{\prime\prime})
is
{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
exact.
∎

We define now the topologies we will use of sheaf cohomology groups.

Definition 12.5
.

Let
X
be a complex manifold (which we suppose
\sigma
-compact). For
p,q\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
we give
\Gamma(\Lambda^{pq}_{X}),
the space of
(p,q)
forms on
X,
the Fréchet space topology whose semi-norms are the
C^{l}
norms on compact subsets of
X.
We define also a topology of
\Gamma_{c}(\Lambda^{pq}_{X}),
the
(p,q)
forms with compact support. For every compact
K\subseteq X
denote by
\Gamma_{K}(\Lambda^{pq}_{X})\subseteq\Gamma_{c}(\Lambda^{pq}_{X})
the vector subspace consisting of those
(p,q)
forms supported in
K.
We give this the Fréchet space topology whose semi-norms are the
C^{l}
norms with
l\geqslant 0
an integer. Define a fundamental neighbourhood of the origin
0\in\Gamma_{c}(\Lambda^{pq}_{X})
to be a convex subset
U\subseteq\Gamma(\Lambda^{pq}_{X})
such that for every compact
K\subseteq X
the intersection
U\cap\Gamma_{K}(\Lambda^{pq}_{X})
is an open subset of
\Gamma_{K}(\Lambda^{0q}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).
These fundamental neighbourhoods define a topology of
\Gamma_{c}(\Lambda^{pq}_{X})
with which it is a locally convex topological vector space. Notice that for every compact
K\subseteq X
the inclusion map
\Gamma_{K}(\Lambda^{pq}_{X})\to\Gamma(\Lambda^{pq}_{X})
is continuous. This implies that the inclusion map
\Gamma_{c}(\Lambda^{pq}_{X})\to\Gamma(\Lambda^{pq}_{X})
is continuous.

Write the cohomology groups
H^{q}(X,\Omega^{p}_{X}),H^{q}_{c}(X,\Omega^{p}_{X})
as those of the
{\bar{\partial}}
resolutions and give them the quotient topologies (called the strong topologies in
[
40
, Theorem 2.1]
in which it is proved that these agree with the other topologies corresponding to other expressions of the cohomology groups). The induced map
H^{q}_{c}(X,\Omega^{p}_{X})\to H^{q}(X,\Omega^{p}_{X})
is then continuous.

Remark 12.6
.

For
X
compact the statements above are obvious. For
X
non-compact, however, the cohomology groups are in general of infinite dimension and we need therefore more careful treatment.

We come now to the heart of the proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Theorem 12.7
.

Let
n\geqslant 3
be an integer and
X
a compact Kähler
n
-conifold with
X^{\rm sing}
non-empty, rational and of depth
\geqslant n.
Suppose that for each
x\in X^{\rm sing}
we have
b_{n-2}(C_{x}^{{\rm reg}})=0
or
b_{n-1}(C_{x}^{{\rm reg}})=0.
Let
A
be an Artin local
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebra and
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a deformation of
X.
The natural map
{}_{c}H^{n-2}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})\to{}_{c}H^{n-2}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{1}_{X})
is then surjective.

Proof.

Take any element of
{}_{c}H^{n-2}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{1}_{X})
and represent it on
X^{{\rm reg}},
using Lemma
7.4
, by a harmonic
(1,n-2)
form of order
1-n,
which we call
\phi_{0}.
Using the
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebra homomorphism
{{\mathbb{C}}}\to A
lift
\phi_{0}
to a section of
\Lambda^{n-1}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}A\cong\Lambda^{n-1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}
of the same order, which we call
\phi\in\Gamma(\Lambda^{n-1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).
For
p,q\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
with
p+q=n-1
denote by
\phi^{pq}\in\Gamma(\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
the
(p,q)
part of
\phi.
Since
{\rm d}\phi_{0}=0
it follows that
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi=0
and hence that
{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi^{0\,n-1}=0.
Since
\phi
is of order
>1-n
it follows that
\phi
is
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
exact on a punctured neighbourhood of
X^{\rm sing}\subseteq X,
on which therefore there exists a relative
n-2
form
\theta
of order
>2-n
and such that
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\theta=\phi.
Let
\zeta
be a relative
(0,n-1)
form on
X^{{\rm reg}}
which near
X^{\rm sing}
agrees with the
(0,n-1)
part of
\theta;
such forms may be made using partitions of unity, and any choice will do. Now
\phi^{0\,n-1}-{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\zeta
is a compactly supported
{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
closed relative
(0,n-1)
form on
X^{{\rm reg}}.

We show that
H^{n-1}_{c}(X^{{\rm reg}},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})=0.
Since
n\geqslant 3
it follows that for
U\subseteq X
a Stein neighbourhood of
X^{\rm sing},
if we write
U^{{\rm reg}}:=U\setminus X^{\rm sing}
then
H^{n-2}(U^{{\rm reg}},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})\cong H^{n-1}_{X^{\rm sing}}(U,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X});
and this vanishes because
X^{\rm sing}
is of depth
\geqslant n.
Hence we get an exact sequence
0\to H^{n-1}_{c}(X^{{\rm reg}},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})\xrightarrow{\alpha}H^{n-1}(X^{{\rm reg}},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}).
Recall from Definition
12.5
that the latter
\alpha
is continuous, which implies that the closure of the origin
0\in H^{n-1}_{c}(X^{{\rm reg}},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})
lies in the kernel of
\alpha.
But
\alpha
is injective, so the closure of the origin is a zero vector space. By
[
38
, Theorem 5.11]
therefore
H^{n-1}_{c}(X^{{\rm reg}},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})
is Hausdorff.

By
[
40
, Theorem 2.1]
(or
[
4
, Chapter IIV, Theorem 4.2]
) there exists a Serre duality isomorphism
H^{n-1}_{c}(X^{{\rm reg}},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})\cong H^{1}(X^{{\rm reg}},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}).
Since
X^{\rm sing}
is of depth
\geqslant 3
we get another isomorphism
H^{1}(X^{{\rm reg}},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})\cong H^{1}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}).
By Proposition
4.15
however
H^{1}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})=0.
So
H^{n-1}_{c}(X^{{\rm reg}},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})=0.

By an induction on the length of
A
we can verify that
H^{n-1}_{c}(X^{{\rm reg}},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}})=0.
Since
\phi^{0\,n-1}-{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\zeta
defines an element of this cohomology group it follows that
\phi^{0\,n-1}-{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\zeta={\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\eta
where
\eta
is some compactly supported relative
(0,n-2)
form on
X^{{\rm reg}}.
Thus
\phi={\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}(\eta+\zeta).
Since
\zeta
near
X^{\rm sing}
agrees with
\theta
it follows that
\zeta
is of order
>2-n
and hence that so is
\eta+\zeta.
In particular,
\eta+\zeta
is
L^{2}.
We apply to this the orthogonal decomposition

L^{2}(\Lambda^{0\,n-2}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})=\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}+{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*})\oplus\overline{{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}(\Gamma_{c}(\Lambda^{0\,n-3}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}))}\oplus\overline{{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}(\Gamma_{c}(\Lambda^{0\,n-1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}))}

(12.2)

where the formal adjoint
{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}
is defined with respect to the Hermitian metric in Definition
12.2
(and so different from the
{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}
in §
10
) and where the closures are defined in the
L^{2}
sense. Denote by
\xi\in\overline{{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}(\Gamma_{c}(\Lambda^{0\,n-1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}))}
the last of the three components of
\eta+\zeta.
Since the first two components on the right-hand side of (
12.2
) lie in
\mathop{\rm ker}{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
it follows that
{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\xi={\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}(\eta+\zeta)
and hence that
\phi^{0\,n-1}={\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\xi.
On the other hand, since the last component on the right-hand side of (
12.2
) lies in
\mathop{\rm ker}{\bar{\partial}}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
it follows that
{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}\xi=0.

We show next that if we denote by
R:L^{2}(\Lambda^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to L^{2}(\Lambda^{\bullet}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
the restriction map and if we put
\chi:=\phi^{1\,n-2}-\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\xi
then
R\chi=\phi_{0}.
We show indeed that
R\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\xi=0.
Since
R\phi=\phi_{0}
is a
(1,n-2)
form it follows that
R(\phi^{0\,n-1})=0
and hence that
{\bar{\partial}}_{X}R\xi=R{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\xi=R\phi^{0\,n-1}=0.
Also
{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}\xi=0
implies
{\bar{\partial}}_{X}^{*}R\xi=R({\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}\xi)=0.
Thus
R\xi
is an
L^{2}
harmonic
(0,n-2)
form on
X.
By Lemma
5.15
therefore we have
\partial_{X}R\xi=0
and accordingly
R\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\xi=\partial_{X}R\xi=0.
So
R\chi=R(\phi^{1\,n-2}-\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\xi)=R\phi^{1\,n-2}=\phi_{0}.

As
-{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\xi=\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi^{0\,n-1}
we have
{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\chi={\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}(\phi^{1\,n-2}-\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\xi)={\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi^{1\,n-2}+\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi^{0\,n-1}=0.
Thus
\chi
defines an element of the
{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
cohomology group
H^{n-2}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}).
We show that this lies in the subspace
{}_{c}H^{n-2}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}).
We look at
\phi-{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\xi,
which is
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
cohomologous to
\phi.
Since
\phi
is locally (near
X^{\rm sing}
)
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
exact it follows that so is
\phi-{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\xi.
Write this therefore, near
X^{\rm sing},
as
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\alpha
where
\alpha
is a relative
n-1
form defined near
X^{\rm sing}.
Since
\phi-{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\xi
has vanishing
(0,n-1)
component it follows that if we denote by
\alpha^{0\,n-2}
the
(0,n-2)
part of
\alpha
then
{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\alpha^{0\,n-2}=0.
But as
X^{\rm sing}
is of depth
\geqslant n
we have
H^{n-1}_{X^{\rm sing}}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})=0.
By an induction on the length of
A
we can verify also that
H^{n-1}_{X^{\rm sing}}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}})=0.
If
U
is a Stein neighbourhood of
X^{\rm sing}\subseteq X
and if we put
U^{{\rm reg}}:=U\setminus X^{\rm sing}
then
H^{n-2}(U^{{\rm reg}},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})=0.
Making
U
small enough we can suppose that
\alpha
is defined on
U
and can write then
\alpha^{0\,n-2}={\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\beta
where
\beta
is a relative
(0,n-3)
form on
U^{{\rm reg}}.
Since
\chi=\phi^{1\,n-2}-\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\xi
is the
(1,n-2)
part of
\phi-{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\xi
it follows now that

\chi|_{U}={\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\alpha^{1\,n-3}+\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\alpha^{0\,n-2}={\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\alpha^{1\,n-3}+\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\beta={\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}(\alpha^{1\,n-3}-\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\beta).

Thus
\chi|_{U}
is
{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
cohomologous to zero, which means that
\chi
defines an element of
{}_{c}H^{n-2}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}),
completing the proof.
∎

We show next that (
8.5
) holds in the present circumstances.

Lemma 12.8
.

Let
X
be a compact Calabi–Yau
n
-conifold with
X^{\rm sing}
non-empty. Let
{{\mathbb{K}}}
be
{{\mathbb{R}}}
or
{{\mathbb{C}}},

A
an Artin local
{{\mathbb{K}}}
-algebra and
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a deformation of
X.
Then there exists on
X^{{\rm reg}}
a nowhere-vanishing section of
\Omega^{n}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.15
we have
H^{1}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})=0.
We proceed by an induction on
A.
Let
0\to(\epsilon)\to A\to B\to 0
be a small extension in
({\rm Art})_{{\mathbb{K}}}.
Denote by
{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B
the deformation of
X
induced from
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A.
There is then an
A
-module sheaf exact sequence
0\to\iota_{*}\Omega^{n}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}\to\iota_{*}(\Omega^{n}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to\iota_{*}(\Omega^{n}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to 0.
Since
H^{1}(X,\iota_{*}\Omega^{n}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})=H^{1}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})=0
it follows that the restriction map
\Gamma(\Omega^{n}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to\Gamma(\Omega^{n}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
is surjective. Starting with a nowhere-vanishing section
\Omega
of
\Omega^{n}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}
we get, by the induction on
A,
a section
\Phi
of
\Omega^{n}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}
whose restriction to
\spec{{\mathbb{C}}}
is equal to
\Omega.
Since
\Omega
is nowhere-vanishing it follows that so is
\Phi.

∎

We prove now a corollary of Theorem
12.7
.

Corollary 12.9
.

Let
X
be a compact Calabi–Yau
n
-conifold with
n\geqslant 3
and such that for each
x\in X^{\rm sing}
we have
b_{n-2}(C_{x}^{{\rm reg}})=0
or
b_{n-1}(C_{x}^{{\rm reg}})=0.
Notice that by Lemma
12.8
the condition (
8.5
) holds so that Definition
8.18
makes sense. The map
\delta|_{\mathop{\rm im}\gamma}:\mathop{\rm im}\gamma\to\bigoplus_{x\in X^{\rm sing}}\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{2}_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,x}}(\Omega^{1}_{X,x},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,x})
is then injective.

Proof.

We show first that the following holds:

Let
q\geqslant 1
be an integer and
U\subseteq X
a Stein open neighbourhood of
X^{\rm sing}.
Then for
j=0,\dots,k
we have
H^{q}(U,\Omega^{1}_{X_{j}/A_{j}})=0.

(12.3)

We prove this by induction on
j.
For
j=0
the sheaf
\Omega^{1}_{X}
is a coherent
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}
module and so
H^{q}(U,\Omega^{1}_{X})=0.
Suppose next that
H^{q}(U,\Omega^{1}_{X_{j-1}/A_{j-1}})=0
for some
j>0.
Since the kernel of
{\tau}:\Omega^{1}_{X_{j-1}/A_{j-1}}\to\Omega^{1}_{X_{j}/A_{j}}
is supported on the isolated set
X^{\rm sing}
it follows that
H^{q}(U,\mathop{\rm ker}{\tau})=H^{q+1}(U,\mathop{\rm ker}{\tau})=0
and hence that the natural map
H^{q}(U,\Omega^{1}_{X_{j-1}/A_{j-1}})\to H^{q}(U,\mathop{\rm im}{\tau})
is an isomorphism. The induction hypothesis implies therefore that
H^{q}(U,\mathop{\rm im}{\tau})=0
and the short exact sequence
0\to\mathop{\rm im}{\tau}\to\Omega^{1}_{X_{j}/A_{j}}\to\Omega^{1}_{X}\to 0
implies in turn that
H^{q}(U,\Omega^{1}_{X_{j}/A_{j}})=0,
completing thus the induction argument.

As
n\geqslant 3
we can apply the result to
q=n-2,n-1;
that is, for
j=0,\dots,k
we have
H^{q}(U,\Omega^{1}_{X_{j}/A_{j}})=0.
Lemma
6.2
implies therefore that the image of the natural map
H^{n-2}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X_{j}/A_{j}})\to H^{n-2}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{1}_{X_{j}/A_{j}})
agrees with the image of the natural map
H_{c}^{n-2}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{1}_{X_{j}/A_{j}})\to H^{n-2}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{1}_{X_{j}/A_{j}}).
Using this with
j=0,k
we get a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!H_{X^{\rm sing}}^{n-2}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X_{k}/A_{k}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{n-2}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X_{k}/A_{k}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\!{}_{c}H^{n-2}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{1}_{X_{k}/A_{k}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!H_{X^{\rm sing}}^{n-2}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{n-2}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\!{}_{c}H^{n-2}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{1}_{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

(12.4)

whose rows are the local cohomology exact sequences and whose vertical maps are induced from the sheaf homomorphism
\Omega^{1}_{X_{k}/A_{k}}\to\Omega^{1}_{X}.
Denote by
V
the cokernel of the middle vertical arrow of (
12.4
). Since
H^{n-2}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X})
is a
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-vector space it follows that so is
V.
Denote by
\pi:H^{n-2}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X})\to V
the natural projection. Recall from Theorem
12.7
that the right vertical arrow of (
12.4
) is surjective. We see then easily by diagram chase that
\pi\circ\sigma
is surjective.

Recall from
[
28
, Lemma 2.4(c)]
that the dual of
\sigma
agrees with the
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-linear map
\delta:\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{2}_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}}(\Omega^{1}_{X},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})\to\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{2}_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,x}}(\Omega^{1}_{X,x},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,x})
in (
8.10
). The composite map

\hom_{{\mathbb{C}}}(V,{{\mathbb{C}}})\to\hom_{{\mathbb{C}}}(H^{n-2}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X}),{{\mathbb{C}}})=\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{2}_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}}(\Omega^{1}_{X},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})\to\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{2}_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,x}}(\Omega^{1}_{X,x},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,x})

(12.5)

is thus dual to the surjection
\pi\circ\sigma
and in particular injective.

We show finally that
\hom_{{\mathbb{C}}}(V,{{\mathbb{C}}})
may be identified with the image of
\gamma
in (
8.10
) and that the map
\hom_{{\mathbb{C}}}(V,{{\mathbb{C}}})\to\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{2}_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}}(\Omega^{1}_{X},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})
from (
12.5
) then agrees with the inclusion
\mathop{\rm im}\gamma\to\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{2}_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}}(\Omega^{1}_{X},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}).
Recall from (
8.7
) that
V
is isomorphic to the kernel of the map
H^{n-1}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X_{k-1}/A_{k-1}})\to H^{n-1}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X_{k}/A_{k}}).
The image of the latter arrow of (
8.8
) may then be identified with
\hom_{A_{k}}(V,A_{k})\subseteq\hom_{A_{k}}(H^{n-2}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X}),A_{k}).
The image of the latter arrow of (
8.9
) may in turn be identified with
\hom_{{\mathbb{C}}}(V,{{\mathbb{C}}})\subseteq\hom_{{\mathbb{C}}}(H^{n-2}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X}),{{\mathbb{C}}})=\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{2}_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}}(\Omega^{1}_{X},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}).
The last map thus agrees with
\mathop{\rm im}\gamma\to\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{2}_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}}(\Omega^{1}_{X},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})
in (
8.6
), which completes the proof.
∎

Remark 12.10
.

Corollary
12.9
is true also for
n=1,2.
For
n=1
the normal complex space
X
is non-singular and we have
\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{2}_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}}(\Omega^{1}_{X},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})\cong H^{2}(X,\Theta_{X})=0.
So
\mathop{\rm im}\gamma=0
and it is obvious that
\delta|_{\mathop{\rm im}\gamma}
is injective.

For
n=2,
although the stronger result (Theorem
1.2
) is known it may be worthwhile to give a direct proof of the current statement (Corollary
12.9
). We show first that
\Omega^{1}_{X_{k}/A_{k}}
is a flat
A_{k}
module sheaf. Recall for instance from
[
32
, Theorem 7.5.1(iv)]
that every
x\in X^{\rm sing}
is a hypersurface singularity, defined in
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{3}
by a single equation
f=0.
By the definition of
\Omega^{1}_{X}
there is an exact sequence
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}\to\Omega^{1}_{{{\mathbb{C}}}^{3}}\otimes_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{{\mathbb{C}}}^{3}}}{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}\to\Omega^{1}_{X}\to 0.
The first arrow is injective because its kernel vanishes at every point of
X^{\rm reg}.
There is thus an exact sequence

0\to{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}\to\Omega^{1}_{{{\mathbb{C}}}^{3}}\otimes_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{{\mathbb{C}}}^{3}}}{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}\to\Omega^{1}_{X}\to 0.

(12.6)

It is also known that the germ at
x
of
X_{k}/A_{k}
is an unfolding of
f
in
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{3},
defined by some
F\in{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{{\mathbb{C}}}^{3}}\times_{{\mathbb{C}}}A_{k}
extending
f.
Generalizing (
12.6
) we get an exact sequence

0\to{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X_{k}}\to\Omega^{1}_{({{\mathbb{C}}}^{3}\times\spec A)/\spec A}\otimes_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{{\mathbb{C}}}^{3}\times\spec A}}{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X_{k}}\to\Omega^{1}_{X_{k}/A_{k}}\to 0.

(12.7)

Now (
12.6
) is obtained from (
12.7
) after tensoring with
{{\mathbb{C}}}
as
A_{k}
modules. But then
\mathrm{Tor}_{1}({{\mathbb{C}}},\Omega^{1}_{X_{k}/A_{k}})
vanishes and
\Omega^{1}_{X_{k}/A_{k}}
is flat over
A_{k}.

So there is an exact sequence
0\to\Omega_{X_{k-1}/A_{k-1}}\to\Omega^{1}_{X_{k}/A_{k}}\to\Omega^{1}_{X}\to 0.
On the other hand, there is also an exact sequence
0\to{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X_{k-1}/A_{k-1}}\to{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X_{k}/A_{k}}\to{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}\to 0.
In particular, passing to the cohomology groups we see that for
p,q\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
with
p+q=1
we have

\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}H^{q}(X,\Omega^{p}_{X_{k}/A_{k}})\leqslant\penalty\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}H^{q}(X,\Omega^{p}_{X})+\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}H^{q}(X,\Omega^{p}_{X_{k-1}/A_{k-1}}).

(12.8)

Hence it follows by induction on
k
that

\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}H^{q}(X,\Omega^{p}_{X_{k}/A_{k}})\leqslant\penalty(k+1)\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}H^{q}(X,\Omega^{p}_{X}).

(12.9)

On the other hand, from the Hodge spectral sequence
H^{q}(X,\Omega^{p}_{X_{k}/A_{k}})\Rightarrow\gr^{p}H^{1}(X,A_{k})
we get

\sum_{p+q=1}\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}H^{q}(X,\Omega^{p}_{X_{k}/A_{k}})\geqslant\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}H^{1}(X,A_{k})=(k+1)\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}H^{1}(X,{{\mathbb{C}}}).

(12.10)

We show now that
X
has an orbifold Kähler form. Recall again from
[
32
, Theorem 7.5.1(xi)]
that every singularity
x\in X^{\rm sing}
is of the form
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{2}/G
with
G<\SU(2)
a finite subgroup. Take a Kähler form on
X^{{\rm reg}}
and near
x\in X^{\rm sing}
pull it back to
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{2}\setminus\{0\}.
By (
4.11
) we can change this to a Kähler form on
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{2}
without changing it at the points far from
x.
Taking the average with respect to
G
we can push it down to an orbifold Kähler metric. Since compact Kähler orbifolds have the Hodge decomposition property
[
1
]
it follows that the inequalities of (
12.8
)–(
12.10
) are in fact equalities. The map
H^{0}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X_{k}/A_{k}})\to H^{0}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X})
is thus surjective. This means the vanishing of the vector space
V
defined in the proof of Corollary
12.9
. The map
\delta
is therefore injective. ∎

We finally prove Theorem
1.2
. Let
X
be a compact Calabi–Yau conifold. By Lemma
12.8
the condition (
8.5
) holds and we can thus apply Lemma
8.19
. Combining it with Corollary
12.9
we complete the proof. ∎.

13
Relative Harmonic Forms

In this section we prove the facts we shall need for the proof of Theorem
1.3
.

Definition 13.1
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler
n
-conifold,
A
an Artin local
{{\mathbb{R}}}
-algebra and
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a locally trivial deformation of
X.
Using Corollary
9.13
choose on
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a Kähler conifold metric, whose Kähler form we denote by
\omega_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\in\Gamma(\Lambda^{11}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}).
Define
g_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
by (
10.2
). For
p\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
and
\alpha\in{{\mathbb{R}}}
define an
A
-bilinear map
L^{2}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\times L^{2}_{2-\alpha-2n}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to A
by
(\phi,\psi)\mapsto\int_{X^{{\rm reg}}}g_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}(\phi,\psi)\omega^{n}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}.
This is well defined because the relative volume form
\omega^{n}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
near each
x\in X^{\rm sing}
may be identified with that of the cone metric at
x.
For the same reason there is an
A
-module isomorphism
L^{2}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\cong L^{2}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{p}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}A.
We give
A
the topology induced by any norms on it, which is well defined because
A
is a finite-dimensional
{{\mathbb{R}}}
-vector space. Using the topology on
L^{2}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{p}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
and that of
A
we give
L^{2}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{p}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}A
the topology of a Banach space. Using the
A
-module isomorphism
L^{2}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\cong L^{2}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{p}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}A
we give
L^{2}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
the topology of a Banach space.

We study the dual spaces of the weighted
L^{2}
spaces for infinitesimal deformations.

Proposition 13.2
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler
n
-conifold,
A
an Artin local
{{\mathbb{R}}}
-algebra and
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a locally trivial deformation of
X.
Using Corollary
9.13
choose on
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a Kähler conifold metric. Fix
p\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
and
\alpha\in{{\mathbb{R}}}.
Denote by
\hom_{A}(L^{2}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}),A)
the space of continuous
A
-module homomorphisms and by
\Xi_{A}:L^{2}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to\hom_{A}(L^{2}_{2-\alpha-n}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}),A)
the
A
-module homomorphism
\phi\mapsto\int_{X^{{\rm reg}}}g_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}(\phi,\bullet)\omega^{n}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}.
The latter is then an
A
-module isomorphism.

Proof.

For
A={{\mathbb{R}}}
this is a standard fact; the
p=0
case is treated in
[
33
, Lemma 2.8]
and the extension to the general
p
is straightforward. We treat the general
A
by an induction on its length. Let
0\to(\epsilon)\to A\to B\to 0
be a small extension in
({\rm Art})_{{\mathbb{R}}}.
Define a deformation
{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B
by
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}:={{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\otimes_{A}B.
There is then a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{2}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{A}(L^{2}_{2-\alpha-n}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}),A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{2}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{B}(L^{2}_{2-\alpha-n}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}),B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi_{B}}

(13.1)

where the horizontal maps, both called
R,
are induced by
A\to B.
We show first that
\Xi_{A}
is injective. Take
\phi\in L^{2}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
with
\Xi_{A}\phi=0.
Then
\Xi_{B}R\phi=R\Xi_{A}\phi=0.
By the induction hypothesis we have
R\phi=0.
So
\phi\in L^{2}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\otimes_{A}(\epsilon)\cong L^{2}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{p}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}(\epsilon)
and we can therefore write
\Xi_{A}\phi=\Xi_{{\mathbb{R}}}\phi.
But we know already that
\Xi_{{\mathbb{R}}}
is injective, which implies
\phi=0
as we have to prove.

We show next that
\Xi_{A}
is surjective. Take
\psi\in\hom_{A}(L^{2}_{2-\alpha-n}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}),A).
By the induction hypothesis there exists
\phi^{\prime}\in L^{2}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
with
\Xi_{B}\phi^{\prime}=R\psi.
Since
\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
is isomorphic to
\Lambda^{p}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}A
it follows that
R:L^{2}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to L^{2}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
is surjective. Take
\phi\in L^{2}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
with
R\phi=\phi^{\prime}.
Then
R\Xi_{A}\phi=\Xi_{B}R\phi=R\psi.
So
\Xi_{A}\phi-\psi
lies in

\hom_{A}(L^{2}_{2-\alpha-n}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}),A)\otimes_{A}(\epsilon)\cong\hom_{{\mathbb{C}}}(L^{2}_{2-\alpha-n}(\Lambda^{p}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}),{{\mathbb{C}}})\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}(\epsilon).

(13.2)

As we know already that
\Xi_{{\mathbb{R}}}
is surjective, there exists
\chi\in L^{2}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{p}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
with
\Xi_{{\mathbb{R}}}\chi=-\Xi_{A}\phi+\psi.
Using the ring homomorphism
{{\mathbb{R}}}\to A
that defines the
{{\mathbb{R}}}
-algebra structure of
A
we get an embedding
\Lambda^{p}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}\to\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
and can then regard
\chi
as an element of
L^{2}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).
Now
\Xi_{A}(\chi+\phi)=\psi
as we want to prove.
∎

We generalize Proposition
5.8
.

Proposition 13.3
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler
n
-conifold,
A
an Artin local
{{\mathbb{R}}}
-algebra and
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a locally trivial deformation of
X.
Using Corollary
9.13
choose on
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a Kähler conifold metric. Then for
\alpha\in{{\mathbb{R}}}
the operator

\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:H^{k}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{pq}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to H^{k-2}_{\alpha-2}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})

(13.3)

is a Fredholm operator if and only if
\alpha
is a non-exceptional value of
\Delta_{X}:H^{2}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{p}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to L^{2}_{\alpha-2}(\Lambda^{p}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).

Proof.

As
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
is locally trivial each
x\in X^{\rm sing}
has a punctured neighbourhood on which we can identify
\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
with
\Delta_{X}\otimes{\rm id}_{A}:\Gamma(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}A\to\Gamma(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}A.
We denote by
C_{x}
the cone of
(X,x)
and say that a section of
\Gamma(\Lambda^{p}_{C^{{\rm reg}}_{x}})\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}A
is
homogeneous of order
\alpha\in{{\mathbb{R}}}
if it is a finite sum
\sum\phi\otimes\psi
where
\phi
is an order
\alpha
homogeneous
p
-form on
C_{x}
and
\psi
an element of
A.
Denote by
\Delta_{x}
the
p
-form Laplacian of
C_{x}.
By
[
42
, Theorem 6.2]
then (
13.3
) is Fredholm if and only if no non-zero order
\alpha
homogeneous section
\chi\in\Gamma(\Lambda^{p}_{C^{{\rm reg}}_{x}})\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}A
satisfies
(\Delta_{x}\otimes{\rm id}_{A})\chi=0.
This is equivalent to saying that
\alpha
is a non-exceptional value as stated above.
∎

We generalize Proposition
5.12
to the locally trivial infinitesimal deformations.

Proposition 13.4
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler
n
-conifold. Let
k\geqslant 0
be an integer and take
A:={{\mathbb{C}}}[t]/(t^{k+1}).
Let
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
be a locally trivial deformation of
X;
and using Corollary
9.13
, choose on
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a Kähler conifold metric. Let
\alpha\in{{\mathbb{R}}}
be a non-exceptional value of
\Delta_{X}:H^{2}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{p}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to L^{2}_{\alpha-2}(\Lambda^{p}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
and take
\phi\in L^{2}_{\alpha-2}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).
Then
\phi
lies in the image of the Fredholm operator
\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:H^{2}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to L^{2}_{\alpha-2}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
if and only if
\phi\cdot\psi=0
for every
\psi\in\mathop{\rm ker}(\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})_{2-\alpha-2n}.

Proof.

Denote by
(\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})_{\alpha}
the map
\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}:H^{2}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to L^{2}_{\alpha-2}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).
This is an
A
-module homomorphism and its cokernel
\mathop{\rm coker}(\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A_{k}})_{\alpha}
is of course an
A
-module. Since
(\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})_{\alpha}
is a Fredholm operator it follows that
\mathop{\rm coker}(\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A_{k}})_{\alpha}
is a Hausdorff topological vector space of finite dimension, isomorphic to the Euclidean space. Denote by
[\mathop{\rm coker}(\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})_{\alpha}]^{*}
the
A
-module dual to
\mathop{\rm coker}(\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})_{\alpha}.
We show that there is an
A
-module isomorphism

[\mathop{\rm coker}(\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A_{k}})_{\alpha}]^{*}\cong\mathop{\rm ker}(\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})_{2-\alpha-2n}.

(13.4)

An element of
[\mathop{\rm coker}(\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A_{k}})_{\alpha}]^{*}\cong\mathop{\rm ker}(\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})_{2-\alpha-2n}
is automatically continuous and is equivalent to a continuous
A
-module homomorphism
L^{2}_{\alpha-2}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to A
which vanishes on
\mathop{\rm im}(\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})_{\alpha}.
Using Proposition
13.2
regard this as an element
\phi\in L^{2}_{2-\alpha-2n}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).
This should then satisfy
\int_{X^{{\rm reg}}}g_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}(\phi,\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\psi)\omega^{n}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}=0
for every
\psi\in L^{2}_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).
As
\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
is self-adjoint with respect to this pairing, the equation is equivalent to
\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi=0;
that is,
\phi\in\mathop{\rm ker}(\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})_{2-\alpha-2n}.
Hence we get (
13.4
).

Recall now from hypothesis that
A={{\mathbb{C}}}[t]/(t^{k+1}).
It is known that every finitely generated
A
-module
M
is a reflexive
A
-module, which is in fact one of the six equivalent definitions
[
36
, Theorem 8(1)–(6)]
of pseudo-Frobenius rings. It is also possible to give a direct proof by an induction on
k,
as we do now. Put
A_{k}:={{\mathbb{C}}}[t]/(t^{k+1}).
The statement is obvious for
k=0
because
A_{0}
is a field. For
k\geqslant 1
let it hold for
k-1
in place of
k.
Let
M
be a finitely generated
A_{k}
module. There is then an
A_{k}
module exact sequence
0\to tM\to M\to M/tM\to 0.
Notice that
tM
is naturally an
A_{k-1}
module and
M/tM
an
A_{0}
module, to which we can apply the induction hypotheses. We see then that the natural maps
tM\to(tM)^{**}
and
M/tM\to(M/tM)^{**},
the maps of taking double duals, are
A_{k}
module isomorphisms. These with the map
M\to M^{**}
form an
A_{k}
module commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces tM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M/tM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(tM)^{**}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{**}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M/tM)^{**}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

(13.5)

Since
A_{k}
is (as in Definition
8.18
) an injective
A_{k}
module it follows that the bottom sequence of (
13.5
) is exact. The five lemma implies therefore that the middle vertical map
M\to M^{**}
is an
A_{k}
module isomorphism, completing the induction step.

Since
\mathop{\rm coker}(\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})_{\alpha}
is a finitely generated
A
-module we get an
A
-module isomorphism
\mathop{\rm coker}(\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})_{\alpha}\cong[\mathop{\rm coker}(\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})_{\alpha}]^{**}.
Hence we get using (
13.4
) an
A
-module isomorphism
\mathop{\rm coker}(\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})_{\alpha}\cong[\mathop{\rm ker}(\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})_{2-\alpha-2n}]^{*}.
The natural projection
L^{2}_{\alpha-2}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to\mathop{\rm coker}(\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})_{\alpha}
may then be identified with the transpose of the inclusion
\mathop{\rm ker}(\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})_{2-\alpha-2n}\subseteq L^{2}_{2-\alpha-2n}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}),
which completes the proof.
∎

We generalize also Proposition
5.13
.

Proposition 13.5
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler
n
-conifold,
A
an Artin local
{{\mathbb{R}}}
-algebra and
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a locally trivial deformation of
X.
Using Corollary
9.13
choose on
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a Kähler conifold metric. Let
\phi\in L^{2}(\Lambda^{n}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
satisfy
\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi=0.
Then
\phi
is in fact of order
>-n;
that is,
\phi\in L^{2}_{\epsilon-n}(\Lambda^{n}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
for some
\epsilon>0.

Proof.

For
A={{\mathbb{R}}}
this holds by Proposition
5.13
. We treat the general case by an induction on the length of
A.
Let
0\to(\epsilon)\to A\to B
be a small extension in
({\rm Art})_{{\mathbb{R}}}.
Define a deformation
{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B
by
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}:={{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\otimes_{A}B.
Denote by
\psi
the restriction of
\phi
to
\Lambda^{n}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}.
Then
\Delta_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}\psi=0
and by the induction hypothesis
\psi
has order
>-n.
Choosing a cochain complex isomorphism
(\Lambda^{n}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}},{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})\cong(\Lambda^{n}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}A,{\rm d}_{X}\otimes{\rm id}_{A})
and an
{{\mathbb{R}}}
-vector space splitting of
A\to B,
lift
\psi
to a section
\psi^{\prime}
of
\Lambda^{n}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}.
Then
\psi^{\prime}
has order
>-n,
and
\phi-\psi^{\prime}
is an
L^{2}
section of
(\Lambda^{n}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}(\epsilon)\cong\Lambda^{n}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}.
Applying Proposition
5.13
to
\phi-\psi^{\prime}
we see that
\phi-\psi^{\prime}
has order
>-n.

∎

We generalize Lemma
5.15
in two steps. The first step is as follows.

Theorem 13.6
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler
n
-conifold. Let
k\geqslant 0
be an integer; take
A:={{\mathbb{C}}}[t]/(t^{k+1})
and let
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
be a locally trivial deformation of
X.
Using Corollary
9.13
choose on
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a Kähler conifold metric and give
X
the induced conifold metric. Let
p\leqslant\penalty n-2
be an integer. Then the following holds:

For every
\alpha\in(2+p-2n,-p)=:I_{p}
and every
\phi\in\Gamma_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
with
\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi=0,
we have
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi={\rm d}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi=0
and
\phi\in\bigcap_{\beta\in I_{p}}\Gamma_{\beta}(\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).

(13.6)

Proof.

We prove (
13.6
) by a double induction on
p
and
A,
where
p
is an outer parameter and
A
an inner parameter. For
p<0
the statement is automatically true. Suppose that there exists an integer
q\leqslant\penalty n-2
such that (
13.6
) holds for every
p\leqslant\penalty q-1.
We prove by the inner induction on
A
that (
13.6
) holds for
p=q.

For (
13.6
) with
A={{\mathbb{R}}},
by Corollary
2.7
we have
\phi\in\Gamma_{-p}(\Lambda^{p}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).
As
-p\geqslant 2-n
the integration by parts formula
(\Delta_{X}\phi)\cdot\phi={\rm d}_{X}\phi\cdot{\rm d}_{X}\phi+{\rm d}_{X}^{*}\phi\cdot{\rm d}_{X}^{*}\phi
is valid. Since
\Delta_{X}\phi=0
it follows that
{\rm d}_{X}\phi={\rm d}_{X}^{*}\phi=0
as claimed.

For the general
A
we do the inner induction. Let
0\to(\epsilon)\to A\to B\to 0
be a small extension in
({\rm Art})_{{\mathbb{R}}}.
Define a deformation
{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B
by
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}:={{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\otimes_{A}B.
Denote by
R:\Gamma(\Lambda^{q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to\Gamma(\Lambda^{q}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
the restriction map. Take any
\beta\in I_{q}
and let
\phi
be as in (
13.6
) with
p=q.
We show that there exists
\phi^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{\beta}(\Lambda^{q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
with
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi^{\prime}={\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}\phi^{\prime}=0
and
R\phi^{\prime}=R\phi.
By the induction hypothesis we have
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}R\phi={\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}^{*}R\phi=0
and
R\phi\in\Gamma_{\beta}(\Lambda^{q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).
Choose
\psi\in\Gamma_{\beta}(\Lambda^{q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
with
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\psi=0
and
R\psi=R\phi.
Notice that
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}\psi\in\Gamma_{\beta-1}(\Lambda^{q-1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
and that
2-(\beta+1)-2n=1-\beta-2n\in(1-2n+q,-1-q)\subseteq(1-2n+q,1-q)=I_{q-1}.
By the outer induction hypothesis, if
\theta\in\mathop{\rm ker}(\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{q-1})_{1-\beta-2n}
then
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\theta={\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}\theta=0
and
\theta\in\bigcap_{\gamma\in I_{q-1}}\Gamma_{\gamma}(\Lambda^{q-1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).
The order estimate implies that the integration by parts formula
({\rm d}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi)\cdot\theta=\phi\cdot{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\theta
makes sense. Since
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\theta=0
it follows that
{\rm d}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi\cdot\theta=0.
By Proposition
13.4
there exists
\chi\in\Gamma_{\beta}(\Lambda^{q-1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
with
\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\chi={\rm d}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi.
In particular,
\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}{\rm d}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\chi=0.
As
{\rm d}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\chi
has order
\beta-1\in(1+q-2n,-q-1)\subseteq I_{q-1}\subseteq I_{q-2}
it follows from the outer induction hypothesis that
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}\chi
is closed and co-closed; that is,
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}\chi=0.
The equation
\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\chi={\rm d}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\psi
implies in turn that
{\rm d}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\psi={\rm d}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\chi.
Applying the restriction map
R
we get
{\rm d}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}R\phi={\rm d}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}R\chi.
But we know already that
{\rm d}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}R\phi=0
so that
{\rm d}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}R\chi=0.
Since
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}\chi=0
it follows that
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}^{*}R\chi=0.
This with
