{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}\chi=0
implies
\Delta_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}R\chi=0.
By the inner induction hypothesis we have
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}^{*}R\chi=0.
So
R(\psi-{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}\chi)=R\psi=R\phi;
that is,
\phi^{\prime}:=\psi-{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}\chi
is a lift of
R\phi
we want.

Now
R(\phi-\phi^{\prime})=0
or equivalently
\phi-\phi^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{\beta}(\Lambda^{q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\otimes_{A}(\epsilon)\cong\Gamma_{\beta}(\Lambda^{q}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}(\epsilon).
So
0=\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}(\phi-\phi^{\prime})=\Delta_{X}(\phi-\phi^{\prime}).
As we have shown in the initial step of the inner induction, we have
{\rm d}_{X}(\phi-\phi^{\prime})={\rm d}_{X}^{*}(\phi-\phi^{\prime})=0
and
\phi-\phi^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{\beta}(\Lambda^{q}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}(\epsilon)
for every
\beta\in I_{q}.
Or equivalently
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}(\phi-\phi^{\prime})={\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}(\phi-\phi^{\prime})=0
and
\phi-\phi^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{\beta}(\Lambda^{q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\otimes_{A}(\epsilon).
Since
\phi^{\prime}
is closed and co-closed and since
\phi^{\prime}
is of order
\beta
it follows that
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi={\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}(\phi-\phi^{\prime})=0
and
\phi\in\Gamma_{\beta}(\Lambda^{q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).
As
\beta\in I_{q}
has been arbitrary, we have
\phi\in\bigcap_{\beta\in I_{q}}\Gamma_{\beta}(\Lambda^{q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}),
completing the proof.
∎

Remark 13.7
.

The hypothesis
A={{\mathbb{C}}}[t]/(t^{k+1})
is relevant only to the step of applying Proposition
13.4
.

By the same technique we prove the following corollary, which generalizes Lemma
5.15
.

Corollary 13.8
.

Let
X
be a compact Calabi–Yau
n
-conifold. Let
k\geqslant 0
be an integer; and take
A:={{\mathbb{C}}}[t]/(t^{k+1})
and let
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
be a locally trivial deformation of
X.
Using Corollary
9.13
choose on
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a Kähler conifold metric and give
X
the induced conifold metric. Let
\epsilon>0
be so small that
[1-n-\epsilon,1-n)
contains no exceptional values of the
n-1
form Laplacian over
X^{{\rm reg}}.
Let
\phi\in\Gamma_{1-n-\epsilon}(\Lambda^{n-1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
satisfy
\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi=0.
Then
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi={\rm d}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi=0.

Proof.

For
A={{\mathbb{R}}},
near each
x\in X^{\rm sing}
write
\phi
as a countable sum of homogeneous
n-1
forms on
C_{x}.
Making
\epsilon
small enough it follows that
\phi
begins with an order
1-n
homogeneous
n-1
form
\phi^{\prime}.
By Corollary
2.11
we have
{\rm d}\phi^{\prime}={\rm d}^{*}\phi^{\prime}=0.
So
{\rm d}\phi
and
{\rm d}^{*}\phi
have order
\delta-n
for some
\delta>0.
Supposing
\epsilon<\delta
it follows that the integration by parts formula
\phi\cdot\Delta\phi={\rm d}\phi\cdot{\rm d}\phi+{\rm d}^{*}\phi\cdot{\rm d}^{*}\phi
is valid. As
\Delta\phi=0
we have
{\rm d}\phi={\rm d}^{*}\phi=0
as required.

For the general
A
we proceed by an induction (which is similar to the inner induction process in the proof of Theorem
13.6
). Let
0\to(\epsilon)\to A\to B\to 0
be a small extension in
({\rm Art})_{{\mathbb{R}}}.
Define a deformation
{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B
by
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}:={{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\otimes_{A}B.
Denote by
R:\Gamma(\Lambda^{q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to\Gamma(\Lambda^{q}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
the restriction map. Then
R\phi\in\Gamma_{1-n-\epsilon}(\Lambda^{n-1}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
and
\Delta_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}R\phi=0.
By the induction hypothesis therefore
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}R\phi={\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}^{*}R\phi=0.
Choose
\psi\in\Gamma_{1-n-\epsilon}(\Lambda^{n-1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
with
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\psi=0
and
R\psi=R\phi.
Notice that
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}\psi\in\Gamma_{-n-\epsilon}(\Lambda^{n-2}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).
Putting
\alpha:=2-n-\epsilon
we have
2-\alpha-2n=-n+\epsilon.
By Theorem
13.6
, if
\theta\in\mathop{\rm ker}(\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{n-2})_{-n+\epsilon}
then
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\theta=0.
So
({\rm d}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\psi)\cdot\theta=\psi\cdot{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\theta=0.
By Proposition
13.4
therefore there exists
\chi\in\Gamma_{\alpha}(\Lambda^{n-2}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
with
\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\chi={\rm d}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\psi.
In particular,
\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}{\rm d}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\chi=0.
But
{\rm d}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\chi\in\Gamma_{\alpha-1}(\Lambda^{n-2}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
with
\alpha-1=1-n-\epsilon\in I_{n-3}
in the notation of Theorem
13.6
. So
{\rm d}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\chi
is closed; that is,
0={\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}{\rm d}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}={\rm d}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}(\psi-{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\chi).
Now
\phi^{\prime}:=\psi-{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\chi
is a relative closed and co-closed form whose image under
R
is equal to that of
\phi.
We have
R(\phi^{\prime}-\phi)=0
and can write
\phi^{\prime}-\phi\in\Gamma(\Lambda^{n-1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\otimes_{A}(\epsilon)\cong\Gamma(\Lambda^{n-1}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}(\epsilon).
We have also
\Delta_{X}(\phi^{\prime}-\phi)=\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}(\phi^{\prime}-\phi)=0.
The result in the
A={{\mathbb{R}}}
case implies then
{\rm d}_{X}(\phi^{\prime}-\phi)={\rm d}_{X}(\phi-\phi^{\prime})=0
and accordingly
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}(\phi^{\prime}-\phi)={\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}(\phi-\phi^{\prime})=0.
Since
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi^{\prime}={\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi^{\prime}=0
it follows that
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi={\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}\phi=0,
completing the proof.
∎

We generalize now Theorem
6.5
.

Proposition 13.9
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler
n
-conifold,
A
an Artin local
{{\mathbb{R}}}
-algebra and
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a locally trivial deformation of
X.
Using Corollary
9.13
choose on
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a Kähler conifold metric. Then there is an injective
A
-module homomorphism
\mathop{\rm ker}({\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}+{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*})^{n}_{-n}\cong{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},A).

Proof.

Use the cochain complex isomorphism
\Lambda^{n}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}\cong\Lambda^{n}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}A
and note that the Kähler conifold metric is
q
-bounded in the sense of
[
41
, Theorem 7.4]
. This result then extends to relative forms; that is,

L^{2}(\Lambda^{n}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\cap{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}[\Gamma(\Lambda^{n-1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})]\subseteq\overline{{\rm d}[\Gamma_{c}(\Lambda^{n-1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})]}

(13.7)

where the bar on the right-hand side is to take the closure in
L^{2}(\Lambda^{n}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}).
Consider now the natural
A
-module homomorphism
\mathop{\rm ker}({\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}+{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*})^{n}_{-n}\to{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},A)
which takes the
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
cohomology class. By (
13.7
) this map is injective.
∎

Remark 13.10
.

The map
\mathop{\rm ker}({\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}+{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*})^{n}_{-n}\to{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},A)
is in fact surjective too. For
A={{\mathbb{R}}}
this follows from the Poincaré duality property
[
52
, Chapter IV, Theorem 17’]
and Kodaira’s decomposition theorem
[
52
, Chapter V, Theorem 24]
. We can show by an induction on
A
that these results extend to the infinitesimal deformation
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A.
We omit the details because we shall not need them.

We end with a remark which applies to the results above in the present section. In these statements the Kähler condition has not played essential rôles in the proofs. They will extend to locally trivial deformations of compact Riemannian conifolds once we have made the right definition of such objects. We have not done that just because the current statements will do for our purpose.

14
Proof of Theorem
1.3

We prove a lemma about relative tangent sheaves.

Lemma 14.1
.

Let
X
be a normal complex space,
A
an Artin local
{{\mathbb{C}}}
-algebra and
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a deformation of
X.
Denote by
\Theta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
the
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
module dual to
\Omega^{1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}.
Then the following three statements hold:
(i)
the natural
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
module homomorphism
\Theta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\iota_{*}(\Theta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
is an isomorphism;
(ii)
if
U\subseteq X
is a Stein neighbourhood of
X^{\rm sing}
then for
q=1,2,3,\dots
we have
H^{q}(U,\Theta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})=0;
and

(iii)

H^{1}_{X^{\rm sing}}(X,\Theta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})=0.

Proof.

We prove these by an induction on the length of
A.
For
A={{\mathbb{C}}}
it is a well-known property of the reflexive sheaf
\Theta_{X}.
Suppose now that
0\to(\epsilon)\to A\to B\to 0
is a small extension in
({\rm Art})_{{\mathbb{C}}}.
Define a deformation
{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B
by
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}:={{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\otimes_{A}B.
As
\Theta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
is flat over
X^{{\rm reg}},
tensoring
\Theta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}
with the small extension sequence we get a short exact sequence
0\to\Theta_{X^{{\rm reg}}}\to\Theta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}\to\Theta_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}\to 0.
Pushing forward these by
\iota_{*}
and using the isomorphism
\iota_{*}\Theta_{X^{{\rm reg}}}\cong\Theta_{X}
we get a short exact sequence

0\to\Theta_{X}\to\iota_{*}(\Theta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to\iota_{*}(\Theta_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to 0.

(14.1)

On the other hand, using the natural transformation
{\rm id}\to\iota_{*}\iota^{*}
we get a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{*}(\Theta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{*}(\Theta_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

(14.2)

By the induction hypothesis the rightmost vertical map
\beta
is an isomorphism. Although the top left part is missing in (
14.2
) we can show directly by diagram chase that the five lemma applies to the current circumstances; that is,
\alpha
is an isomorphism, which proves (i).

Now (
14.1
) becomes
0\to\Theta_{X}\to\Theta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Theta_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}\to 0.
Let
U\subseteq X
be a Stein neighbourhood of
X^{\rm sing}.
Then for
q=1,2,3,\dots
there is an exact sequence
H^{q}(U,\Theta_{X})\to H^{q}(U,\Theta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})\to H^{q}(U,\Theta_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}).
But
H^{1}(U,\Theta_{X})=0
and by the induction hypothesis
H^{1}(U,\Theta_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B})=0.
So
H(U,\Theta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})=0
as in (ii).

From the short exact sequence
0\to\Theta_{X}\to\Theta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\to\Theta_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}\to 0
we get also an exact sequence
H^{1}_{X^{\rm sing}}(X,\Theta_{X})\to H^{1}_{X^{\rm sing}}(X,\Theta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})\to H^{1}_{X^{\rm sing}}(X,\Theta_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}).
But as
\Theta_{X}
is reflexive, the leftmost term
H^{1}_{X^{\rm sing}}(X,\Theta_{X})
vanishes; and by the induction hypothesis, the rightmost term
H^{1}_{X^{\rm sing}}(X,\Theta_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B})
vanishes. Accordingly so does the middle term, which proves (iii).
∎

We generalize Corollary
6.3
as follows.

Corollary 14.2
.

In the circumstances of Lemma
14.1
there exists an isomorphism
{}_{c}H^{1}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Theta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})\cong H^{1}(X,\Theta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}).

Proof.

Lemmas
6.2
and
14.1
(ii) imply that
{}_{c}H^{1}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Theta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})
agrees with the image of the natural map
H^{1}(X,\Theta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})\to H^{1}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Theta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}).
Lemma
14.1
(iii) implies that the latter map is injective, from which we get the isomorphism we want.
∎

We generalize Lemma
6.6
.

Lemma 14.3
.

Let
n\geqslant 3
be an integer and
X
a compact Calabi–Yau
n
-conifold with
X^{\rm sing}
non-empty. Let
A
be an Artin local
{{\mathbb{R}}}
-algebra and
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a locally trivial deformation of
X.
Using Corollary
9.13
choose on
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a Kähler conifold metric and give
X
the induced conifold metric.
Let
\phi
be a section of
\Lambda^{n1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}
of order
>-n-1
with
{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi={\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}\phi=0.
Then there exists
\chi\in\Gamma(\Lambda^{n0}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
of order
>-n
and with
{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\chi=\phi.

Proof.

We show by an induction on
A
that
H^{1}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})=0.
Let
0\to(\epsilon)\to A\to B\to 0
be a small extension in
({\rm Art})_{{\mathbb{R}}}.
Define a deformation
{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B
by
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}:={{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\otimes_{A}B.
There are then exact sequences
0\to{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}\to{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\to{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}\to 0
and
0=H^{1}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})\to H^{1}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}})\to H^{1}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}).
By the induction hypothesis we have
H^{1}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}})=0.
So
H^{1}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}})=0.

Since
X
is of depth
\geqslant 3
it follows that
H^{1}_{X^{\rm sing}}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})=H^{2}_{X^{\rm sing}}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X})=0.
Again by an induction on
A
we can show that
H^{1}_{X^{\rm sing}}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}})=H^{2}_{X^{\rm sing}}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}})=0.
This with
H^{1}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}})=0
implies that
H^{1}(X^{{\rm reg}},{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}})=0.
So we can write
\phi={\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\chi
with
\chi
an
(n,0)
form on
X^{{\rm reg}}.
Then
{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\chi=0;
or equivalently,
\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\chi=0.
Since
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
is locally trivial it follows that for each
x\in X^{\rm sing}
there exists a punctured neighbourhood
U
of it on which
\Lambda^{n0}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{U}\cong\Lambda^{n0}_{U}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}A.
We can also identify
\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{U}
with
\Delta_{U}\otimes{\rm id}_{A}
where
\Delta_{U}
is the Laplacian over
U\subseteq X.
After choosing an
{{\mathbb{R}}}
-vector space basis of
A
we can then regard
\chi
as a finite system of harmonic
(n,0)
forms. To these we can apply the same argument as in the proof of Lemma
6.6
, which shows that
\chi
has order
>-n
as we want to prove.
∎

Lemma
7.3
generalizes readily as follows. We leave the proof to the reader as an exercise.

Proposition 14.4
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler
n
-conifold,
A
an Artin local
{{\mathbb{R}}}
-algebra and
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a locally trivial deformation of
X.
Using Corollary
9.13
choose on
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a Kähler conifold metric. Let
p
be an integer and
\phi
a section of order
>-p
of
\Lambda^{p}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}.
Then every
x\in X^{\rm sing}
has a punctured neighbourhood
U^{{\rm reg}}
on which
\phi
is
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
exact. ∎

We prove an integration by parts formula for relative harmonic forms.

Lemma 14.5
.

Let
X
be a compact Calabi–Yau
n
-conifold,
A
an Artin local
{{\mathbb{R}}}
-algebra and
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a deformation of
X.
Using Corollary
9.13
choose on
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a Kähler conifold metric and give
X
the induced conifold metric. Let
p,q\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
and
\alpha\in{{\mathbb{R}}}
be such that for every
\chi\in\mathop{\rm ker}\Delta^{pq}_{\alpha}
we have
{\rm d}\chi=0.
Then for every
\phi\in\mathop{\rm ker}(\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{pq})_{\alpha}
we have
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi=0.

Proof.

We prove this by an induction on the length of
A.
Let
0\to(\epsilon)\to A\to B\to 0
be a small extension in
({\rm Art})_{{\mathbb{R}}}.
Define a deformation
{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B
by
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}:={{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\otimes_{A}B.
Denote by
\psi
the restriction to
{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B
of
\phi.
Then
\psi
is of order
\alpha
and satisfies
\Delta_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}\psi=0.
By the induction hypothesis we have
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B}\phi=0.
Choosing a cochain complex isomorphism
(\Lambda^{p+q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}},{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})\cong(\Lambda^{p+q}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}A,{\rm d}_{X}\otimes{\rm id}_{A})
and an
{{\mathbb{R}}}
-vector space splitting of
A\to B,
lift
\psi
to a section
\psi^{\prime}
of
\Lambda^{p+q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}.
Then
\psi^{\prime}
has order
\alpha
and satisfies
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\psi^{\prime}=0.
Notice that
\phi-\psi^{\prime}
is a section of
(\Lambda^{p+q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}(\epsilon)\cong\Lambda^{p+q}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}
and satisfies
\Delta_{X}(\phi-\psi^{\prime})=0.
By hypothesis we have
{\rm d}_{X}(\phi-\psi^{\prime})=0.
Regarding
\phi-\psi^{\prime}
as a section of
(\Lambda^{p+q}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}(\epsilon)
we have
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}(\phi-\psi^{\prime})=0.
So
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi=0.

∎

Remark 14.6
.

Suppose that every
\chi\in\mathop{\rm ker}\Delta^{pq}_{\alpha}
satisfies also
{\rm d}_{X}^{*}\chi=0.
It will then be reasonable to ask whether
{\rm d}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi=0.
For our purpose however we shall not need an answer to this question and therefore not discuss it further.

We generalize now Theorem
6.7
.

Theorem 14.7
.

Let
n\geqslant 3
be an integer and
X
a compact Calabi–Yau
n
-conifold with
X^{\rm sing}
non-empty.
Let
k\geqslant 0
be an integer and take
A:={{\mathbb{C}}}[t]/(t^{k+1}).
Let
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
be a locally trivial deformation of
X;
and using Corollary
9.13
, choose on
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a Kähler conifold metric. Then there is an injective
A
-module homomorphism
\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}+{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}\to\gr^{n-1}{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},A).

Proof.

We show first that for every
\phi\in\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}+{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}
there exists on
X^{{\rm reg}}
a relative
(n,0)
form
\psi
of order
>-n
such that
{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\psi=\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi.
Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
be so small that
\phi
has order
\epsilon-n.
Then
{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi
is a relative
(n,0)
form of order
\epsilon-n-2.
Put
\alpha:=\epsilon-n
and take
\chi\in\mathop{\rm ker}(\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})^{n-1\,1}_{2-\alpha-2n}=\mathop{\rm ker}(\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})^{n-1\,1}_{2-\epsilon-n}.

Lemma
14.5
implies then that
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\chi=0.
As
\chi\in\Gamma(\Lambda^{n-1\,1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})
we have
{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\chi=0
and accordingly
\chi\cdot{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi=0.
So by Proposition
13.4
there exists an
(n,0)
form
{\tau}
of order
\alpha=\epsilon-n
and with
\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\theta={\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi.
Then
\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi-{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}{\tau}
is
{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
closed and co-closed. By Lemma
14.3
there exists
\sigma\in\Gamma(\Lambda^{n0}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
of order
>-n
and with
{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\sigma=\phi.
Put
\psi:=\sigma+{\tau},
which is a section of
\chi\in\Gamma(\Lambda^{n0}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
of order
>-n.
Moreover,
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}(\phi-\psi)={\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi+\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi-{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\psi=0.

By Proposition
14.4
the de Rham cohomology class
[\phi-\psi]
lies in
{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},A).
Recalling again that
\phi
is a relative
(n-1,1)
form and
\psi
a relative
(n,0)
form, we can also define
[\phi-\psi]\in\gr^{n-1}{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},A).
Notice that
[\phi-\psi]\in{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},A)
may depend on the choice of
\psi
but that
[\phi-\psi]\in\gr^{n-1}{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},A)
does not. The map
\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}+{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}\to\gr^{n-1}{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},A)
is thus well defined. This is of course an
A
-module homomorphism. We show now that it is injective.

Let
\phi\in\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}+{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}
be an element of the kernel of the map
\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}+{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}\to\gr^{n-1}{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A});
that is, there exists an
(n,0)
form
\psi
of order
>-n,
with
\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\psi={\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\phi
and with
[\phi-\psi]=0\in\gr^{n-1}{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},A).
Then there exists another
(n,0)
form
\psi^{\prime}
with
{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\psi^{\prime}=0
and
[\phi-\psi-\psi^{\prime}]=0\in H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},A).

Recall from Lemma
12.8
that there exists a nowhere-vanishing section
\Phi
of
\Omega^{n}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}.
We show that this is
L^{2}.
Since
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
is locally trivial it follows that for each
x\in X^{\rm sing}
there exists a punctured neighbourhood
U
of it on which
\Omega^{n}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}|_{U}\cong\Omega^{n}_{U}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}A.
So after choosing an
{{\mathbb{R}}}
-vector space basis of
A
we can regard
\Phi
as a finite system of sections of
\Omega^{n}_{U}.
But these are automatically
L^{2}
and accordingly so is
\Phi.

Write
\psi^{\prime}=f\Phi
with
f
some holomorphic function
X^{{\rm reg}}\to A.
Since
H^{1}_{X^{\rm sing}}(X,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}})=0
it follows that
f
extends to
X^{\rm sing}.
In particular,
f
is bounded and
f\Phi=\psi^{\prime}
is
L^{2}.
Proposition
13.5
implies that
\psi^{\prime}
has order
>-n.

Thus
\phi-\psi-\psi^{\prime}
has order
>-n;
and accordingly,
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}(\phi-\psi-\psi^{\prime})
has order
>-n-1.
Take
\epsilon>0
and put
\beta:=1+\epsilon-n.
Take
\chi\in\mathop{\rm ker}(\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{n-1})_{2-\beta-n}=\mathop{\rm ker}(\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{n-1})_{1-n-\epsilon}.
Letting
\epsilon>0
be small enough and using Corollary
13.8
we see that
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\chi={\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}\chi=0
and that
\chi\cdot{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}(\phi-\psi-\psi^{\prime})=0.
By Proposition
13.4
there exists on
X^{{\rm reg}}
a relative
n-1
form
\theta
of order
\beta=1+\epsilon-n
and with
\Delta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\theta={\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}(\phi-\psi-\psi^{\prime}).
Then
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}\theta={\rm d}^{*}(\phi-\psi-\psi^{\prime}-{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\theta).
Applying
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}
we get
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}\theta=0;
that is,
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}\theta
is a relative harmonic
n-2
form of order
\epsilon-n.
By Theorem
13.6
however
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}\theta
is closed and co-closed; that is,
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}\theta=0.
Or equivalently
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}(\phi-\psi-\psi^{\prime}-{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\theta)=0.
Thus
\phi-\psi-\psi^{\prime}-{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\theta\in\mathop{\rm ker}({\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}+{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*})^{n}_{-n}.
Since
[\phi-\psi-\psi^{\prime}]=0\in{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},A)\cong\mathop{\rm ker}({\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}+{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*})^{n}_{-n}
it follows by Theorem
13.9
that
\phi-\psi-\psi^{\prime}-{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\theta=0;
that is,
\phi-\psi-\psi^{\prime}={\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\theta.
We now generalize Lemma
6.8
as follows.

Lemma 14.8
.

Let
p,q\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
be such that
p+q=n-1.
Let
\chi
be a relative
(p,q)
form of order
>1-n
and with
{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\chi=0.
Then there exists a relative
(p,q-1)
form
\zeta
of order
2-n
and such that
\chi-{\bar{\partial}}\zeta
is
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
closed and co-closed.

If we use Proposition
13.4
and Theorem
13.6
the proof of Lemma
14.8
will be similar to that of Lemma
6.8
, which we leave to the reader as an exercise. For
(p,q)
with
p+q=n-1
denote by
\theta^{pq}
the
(p,q)
component of the
n-1
form
\theta.
So
\theta=\theta^{n-1\,0}+\theta^{n-2\,1}.
Since
{\rm d}\theta=\phi-\psi-\psi^{\prime}
has only
(n,0)
and
(n-1,1)
components it follows that
{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\theta^{n-2\,1}=0.
Applying Lemma
14.8
to
\theta^{n-2\,1}
we find some
(n-2,0)
form
\zeta
of order
2-n
such that
\theta^{n-2\,1}-{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\zeta
is
{\rm d}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}
closed. In particular,
\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\theta^{n-2\,1}=\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\zeta=-{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\zeta.
Put
\eta:=\theta^{n-1\,0}-{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\zeta.
Then
{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\eta={\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\theta^{n-1\,0}\theta+\partial_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}\theta^{n-2\,1},
which is exactly the
(n-1,1)
component of
{\rm d}\theta=\phi-\psi-\psi^{\prime},
that is,
\phi.
Since
\eta
is of order
1-n
and
\phi
of order
>-n
it follows that the integration by parts formula
\phi\cdot\phi={\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}\phi\cdot\eta=0
makes sense. But by the initial hypothesis we have
{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*}\phi=0
and so
\phi\cdot\phi=0.
We can then verify by an induction on
A
that the inner product is non-degenerate or more precisely that
\phi=0.
The map
\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}+{\bar{\partial}}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}\to\gr^{n-1}{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{\bullet}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
is thus injective.
∎

We next generalize Corollary
6.9
.

Corollary 14.9
.

Let
X
be a compact Calabi–Yau
n
-conifold. Let
k\geqslant 0
be an integer and take
A:={{\mathbb{C}}}[t]/(t^{k+1}).
Let
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
be a locally trivial deformation of
X;
and using Corollary
9.13
, choose on
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a Kähler conifold metric. The natural projection
\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}+{\bar{\partial}}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}\to H^{1}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{n-1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})
is then injective.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Corollary
6.9
, which we leave to the reader as an exercise.
∎

We generalize also Lemma
6.10
.

Lemma 14.10
.

Let
n\geqslant 3
be an integer and
X
a compact Calabi–Yau
n
-conifold with
X^{\rm sing}
non-empty.
Let
k\geqslant 0
be an integer and take
A:={{\mathbb{C}}}[t]/(t^{k+1}).
Let
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
be a locally trivial deformation of
X;
and using Corollary
9.13
, choose on
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a Kähler conifold metric. Then
{}_{c}H^{1}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{n-1}_{X^{{\rm reg}}})
lies in the image of the natural projection
\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}+{\bar{\partial}}^{*}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}\to H^{1}(X^{{\rm reg}},\Omega^{n-1}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}).

Proof.

If we use Corollary
13.8
, Proposition
13.4
and Theorem
13.6
then the proof of this lemma will be similar to that of Lemma
6.10
, which we leave to the reader as an exercise.
∎

We finally generalize Theorem
6.11
.

Theorem 14.11
.

Let
n\geqslant 3
be an integer and
X
a compact Calabi–Yau
n
-conifold with
X^{\rm sing}
non-empty and
H^{2}_{X^{\rm sing}}(X,\Omega^{n-2}_{X})=0.

Let
k\geqslant 0
be an integer and take
A:={{\mathbb{C}}}[t]/(t^{k+1}).
Let
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
be a locally trivial deformation of
X;
and using Corollary
9.13
, choose on
{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a Kähler conifold metric. Then there exist
A
-module isomorphisms

H^{1}(X,\Theta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})\cong\gr^{n-1}{}_{c}H^{n}(X^{{\rm reg}},A)\cong\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}+{\bar{\partial}}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}.

(14.3)

Proof.

By hypothesis every
x\in X^{\rm sing}
has a punctured neighbourhood
U\subseteq X^{{\rm reg}}
such that
H^{1}(U,\Omega^{n-2}_{X})\cong H^{2}_{x}(X,\Omega^{n-2}_{X})=0.
We can then verify by an induction on
A
that
H^{1}(U,\Omega^{n-2}_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})=0.
If we use Corollary
14.9
and Lemma
14.10
the rest of the proof will be similar to that of Theorem
6.11
, which we leave to the reader as an exercise.
∎

Using Theorem
14.11
we prove

Theorem 14.12
.

Let
n\geqslant 3
be an integer and
X
a compact Calabi–Yau
n
-conifold with
X^{\rm sing}
non-empty and
H^{2}_{X^{\rm sing}}(X,\Omega^{n-2}_{X})=0.
Let
k\geqslant 1
be an integer and put
A_{k}:={{\mathbb{C}}}[t]/(t^{k+1}).
Let
X_{k}/A_{k}
a locally trivial deformation of
X,
and
X_{k-1}/A_{k-1}
the deformation of
X
defined by
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X_{k-1}}:={{\mathcal{O}}}_{X_{k}}\otimes_{A_{k}}A_{k-1}.
The natural map
H^{1}(X,\Theta_{X_{k}/A_{k}})\to H^{1}(X,\Theta_{X_{k-1}/A_{k-1}})
is then surjective.

Proof.

We show by an induction on
k
that

\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}H^{1}(X,\Theta_{X_{k}/A_{k}})\leqslant\penalty(k+1)\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}H^{1}(X,\iota_{*}\Theta_{X}).

(14.4)

This holds automatically for
k=0.
The small extension
0\to(t^{k})\to A_{k}\to A_{k-1}\to 0
induces an
A_{k}
module sheaf exact sequence
0\to\iota_{*}\Theta_{X^{{\rm reg}}}\to\iota_{*}(\Theta_{X_{k}/A_{k}}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to\iota_{*}(\Theta_{X_{k-1}/A_{k-1}}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to 0.
This is by Lemma
14.1
(i) equivalent to an exact sequence
0\to\Theta_{X}\to\Theta_{X_{k}/A_{k}}\to\Theta_{X_{k-1}/A_{k-1}}\to 0.
Passing to the cohomology groups we get an
A_{k}
module exact sequence
H^{1}(X,\Theta_{X^{{\rm reg}}})\to H^{1}(X,\Theta_{X_{k}/A_{k}})\to H^{1}(X,\Theta_{X_{k-1}/A_{k-1}}).
The latter implies

\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}H^{1}(X,\Theta_{X_{k}/A_{k}})\leqslant\penalty\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}H^{1}(X,\Theta_{X_{k-1}/A_{k-1}})+\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}H^{1}(X,\Theta_{X}).

(14.5)

By the induction hypothesis we have
\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}H^{1}(X,\Theta_{X_{k-1}/A_{k-1}})\leqslant\penalty k\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}H^{1}(X,\Theta_{X}),
which with (
14.5
) implies (
14.4
).

We show also by an induction on
k
that

\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}_{X_{k}/A_{k}}+{\bar{\partial}}_{X_{k}/A_{k}}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}\geqslant(k+1)\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}_{X}+{\bar{\partial}}_{X}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}.

(14.6)

This holds again automatically for
k=0.
We show that the restriction map
R:L^{2}(\Lambda^{n-1\,1}_{X_{k}/A_{k}})\to L^{2}(\Lambda^{n-1\,1}_{X_{k-1}/A_{k-1}})
maps
\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}_{X_{k}/A_{k}}+{\bar{\partial}}_{X_{k}/A_{k}}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}
surjectively to
\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}_{X_{k-1}/A_{k-1}}+{\bar{\partial}}_{X_{k-1}/A_{k-1}}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}.
Let
\phi\in\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}_{X_{k-1}/A_{k-1}}+{\bar{\partial}}_{X_{k-1}/A_{k-1}}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}
be any element and regard it as a section of
\Lambda^{n}_{X_{k-1}/A_{k-1}}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}\cong\Lambda^{n}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}A_{k-1}.
Using a splitting of
A_{k}\to A_{k-1}
lift
\phi
to a section
\psi
of
\Lambda^{n}_{X^{{\rm reg}}}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}A_{k}\cong\Lambda^{n}_{X_{k}/A_{k}}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}.
Then
\psi
is
L^{2}
and we can project it to an element
\chi\in\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}_{X_{k}/A_{k}}+{\bar{\partial}}_{X_{k}/A_{k}}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}.

We look now at the
L^{2}
orthogonal decomposition

\begin{split}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!L^{2}(\Lambda^{n-1\,1}_{X_{k}/A_{k}}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}})=\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}_{X_{k}/A_{k}}+{\bar{\partial}}_{X_{k}/A_{k}}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}\oplus\overline{{\bar{\partial}}_{X_{k}/A_{k}}(\Gamma_{c}(\Lambda^{n-1\,0}_{X_{k}/A_{k}}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}))}\\
\oplus\overline{{\bar{\partial}}_{X_{k}/A_{k}}^{*}(\Gamma_{c}(\Lambda^{n-1\,2}_{X_{k}/A_{k}}|_{X^{{\rm reg}}}))}\end{split}

(14.7)

and its version with
k-1
in place of
k.
The projection
A_{k}\to A_{k-1}
induces the four restriction maps from the respective four terms on (
14.7
) to their versions with
k-1
in place of
k.
These moreover commute with the equality in (
14.7
) and its version with
k-1
in place of
k.

So
R\chi=\phi
and the restriction map
\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}_{X_{k}/A_{k}}+{\bar{\partial}}_{X_{k}/A_{k}}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}\to\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}_{X_{k-1}/A_{k-1}}+{\bar{\partial}}_{X_{k-1}/A_{k-1}}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}
is surjective. As the kernel of this map contains
\mathop{\rm ker}({\bar{\partial}}_{X}+{\bar{\partial}}_{X}^{*})^{n-1\,1}_{-n}
it follows from the induction that (
14.6
) holds.

Combining (
14.6
), (
14.4
) and Theorem
14.11
we see that the all the inequalities in (
14.4
)–(
14.6
) are in fact equalities. In particular, the map
H^{1}(X,\Theta_{X_{k}/A_{k}})\to H^{1}(X,\Theta_{X_{k-1}/A_{k-1}})
is surjective as we have to prove.
∎

Remark 14.13
.

For
n=2,
as mentioned in the introduction it is known that the locally trivial deformations are unobstructed. But we can also verify directly the following statement: let
X
be a compact Calabi–Yau
2
-conifold,
0\to(\epsilon)\to A\to B\to 0
a small extension in
({\rm Art})_{{\mathbb{R}}},

{{\mathcal{X}}}/A
a locally trivial deformation of
X,
and
{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B
the deformation of
X
defined by
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}:={{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\otimes_{A}B;
then the natural map
H^{1}(X,\Theta_{{{\mathcal{X}}}/A})\to H^{1}(X,\Theta_{{{\mathcal{Y}}}/B})
is surjective. This may be done using the degenerate spectral sequence as in Remark
12.10
.

By Theorem
14.12
we can use the
T^{1}
lift method as in Example
8.15
, which proves Theorem
1.3
. ∎

15
Remarks on Theorem
1.3

The hypothesis
H^{2}_{X^{\rm sing}}(X,\Omega^{n-2})=0
in Theorem
1.3
is equivalent to saying that for each
x\in X^{\rm sing},
if
U\subseteq X
is a Stein neighbourhood of
x
then we should have
H^{1}(U\setminus\{x\},\Omega^{n-2}_{U})=0.
We give in Proposition
15.3
below a simple example for which this holds. We begin by recalling the standard facts we will use about Fano manifolds.

Definition 15.1
.

A
Fano
manifold is a compact connected complex manifold
Y
whose anti-canonical bundle
K_{Y}^{*}
is ample. For a Fano manifold
Y
we define its
index

\mathop{\rm ind}\nolimits Y\in\{1,2,3,\dots\}.
Recall (for instance from
[
8
, Theorem 3.6.9]
) that
Y
is simply connected. By the universal coefficient theorem there is a group isomorphism
H^{2}(Y,{{\mathbb{Z}}})\cong\hom(H_{2}(Y,{{\mathbb{Z}}}),{{\mathbb{Z}}}).
The first Chern class
c_{1}(K_{Y})
defines now a non-zero homomorphism
H_{2}(Y,{{\mathbb{Z}}})\to{{\mathbb{Z}}}
whose image may be written uniquely as
k{{\mathbb{Z}}}
with
k>0.
We then set
\mathop{\rm ind}\nolimits Y:=k.
In particular, we can define an element
\frac{1}{\mathop{\rm ind}\nolimits Y}c_{1}(K_{Y})\in H^{2}(Y,{{\mathbb{Z}}}).

By the Kodaira vanishing theorem we have
H^{1}(Y,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{Y})=H^{2}(Y,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{Y})=0
and so the Picard group of
Y
is isomorphic to
H^{2}(Y,{{\mathbb{Z}}}).
Let
L\to Y
be a holomophic line bundle with
c_{1}(L)=\frac{1}{\mathop{\rm ind}\nolimits Y}c_{1}(K_{Y})\in H^{2}(Y,{{\mathbb{Z}}}).
Recall from the proof of
[
14
, Theorem 3.1]
that there exist a normal Stein space
C
with a unique singular point
{\rm vx}\in C
and a complex space morphism
L\to C
which maps the zero-section
Y\subseteq L
onto
{\rm vx}
and maps
L\setminus Y
biholomorphically onto
C^{{\rm reg}}.
Choose now on the holomorphic line bundle
L\to Y
a Hermitian metric
h
whose curvature
(1,1)
form represents
c_{1}(L).
Denote by
r:L\to[0,\infty)
the distance relative to
h
from the zero-section
Y\subseteq L,
and by
A
the connection
1
-form relative to
h
on the principal
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{*}
bundle
L\setminus Y\to Y.
Restricting
r
to
L\setminus Y\cong C^{{\rm reg}}
we get on
C^{{\rm reg}}
a
2
-form
-\frac{1}{2}{\rm d}(r^{2}A).
This defines then on
C^{{\rm reg}}
a Kähler cone structure with contact
1
-form
-A.
The link
C^{{\rm lk}}
has the corresponding Sasakian structure.

We prove that
(C,{\rm vx})
above has the following properties.

Lemma 15.2
.

Let
Y
be a Fano manifold and
(L,Y)\to(C,{\rm vx})
as in Definition
15.1
. Then
C^{{\rm reg}}\cong L\setminus Y
is simply connected; its canonical bundle
K_{C^{{\rm reg}}}\to C^{{\rm reg}}
is a trivial holomorphic line bundle; and
(C,{\rm vx})
is a rational singularity.

Proof.

We use the homotopy exact sequence
\pi_{2}(L\setminus Y)\to\pi_{2}Y\to\pi_{1}{{\mathbb{C}}}^{*}\to\pi_{1}(L\setminus Y)\to\pi_{1}Y=\{1\}.
If we choose a point
y\in Y
and denote its fibre by
F\subseteq L\setminus Y
then there is a group isomorphism
\pi_{2}Y=\pi_{2}(Y,y)\cong\pi_{2}(L\setminus Y,F).
So every element of
\pi_{2}(Y,y)
may be represented by a map
\alpha:(D^{2},\partial D^{2})\to(L\setminus Y,F).
The map
\pi_{2}(Y,y)\to\pi_{1}F
is then defined by restricting
\alpha
to
\partial D^{2}.
On the other hand, define the
1
-form
A
as in Definition
15.1
; and restricting it to the fibre
F\cong{{\mathbb{C}}}^{*}
we get an isomorphism
\pi_{1}F\cong{{\mathbb{Z}}}.
The composite map
\pi_{2}Y\to\pi_{1}F\cong{{\mathbb{Z}}}
is thus defined by integrating
A
over each
\alpha.
By Stokes’ theorem the map
\pi_{2}Y\to{{\mathbb{Z}}}
is the pairing with
c_{1}(L).
Since
Y
is simply connected we get, by the Hurewicz and universal coefficient theorems, a group isomorphism
H^{2}(Y,{{\mathbb{Z}}})\cong\hom(\pi_{2}Y,{{\mathbb{Z}}}).
The map
\pi_{2}Y\to{{\mathbb{Z}}}
thus contains all the information of
c_{1}(L)\in H^{2}(Y,{{\mathbb{Z}}}).
The definition of
\mathop{\rm ind}\nolimits Y
implies now that the group homomorphism
\pi_{2}Y\to{{\mathbb{Z}}}
is surjective. Returning to the homotopy exact sequence above we see that
\pi_{1}(L\setminus Y)
is a trivial group or equivalently that
L\setminus Y
is simply connected.

Notice that the cohomology class
c_{1}(K_{Y}^{*})=-\mathop{\rm ind}\nolimits(Y)c_{1}(L)
may be represented by the closed
2
-form
-\mathop{\rm ind}\nolimits(Y){\rm d}A.
Denote by
\pi:C^{{\rm lk}}\to Y
the composite map
C^{{\rm lk}}\subseteq C^{{\rm reg}}\cong L\setminus Y\subseteq L\to Y
ending with the bundle projection. We call
\pi^{*}c_{1}(K_{Y}^{*})
the
basic Chern class of the Sasakian
C^{{\rm lk}}.
This may be represented by the
2
-form
-\mathop{\rm ind}\nolimits(Y){\rm d}A|_{C^{{\rm lk}}}
and thus satisfies the condition (i) of
[
14
, Proposition 2.5]
. The proof of the same proposition shows that there exists a function
f:Y\to(0,\infty),
identified with its pull-back to
C^{{\rm reg}},
such that the holomorphic line bundle
K_{C^{{\rm reg}}}\to C^{{\rm reg}}
is flat with respect to the conformal change
fg
of the Kähler cone metric
g
of
C^{{\rm reg}}.
Since
C^{{\rm reg}}
is simply connected we get a nowhere-vanishing parallel section
\Omega
of
K_{C^{{\rm reg}}}.
This is in particular holomorphic and so
K_{C^{{\rm reg}}}
is holomorphically trivial. Also
\Omega
has constant pointwise norm with respect to
fg,
and
f
is the pull-back of a function on
Y.
These imply that
\Omega
is
L^{2}
on a punctured neighbourhood of
{\rm vx}\in C.
Hence it follows as in Remark
4.14
that
(C,{\rm vx})
is rational.
∎

We turn now to the case in which the Fano manifolds are toric.

Proposition 15.3
.

Let
n\geqslant 5
be an integer and
Y
a toric Fano manifold of dimension
n-1.
Let
(L,Y)\to(C,{\rm vx})
be as in Definition
15.1
, and
U\subseteq C
a Stein neighbourhood of
{\rm vx}.
Then
H^{1}(U\setminus\{{\rm vx}\},\Omega^{n-2}_{C})=0
and
H^{1}(U\setminus\{{\rm vx}\},\Omega^{n-1}_{C})=0.

Proof.

For
k\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
denote by
L^{-k}
the invertible sheaf on
Y
corresponding to the line bundle
(L^{*})^{\otimes k}.
We define on
Y
an
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{L}|_{Y}
module sheaf exact sequence

0\to\Omega^{1}_{Y}\otimes_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{Y}}({{\mathcal{O}}}_{L}|_{Y})\to\Omega^{1}_{L}|_{Y}\to L^{-1}\otimes_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{Y}}({{\mathcal{O}}}_{L}|_{Y})\to 0.
The injective map
\Omega^{1}_{Y}\otimes_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{Y}}({{\mathcal{O}}}_{L}|_{Y})\to\Omega^{1}_{L}|_{Y}
is defined by
{\rm d}z\otimes w\mapsto w{\rm d}z
where
z
is a local function on
Y,
and
w
a local function on
L.
The surjective map
\Omega^{1}_{L}|_{Y}\to L^{-1}\otimes_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{Y}}{{\mathcal{O}}}_{L}|_{Y}
is defined as follows. Let
\omega
be a
1
-form defined near
y\in Y.
For each point
x\in L
in the fibre over
y
choose in the same fibre a path from
y
to
x
and integrate
\omega
over it. Then we get an element of
L^{-1}\otimes_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{Y}}{{\mathcal{O}}}_{L}|_{Y}.
This map
\Omega^{1}_{L}|_{Y}\to L^{-1}\otimes_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{Y}}{{\mathcal{O}}}_{L}|_{Y}
has a splitting
L^{-1}\otimes_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{Y}}\iota^{-1}{{\mathcal{O}}}_{L}\to\Omega^{1}_{L}
(which differentiates the elements of
L^{-1}\otimes_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{Y}}\iota^{-1}{{\mathcal{O}}}_{L}
). So there is an isomorphism
\Omega^{1}_{L}|_{Y}\cong(\Omega^{1}_{Y}\otimes_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{Y}}{{\mathcal{O}}}_{L}|_{Y})\oplus(L^{-1}\otimes_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{Y}}{{\mathcal{O}}}_{L}|_{Y}).
Hence we get an isomorphism

\Omega^{p}_{L}|_{Y}\cong(\Omega^{p}_{Y}\otimes_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{Y}}{{\mathcal{O}}}_{L}|_{Y})\oplus(\Omega^{p-1}_{Y}\otimes_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{Y}}L^{-1}\otimes_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{Y}}{{\mathcal{O}}}_{L}|_{Y}).

(15.1)

Denote by
\wedge
the functor of taking completions on
Y\subseteq L.
Then
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{L}|_{Y}^{\wedge}
is an
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{Y}
algebra sheaf on
Y
isomorphic to
\prod_{k=0}^{\infty}L^{-k}.
From (
15.1
) we get an
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{Y}
module sheaf isomorphism

\begin{split}\Omega^{p}_{L}|_{Y}^{\wedge}\cong(\Omega^{p}_{Y}\otimes_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{Y}}{{\mathcal{O}}}_{L}|_{Y}^{\wedge})\oplus(\Omega^{p-1}_{Y}\otimes_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{Y}}L^{-1}\otimes_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{Y}}{{\mathcal{O}}}_{L}|_{Y}^{\wedge})\\
\cong\prod_{k=0}^{\infty}(\Omega^{p}_{Y}\otimes_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{Y}}L^{-k})\oplus\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}(\Omega^{p}_{Y}\otimes_{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{Y}}L^{-k}).\end{split}

(15.2)

Recall (for instance from
[
16
, Theorem 9.3.1]
) that the toric variety
Y
has the Bott vanishing property, which in the current circumstances implies that for
q\geqslant 1
we have
H^{q}(Y,\Omega^{p}_{Y}\otimes L^{-k})=0.
Denote by
V\subseteq L
the pull-back of
U\subseteq C
by the contraction map
(L,Y)\to(C,{\rm vx}).
Then
V
is a
1
-convex space; and by
[
27
, Chapter V, Theorem 2.1.7]
, for
q\geqslant 1
we have
H^{q}(V,\Omega^{p}_{V})\cong H^{q}(Y,\Omega^{p}_{L}|_{Y}^{\wedge})\cong H^{q}(Y,\Omega^{p}_{Y}).
As
Y
is toric, by
[
16
, Theorem 9.4.11]
we have
H^{q}(Y,\Omega^{p}_{Y})=0
for
p\neq q.
Since
n\geqslant 5
it follows that
H^{1}(Y,\Omega^{n-2}_{Y})=H^{n-2}(Y,\Omega^{2}_{Y})=0.
By the duality theorem
[
35
]
we have also
H^{2}_{Y}(V,\Omega^{n-2}_{L})=0.
The long exact sequence
0=H^{1}(V,\Omega^{n-2}_{L})\cong H^{1}(V\setminus Y,\Omega^{n-2}_{L})\to H^{2}_{Y}(V,\Omega^{n-2}_{L})=0
implies now
H^{1}(V\setminus Y,\Omega^{n-2}_{L}).
Identifying
V\setminus Y
with
U\setminus\{{\rm vx}\}
we see that
H^{1}_{\rm vx}(U\setminus\{{\rm vx}\},\Omega^{n-2}_{C})=0.
In the same way we can verify also that
H^{1}(U\setminus\{{\rm vx}\},\Omega^{n-1}_{C})=0.

∎

Remark 15.4
.

The algebraic scheme version of (
15.2
) is proved in
[
58
, Lemma A.1]
.

As in
[
56
]
the vector space
H^{1}(U\setminus\{{\rm vx}\},\Theta_{C})\cong H^{1}(U\setminus\{{\rm vx}\},\Omega^{n-1}_{C})=0
parametrizes the infinitesimal deformations of
(C,{\rm vx})
which must therefore be trivial. In particular, the deformations of
(C,{\rm vx})
are unobstructed. If every singularity of a compact Calabi–Yau conifold
X
is of this kind then the locally trivial deformations of
X
are equivalent to the full deformations of
X.
The difference from Theorem
1.2
is that we do not need the hypothesis that either
b_{n-1}(C^{{\rm reg}})=0
or
b_{n-2}(C^{{\rm reg}})=0.

It does not seem easy to find examples of
(C,{\rm vx})
with
H^{1}(U\setminus\{{\rm vx}\},\Omega^{n-2}_{C})=0.
We end by showing that even the ordinary double points do not satisfy this condition.

Proposition 15.5
.

Let
n\geqslant 3
be an integer and
(C,{\rm vx})\subseteq{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n+1}
the isolated singularity defined by
z_{1}^{2}+\dots+z_{n+1}^{2}=0
using the coordinates
z_{1},\dots,z_{n+1}
of
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n+1}.

Let
U\subseteq C
be a Stein neighbourhood of
{\rm vx}.
Then
H^{1}(U\setminus\{{\rm vx}\},\Omega^{n-2}_{C})
is non-zero.

Proof.

Denote by
Y\subseteq{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
the Fano manifold defined by the same homogeneous polynomial. In Definition
15.1
then we can take
L
to be the line bundle corresponding to
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{Y}(-1):={{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}(-1)|_{Y}.
Contracting the zero-section
Y\subseteq L
we reproduce
(C,{\rm vx}).
Suppose
n=3.
Then
Y
is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
and in particular toric. The proof of Proposition
15.3
shows that there are isomorphisms
H^{1}_{Y}(L,\Omega^{1}_{L})\cong H^{2}(Y,\Omega^{2}_{Y})\cong{{\mathbb{C}}},

H^{1}(L,\Omega^{1}_{L})\cong H^{1}(Y,\Omega^{1}_{Y})\cong{{\mathbb{C}}}^{2}
and
H^{2}_{Y}(L,\Omega^{1}_{L})\cong H^{1}(Y,\Omega^{2}_{Y})=0.
The exact sequence
H^{1}_{Y}(L,\Omega^{1}_{L})\to H^{1}(L,\Omega^{1}_{L})\to H^{1}(U\setminus\{{\rm vx}\},\Omega^{1}_{C})\to H^{2}_{Y}(L,\Omega^{1}_{L})
implies therefore
H^{1}(U\setminus\{{\rm vx}\},\Omega^{1}_{C})\neq 0.

For
n\geqslant 4
we do different computation. We show first that
H^{1}(L,\Omega^{n-2}_{L})
is non-zero. For
p,k\in{{\mathbb{Z}}}
put
\Omega^{p}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}(k):=\Omega^{p}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}\otimes{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}(k)
and
\Omega_{Y}^{p}(k)\cong\Omega^{p}_{Y}\otimes{{\mathcal{O}}}_{Y}(k).
By
[
7
, Proposition 14.4]
we have

H^{q}(\mathbb{P}^{n},\Omega^{p}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(k))=0
unless
(i)

k=0
and
p=q,

(ii)

q=0
and
k>p,
or
(iii)

q=n
and
k<p-n.

(15.3)

In particular,
H^{1}({\mathbb{P}}^{n},\Omega^{n-1}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}(n-3))=0
and
H^{0}({\mathbb{P}}^{n},\Omega^{n-1}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}(n-1))=0.
The exact sequence
0\to\Omega^{n-1}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}(n-3)\to\Omega^{n-1}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}(n-1)\to\Omega^{n-1}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}(n-1)|_{Y}\to 0
implies then
H^{0}(Y,\Omega^{n-1}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}(n-3))=0.
The exact sequence
0\to\Omega^{n-2}_{Y}(n-3)\to\Omega^{n-1}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}(n-1)|_{Y}\to\Omega^{n-1}_{Y}(n-1)\to 0
induces therefore an injective map
H^{0}(Y,\Omega^{n-1}_{Y}(n-1))\to H^{1}(Y,\Omega^{n-2}_{Y}(n-3)).

On the other hand, there are isomorphisms
\Omega^{n-1}_{Y}\cong\Omega^{n}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}(2)|_{Y}\cong{{\mathcal{O}}}_{Y}(1-n)
and
H^{0}(Y,\Omega^{n-1}_{Y}(n-1))\cong H^{0}(Y,{{\mathcal{O}}}_{Y})\cong{{\mathbb{C}}}\neq 0.
This may however be regarded as a subspace of
H^{1}(Y,\Omega^{n-2}_{Y}(n-3))
so the latter is non-zero. The proof of Proposition
15.3
shows that

H^{1}(L,\Omega^{n-2}_{L})\cong\Bigl(\prod_{k=0}^{\infty}H^{1}(L,\Omega^{n-2}_{L}(k))\Bigr)\oplus\Bigl(\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}H^{1}(L,\Omega^{n-3}_{L}(k))\Bigr)

(15.4)

which is non-zero too.

We show next that
H^{n-1}(L,\Omega^{2}_{L})=0.
For
k\geqslant 0
it follows from (
15.3
) with
n\geqslant 4
that
H^{n-1}({\mathbb{P}}^{n},\Omega^{2}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}(k))=H^{n}({\mathbb{P}}^{n},\Omega^{2}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}(k-2))=0.
The exact sequence
0\to\Omega^{2}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}(k-2)\to\Omega^{2}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}(k)\to\Omega^{2}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}(k)|_{Y}\to 0
implies therefore
H^{n-1}(Y,\Omega^{2}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}(k))=0.
As
Y
is of dimension
n-1
we have also
H^{n}(Y,\Omega^{1}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}(k-2))=0.
The exact sequence
0\to\Omega^{1}_{Y}(k-2)\to\Omega^{2}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}(k)|_{Y}\to\Omega^{2}_{Y}(k)\to 0
implies now
H^{n-1}(Y,\Omega^{2}_{Y}(k))=0.
In the same way we can verify that for
k\geqslant 1
we have
H^{n-1}(Y,\Omega^{1}_{Y}(k))=0.
The proof of Proposition
15.3
shows that

H^{n-1}(L,\Omega^{2}_{L})\cong\Bigl(\prod_{k=0}^{\infty}H^{n-1}(L,\Omega^{2}_{L}(k))\Bigr)\oplus\Bigl(\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}H^{n-1}(L,\Omega^{1}_{L}(k))\Bigr)

(15.5)

which vanishes. By the duality theorem
[
35
]
therefore
H^{1}_{Y}(L,\Omega^{n-2}_{L})=0
and there is accordingly an exact sequence
0\to H^{1}(L,\Omega^{n-2}_{L})\to H^{1}(U\setminus\{{\rm vx}\},\Omega^{n-2}_{L}).
Hence we find that
H^{1}(U\setminus\{{\rm vx}\},\Omega^{n-2}_{L})\neq 0.

∎
